Viscous vortex crystals
Michele Dolce, Martin Donati

Abstract
. We study the solution to the two-dimensional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations arising from a sum of Dirac masses in a particular co-rotating configuration. This configuration consists of a polygonal vortex crystal with or without a central vortex. By exploiting the symmetries and stability properties of the system, we describe and control the solution up to sub-diffusive time scales, prior to the expected onset of vortex merging.



1.
Introduction

To understand large-scale atmospheric dynamics, it is essential to characterize the motion and interaction of coherent vortices. These vortices can be relatively short-lived and extreme, such as cyclones, or persistent and somewhat stable, such as Earth’s tropospheric polar vortices. In this paper, we examine a specific yet not uncommon scenario where a group of vortices organizes into a highly stable structure, achieving an abnormally long lifetime due to this particular configuration. A striking example of this phenomenon is currently observed at Jupiter’s poles, where polygonally arranged vortices rotate collectively in an almost rigid manner around each pole, see
[
1
]
.

More broadly, relative equilibria of vortices manifest in diverse contexts, including metastable states in electron plasmas
[
44
]
, wake structures behind wind turbines, and as outcomes of two-dimensional turbulence relaxation
[
19
]
. In the latter case, the stability of these equilibria is pivotal, as it determines which structures persist even with turbulent chaos. For a comprehensive mathematical analysis of this phenomenon, we refer to
[
17
]
, while
[
45
]
provides numerical simulations illustrating the formation of such vortex structures from turbulence, particularly near the poles of rotating planets. Moreover, the emergence of large vortex structures following a turbulent phase was explored in
[
40
]
, where a finite-dimensional approximation of ideal fluids on a sphere, known as the
Zeitlin model
, is used. The authors consistently observed at most four large vortices in the long-time regime (see also
[
41
]
for the mathematical foundations of the matrix hydrodynamic model used in
[
40
]
).

Despite the inherent complexity of real atmospheric systems, where multiple physical processes interact, many fundamental stability mechanisms and emergent behaviors can be effectively captured by even the simplest fluid models. For instance, the
point-vortex
model pioneered by Helmholtz
[
29
]
and Kirchhoff
[
31
]
, in which vortices are represented as singularities concentrated at discrete points, provides an accurate first approximation of the motion of strong, concentrated vortices. Within this model, polygonally arranged point vortices indeed rotate in a rigid manner. The study of their stability properties dates back to Thomson’s 1883 foundational result
[
46
]
. Since then, the topic has been extensively studied, with
[
6
,
3
]
providing modern results and historical accounts.

Another natural research direction involves the
desingularization
of the point-vortex model to account for finite-size effects. In this context, particular exact concentrated solutions can be constructed through variational methods (e.g.
[
47
,
7
]
), the study of contour dynamics
[
30
,
27
,
28
]
, or the
gluing method
introduced in
[
10
]
, while the control of any non-viscous concentrated solution to the planar incompressible Euler equation can be obtained by the
vortex blobs method
, e.g.
[
36
,
5
]
.
Furthermore, the second author
[
15
]
proved that non-singular, concentrated vortices also exhibit robust stability properties when arranged in polygonal configurations. In the present work, we consider these polygonal configurations in weakly viscous fluid flows.

1.1.
Mathematical setting

We consider a two-dimensional incompressible and viscous fluid flow, described by a velocity field
u
with vorticity
\omega=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u
and viscosity
\nu>0
. This evolves according to the Navier-Stokes equations in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which in vorticity form are given by:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\omega(x,t)+u(x,t)\cdot\nabla\omega(x,t)=\nu\Delta\omega(x,t),&x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad t>0\\
\displaystyle u(x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(x-y)^{\perp}}{|x-y|^{2}}\omega(y,t){\rm d}y,&x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad t\geq 0\\
\displaystyle\omega|_{t=0}=\omega^{in},\end{cases}

where the initial datum
\omega^{in}
has to be specified. We aim to describe the evolution of vortices that are very concentrated in space around their center of mass. It is well known that, at least over some time interval, if the viscosity of the fluid is small enough, the motion of those vortices (the motion of their center of mass) is well approximated by the so-called
point-vortex dynamics
, or
Helmholtz-Kirchhoff
equations

(1.2)

\displaystyle z_{j}^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N+1}\frac{\Gamma_{l}}{2\pi}\frac{(z_{j}(t)-z_{l}(t))^{\perp}}{|z_{j}(t)-z_{l}(t)|^{2}},\qquad j,l=1,\dots,N+1,

(1.3)

\displaystyle z_{j}(0)=z_{j}^{in}.

where
z_{1}(t),\dots,z_{N+1}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
are the positions of the
N+1
point-vortices and
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{N+1}\in\mathbb{R}
their circulations. Going back to Helmholtz
[
29
]
, a rigorous proof of this statement was made for a non-viscous fluid in
[
36
]
(see also
[
37
]
). For weakly-viscous fluids, it was done in
[
25
]
in the case of a sum of Dirac masses of vorticity (point-vortices) as initial data, where control of the solution was established on a non-viscous time scale. In the case of concentrated (but not infinitely concentrated) initial vortices, it was done by

[
38
,
9
]
with a viscosity depending on the considered initial data, then by
[
8
]
with fixed small viscosity. In
[
12
]
, the same study as
[
25
]
was conducted, but on a particular configuration of vortices: the translating pair of opposite vortices. In that case, using the specific symmetries and stability of the configuration, the control of the solution is obtained on a much larger time scale, nearly reaching what we call the
diffusive time
, which is the time after which the diffusive effects become so strong that the vortices cannot be considered concentrated anymore. In the recent contribution
[
49
]
, the authors treated the case of two vortices of any given intensity, obtaining the result on the same large time scales reached in
[
12
]
. For details regarding the ODE system (
1.2
) itself, we refer to
[
2
]
.

In this paper, we perform the same analysis but on a configuration consisting of equal vortices placed at the vertices of a regular polygon, with or without a central vortex of given circulation.
We thus consider equations
Eq.

1.1
with initial datum

(1.4)

\omega^{in}=\Gamma\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{z_{j}^{in}}+\gamma\Gamma\delta_{0},

where
N\geq 2
,
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and the
z_{j}^{in}
are

(1.5)

z_{j}^{in}=\mathsf{r}\Big(\cos\big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}\big),\sin\big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}\big)\Big),\qquad\text{for}\quad j=1,\dots,N,

with
\mathsf{r}>0
. This initial datum is a sum of equal Dirac masses regularly placed on the disk of center 0 and radius
\mathsf{r}
, with (or without if
\gamma=0
) a central vortex, see Figure
1
. Note that the cases
N=1,\gamma\neq 0
and
N=2
,
\gamma=0
are fully covered by
[
12
,
49
]
, which is why we restrict our attention to
N\geq 2
. Let us recall that existence and uniqueness of a solution to the system
Eq.

1.1
with a measure as initial data were proven in
[
20
]
. Moreover, the solution of the point-vortex dynamics
Eq.

1.2
with initial datum
Eq.

1.5
is well known (see for instance
[
3
]
) to be given by

(1.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}z_{j}(t)=\mathsf{r}\big(\cos\big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\mathsf{a}t\big),\sin\big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\mathsf{a}t\big)\big),\qquad&\text{for}\quad j=1,\dots,N\\
z_{N+1}(t)=(0,0),\\
\mathsf{a}=\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi\mathsf{r}^{2}}(N-1+2\gamma).\end{cases}

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 1
.
Two examples of polygonal vortex configuration as described at
Eq.

1.5
, with
N=6
and
\gamma=1
(left), and
N=5
and
\gamma=0
(right).

This configuration is uniformly rotating around the origin. It is a relative equilibrium of the dynamics, called in this context a
vortex crystal
. The stability of this configuration was studied in
[
6
]
1
1

1

Let us mention that
[
33
]
disagrees with the main result of
[
6
]
concerning the case
N\leq 6
,
\gamma<0
.
, and the result is recalled in Section
6.3
. In the non-viscous case, for a special value of
\gamma
and for
N\geq 4
, a result of long-time stability for non-singular vortices concentrated near this initial configuration was obtained in
[
15
]
, see also
[
14
]
for the same problem on the rotating sphere. In that regard, we now perform a viscous analysis of this configuration, but we have to first introduce several dimensionless parameters that allow us to describe the solution precisely. Let
L_{N}=2\mathsf{r}\sin(\pi/N)
be the length of the sides of our regular polygon and define the characteristic distance in the system as

(1.7)

d:=\min\{\mathsf{r},L_{N}\}.

Note that
L_{N}=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{r}N^{-1})
as
N\to\infty
. We then define our adimensional quantities of interest as

(1.8)

\varepsilon(t):=\frac{\sqrt{\nu t}}{d},\qquad\delta:=\frac{\nu}{\Gamma},\qquad T_{\mathsf{adv}}:=\frac{d^{2}}{\Gamma},\qquad T_{\mathsf{diff}}:=\frac{d^{2}}{\nu},

where
\varepsilon(t)
is the aspect ratio between the core size of the vortices and their mutual distances,
\delta
the inverse circulation Reynolds number,
T_{\rm adv}
the advection time and
T_{\rm diff}
is the standard diffusive time. Note that
T_{\rm adv}
corresponds to a time where the point vortex polygon rotates by a fixed angle, so that one can effectively measure the point vortex dynamics at this time. In fact, to actually observe the inviscid dynamics described by
Eq.

1.6
in the viscous problem, we need to work in the high Reynolds number regime
\delta\ll 1
, which implies
T_{\rm adv}\ll T_{\rm diff}
. Note that
\varepsilon(T_{\rm diff})=1
, and we therefore will only consider sub-diffusive time scales.

1.2.
Main result

We aim to describe the solution of equations
Eq.

1.1
with initial datum given by
Eq.

1.4
up to a large time scale.
Applying the result of
[
25
]
, we know that the solution is well approximated by Lamb-Oseen vortices moving according to the point-vortex dynamics on a non-viscous time scale. Let us state this fact precisely. Let

(1.9)

\omega_{\mathrm{PV}}(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi\nu t}\exp\Big(-\frac{|x-z_{j}(t)|^{2}}{4\nu t}\Big)+\gamma\Gamma\exp\Big(-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4\nu t}\Big)

where the trajectories
t\mapsto z_{j}(t)
are given by (
1.6
).
We have the following.

Proposition 1.1

(Corollary of
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Let
K>0
be a fixed constant. Then there exist constants
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C_{0}>0
such that for every
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, the unique solution
\omega
of equations
Eq.

1.1
with initial datum
Eq.

1.4
satisfies

(1.10)

\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|\omega(x,t)-\omega_{\mathrm{PV}}(x,t)\big|{\rm d}x\leq C_{0}\varepsilon^{2}(t),\qquad\forall t\in(0,KT_{\rm adv}),

where
\delta,\varepsilon(t)
and
T_{\rm adv}
are defined in
Eq.

1.8
.

Fixing
\Gamma
and taking
\nu
sufficiently small, we naturally expect that not only the control of the solution becomes better, as it is already visible in Proposition
1.1
, but also that it should hold for a longer time, as the viscous effects that will eventually destroy the structure are less powerful. As remarked in
[
13
]
, it is only possible in all generality to adapt the proof made in
[
25
]
to obtain the control of the solution up to a time of order
T_{\rm adv}|\ln\delta|
, which is also the result obtained in
[
8
]
. So far, to the best of our knowledge, the only known way to push further in time is to take advantage of stability properties arising from special vortex configurations. This was done in
[
12
]
for the pair of counter-rotating vortices and in
[
49
]
for any pair of vortices. We establish an equivalent result for our configuration of polygonally arranged vortices with (or without) central vortex. We show that for longer times, the angular velocity of the configuration of vortices starts changing due to viscous effects.
We thus introduce corrected approximate positions as follows.
For all
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, let

(1.11)

\zeta_{j}(t)=R(t)\Big(\cos\Big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\alpha(t)\Big),\sin\Big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\alpha(t)\Big)\Big),

where with
R(0)=\mathsf{r}
and
\alpha(0)=0
. These quantities act as
modulation parameters
used to precisely track the center of each Lamb-Oseen vortex. We then define

(1.12)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{PV}_{\nu}}(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi\nu t}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\exp\Big(-\frac{|x-\zeta_{j}(t)|^{2}}{4\nu t}\Big)+\gamma\exp\Big(-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4\nu t}\Big)\Bigg),

(1.13)

\displaystyle\omega_{{\rm app},M}(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{m=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{m}(t)\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega_{m}\Big(\frac{x-\zeta_{j}(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}}\Big)+\gamma\widehat{\Omega}_{m}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu t}}\Big)\Bigg).

Here,
\omega_{{\rm app},M}
captures higher-order viscous corrections, up to a fixed order
M
, to the radial profile close to the vortex cores. Note that these corrections slowly evolve in a self-similar way. The main result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\sigma\in[0,1)
and
M>(5-\sigma)/(1-\sigma)
. Then there exist constants
\delta_{1}\in(0,1)
, and
C_{1}>0
such that for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1}]
, there exists
\Omega_{m},\widehat{\Omega}_{m}
for
m=2,\dots,M
, and modulation parameters
R(t),\alpha(t)
such that the unique solution
\omega
of equations
Eq.

1.1
with initial datum
Eq.

1.4
satisfies

(1.14)

\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|\omega-(\omega_{\mathrm{PV}_{\nu}}+\omega_{{\rm app},M})\big|(x,t){\rm d}x\leq C_{1}\delta\varepsilon^{2}(t),\qquad\forall t\in\big(0,T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}\big).

Furthermore, the radius
R(t)
and the angular speed
\alpha^{\prime}(t)
are smooth functions satisfying

(1.15)

R(t)=\mathsf{r}\big(1+r_{6}\varepsilon^{6}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}(t))\big),\qquad\alpha^{\prime}(t)=\mathsf{a}\big(1+\alpha_{4}\varepsilon^{4}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{5}(t)+\delta\varepsilon^{4}(t))\big),

where
\alpha(0)=0
and the exact values of the constants
r_{6}
and
\alpha_{4}
are given in Proposition
4.15
.

Naturally, as
\delta\to 0
, the error in
Eq.

1.14
is much smaller than that in Proposition
1.1
2
2

2

In fact, in
[
25
]
the final result includes the approximate solution up to order
M=4
with
\delta^{-\sigma}
replaced by the constant
K
in Proposition
1.1
. The analogue of Theorem
1.2
is proved with
\delta\varepsilon^{2}(t)
replaced by
\varepsilon^{3}(t)
, but on the time scale under consideration,
\delta\ll\varepsilon(t)
for any
t\geq T_{\rm adv}
and thus the error is still much smaller after the advection time.
.
Moreover, since
T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}\gg T_{\rm adv}
and
T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}\gg T_{\rm adv}|\ln\delta|
, Theorem
1.2
controls the solution on a significantly longer time scale than Proposition
1.1
. Regarding the radius and angular speed,
Eq.

1.15
only highlights the leading-order viscous corrections to the inviscid Helmholtz-Kirchhoff dynamics. The full, rigorous characterization of
R(t)
and
\alpha(t)
is not entirely captured by
Eq.

1.15
alone. Their precise definition is one of the central components of our proof and relies on technical constraints that we will carefully detail later in the paper. Nonetheless, we emphasize that relation
Eq.

1.15
implies

(1.16)

\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}|\zeta_{j}(t)-z_{j}(t)|=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{t}{T_{\rm adv}}\varepsilon^{4}(t)\Big).

Observing that
t\varepsilon^{4}(t)/T_{\rm adv}\ll 1
is valid only for
t\ll T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-2/3}
, it follows that the point-vortex dynamics in
Eq.

1.6
remains a good approximation of the viscous vortices up to a time of order
\mathcal{O}(T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-2/3})
. Beyond this time scale, the configuration becomes out of phase relative to the inviscid point-vortex motion, see Remark
4.16
and Corollary
5.10
for more details.

To prove Theorem
1.2
, we proceed similarly to
[
12
]
. After reformulating the equations in self-similar variables, the first step is to construct an approximate solution
\omega_{\rm app}=\omega_{\mathrm{PV}_{\nu}}+\omega_{{\rm app},M}
that remains close to
\omega
over the time interval
(0,T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma})
up to any power of
\varepsilon(t)
. The construction of such an accurate approximate solution is essential for the following reason: the control of the configuration over such long time scales requires an extremely accurate knowledge of the vortex positions, as any positional error would accumulate in time and eventually invalidate an estimate like in
Eq.

1.14
.
In addition, the controls are obtained in more refined norms. We observe that for the particular choice of
\gamma
used in
[
15
]
our approximate solution starts at order
\varepsilon^{3}(t)
rather than the standard
\varepsilon^{2}(t)
found in all previous constructions
[
12
,
49
]
, see discussion in the next subsection.

Remark 1.3

(Bounds on the time scale)
.

Note that, in this vortex crystal configuration, it is expected that vortices merge abruptly at a time of order
\mathcal{O}(T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-1})=\mathcal{O}(T_{\rm diff})
, which corresponds to the regime where the aspect ratio
\varepsilon(t)
becomes of order one. Therefore, approximating
\omega
by
\omega_{\mathrm{PV}_{\nu}}
is only physically meaningful up to a time of at most
t_{0}T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-1}
for some sufficiently small constant
t_{0}>0
. By taking
\sigma\to 1^{-}
, we almost reach this limit. However, our method relies on an asymptotic expansion in
\varepsilon(t)
, but one cannot completely decouple the parameters
\varepsilon(t)
and
\delta
, as the construction introduces remainder terms of size
\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}(t))
. On time scales of order
\mathcal{O}(T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma})
, these errors can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
M
large enough depending on
\sigma
, but this inherently limits the analysis to the regime
\varepsilon(t)\ll\delta^{s}
for some small but strictly positive
s
. Therefore, even though we control the solution up to a time that is arbitrarily close to the maximal time at which vortices remain well separated, we are mathematically far from reaching exactly
\sigma=1
. This limitation is also present in the more structurally stable configuration of a vortex dipole
[
12
]
, where no abrupt merging is expected and the dynamics seem to evolve smoothly for all times
[
11
]
. Hence, we believe that reaching the diffusive time scale is not merely a technical artifact of the proof. Instead, reaching the diffusive time scale requires the introduction of fundamentally new ideas, as the nonlinearity and the diffusion become of the same order of magnitude. In essence, prior to the diffusive time, diffusion is treated purely as a perturbative effect that smoothly changes the shape and position of the vortices.

Remark 1.4

(On the circular vortex sheet limit)
.

If we rescale our initial data in
Eq.

1.4
as

(1.17)

\omega^{in}_{N}=\frac{\Gamma}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{z_{j}}+\gamma\Gamma\delta_{0},

we observe that in the
N\to\infty
limit, this sequence of measures converges to the circular vortex sheet with a central point vortex, given by

(1.18)

\omega^{in}_{\infty}:=\frac{\Gamma}{2\pi\mathsf{r}}\delta_{|x|=\mathsf{r}}+\gamma\Gamma\delta_{0}.

The study of the evolution and stability of vortex sheets is a classical problem in the inviscid setting, where Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities are commonly observed for both flat and circular sheets
[
32
,
35
,
42
,
48
]
. Moreover, a desingularization procedure for more general geometries of these singular structures was established only recently in
[
18
]
. A classical numerical approach to simulate the dynamics of vortex sheets relies on approximating them with a collection of point vortices
[
32
]
. In our setting, due to the specific choice of the initial data in
Eq.

1.17
and the fact that the Navier-Stokes equations preserve the
N
-fold symmetry, one should not see any instability in a suitable limiting regime. Rather, one expects to effectively carry out the
N\to\infty
limit for any fixed
t>0
on an appropriate time scale. Indeed, with the initial data given by
Eq.

1.18
, the unique solution to
Eq.

1.1
is simply the radial function
\omega_{\infty}(t)=e^{\nu t\Delta}\omega_{\infty}^{in}
. This coincides with the solution to the 2D heat equation with data
Eq.

1.18
, which can be approximated by
\widetilde{\omega}_{N}(t):=e^{\nu t\Delta}\omega_{N}^{in}
for any fixed
t>0
as
N\to\infty
.

To formalize the limit
N\to\infty
in our framework, recall that
L_{N}/\mathsf{r}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
, which implies that the dimensionless parameters defined in
Eq.

1.8
(with
\Gamma
replaced by
\Gamma/N
) now scale as

(1.19)

\varepsilon(t)=\mathcal{O}(N\sqrt{\nu t}/\mathsf{r}),\quad T_{\rm adv}=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{r}^{2}/(\Gamma N)),\quad T_{\rm diff}=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{r}^{2}/(\nu N^{2})).

Our analysis requires the time scale separation
T_{\rm adv}\ll T_{\rm diff}
, which is equivalent to imposing
\delta\ll 1/N
. To reach a dynamically relevant conclusion, we must ensure that the upper bound of our time interval,
T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
, does not vanish as
N\to\infty
, so we require
\delta\lesssim N^{-1/\sigma}
. Under these hypotheses, a careful tracking of the
N
-dependence of the constants suggests that it is feasible to take the
N\to\infty
limit for any fixed
t\in(0,T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma})
in Theorem
1.2
.
Specifically, since
\varepsilon^{2}(t)\lesssim N\delta^{1-\sigma}
, if
\delta
decays sufficiently fast with
N
(for instance,
\delta=\min\{N^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}^{-}},N^{-\frac{1}{1-\sigma}^{-}}\}
), all the higher-order corrections in
Eq.

1.13
will vanish as
N\to\infty
. While one must carefully track the
N
-dependent constants in the definition of the approximate solution and the bound in Theorem
1.2
, we observe for example that the profile
\Omega_{2}
defined in Proposition
4.8
contains
N
-dependent constants that precisely balance out to yield a term of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
(see Remark
4.9
), whereas
\widehat{\Omega}_{m}=0
for
m=2,\dots,N-1
by the
N
-fold symmetry.
Furthermore, since the angular speed satisfies
\mathsf{a}\to\Gamma(1+2\gamma)/(4\pi\mathsf{r}^{2})
as
N\to\infty
, Theorem
1.2
implies that the solution is well-approximated by
N
Lamb-Oseen vortices rotating with a constant angular speed. This describes the evolution of
N
Dirac masses under the heat equation in a rotating frame. The only quantitative difference between the Navier-Stokes and the heat equation evolutions then lies in this rotation of the discrete configuration. However, as
N\to\infty
, the limiting measure
Eq.

1.18
is purely radial and thus invariant under continuous rotations. This rotational effect therefore becomes irrelevant in the limiting process, smoothly reconciling the discrete dynamics with the purely diffusive evolution of the continuous vortex sheet.
Consequently, we expect that our analysis can provide a vanishing viscosity justification for the point-vortex approximation of a circular vortex sheet, and we emphasize that reaching time scales of order
\mathcal{O}(T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma})
is a crucial ingredient to carry out this limiting process.

1.3.
Non radial viscous corrections

The original proof of Proposition
1.1
in
[
25
]
already requires constructing an approximate solution that takes into account a non-radial deformation of the vortices due to viscous effects, specifically corresponding to the case
M=4
. This phenomenon of vortex deformation is well known by physicists (see for instance
[
34
,
16
]
), and was also investigated mathematically in more detail in
[
13
]
. Let us discuss and illustrate it briefly in our particular case. There exists a critical value for
\gamma
, which we denote by

(1.20)

\gamma_{N}^{*}:=\frac{(N-1)(N-5)}{12},

which is a threshold in the behavior of the
outer
vortices (meaning all except the central one). When
\gamma>\gamma_{N}^{*}
, the outer vortices deform in an elliptical shape with their larger radius pointing towards the central vortex. When
\gamma<\gamma_{N}^{*}
, the outer vortices deform elliptically with their smaller radius pointing towards the central vortex. The intensity of this deformation is proportional to
\varepsilon^{2}(t)(\gamma-\gamma_{N}^{*})
, and in the critical case
\gamma=\gamma_{N}^{*}
, there is no elliptical deformation of the outer vortices. This can be observed in the numerical simulations presented in Figure
2
. One can still mathematically compute a non-radial deformation at order
\varepsilon^{3}(t)
in that case, which happens to be 3-fold symmetric. Please note that this value
\gamma_{N}^{*}
is the precise choice of
\gamma
that leads to the long-time confinement in the non-viscous case as studied in
[
15
]
.

The central vortex also deforms due to viscous effects. However, the solution
\omega
of the Navier-Stokes equations
Eq.

1.1
with initial data
Eq.

1.4
is
N
-fold symmetric at all times. The central vortex thus can only undergo an
N
-fold symmetric deformation, at order at least
\varepsilon^{N}(t)
. Therefore, unless
N=2
, these effects are very weak, and the instabilities inherent to the numerical simulations will destroy the symmetries before one can hope to see such a subtle deformation become relevant.

Figure 2
.
Initially radially symmetric concentrated vortices (top) evolve in time (bottom), becoming more elliptical with different orientations in the case
\gamma=10
(left) and in the case
\gamma=-10
(middle). In the case
\gamma=\gamma_{6}^{*}=\frac{5}{12}
(right), the vortices do not display any clear 2-fold symmetric deformation. The 3-fold deformation happening in that case, as well as the 6-fold deformation of the central vortices in each case, are too subtle to be seen here. The bottom pictures are taken in all three cases after the whole configuration has completed a rotation of angle
2\pi/3
.

These facts are mostly direct consequences of Theorem
1.2
completed with the explicit computation of the second-order term of the approximate solution that we construct. More examples, with some details and numerical simulations, with various choices of parameters
(N,\gamma)
can be found in Section
6
.

1.4.
Analogies and differences with previous related results

From a technical point of view, the proof of Theorem
1.2
relies on the techniques developed to obtain the analogous result in the simpler configuration of a point vortex dipole in
[
12
]
. That result itself builds upon the recent revisitation of
Arnold’s variational viewpoint
by Gallay and Šverák
[
21
]
, which they successfully applied to the more geometrically involved configuration of a single vortex ring in the 3D axisymmetric Navier–Stokes equations
[
22
]
. The point vortex dipole is arguably the simplest nontrivial configuration to study, in the sense that it enjoys rigid symmetries that prevent violent instability mechanisms, such as the merging commonly observed for like-signed vortices
[
39
]
. From a mathematical perspective, these symmetries are reflected in key properties of the operators appearing in the equation governing the dipole evolution. These properties lead to almost exact symmetries in the approximate solution and crucial cancellations in the nonlinear problem, which play a fundamental role in justifying the validity of the approximate solution
[
12
]
.

In the case of two point vortices with general circulations recently studied by P. Zhang and Y. Zhang in
[
49
]
(corresponding to the case
N=1
,
\gamma\neq-1
in our setting
3
3

3

Note that
N=2
and
\gamma=0
is equivalent to
N=1
and
\gamma=1
up to redefining the center and the distance.
), one can still construct a very accurate approximate solution by adapting the methods of
[
12
]
. However, it is no longer possible to reduce the analysis to a single scalar equation (unless
\gamma=1
), and the operators in the equations exhibit a more complicated dependence on the modulation parameters, such as the radius and angular speed. Although the configuration still enjoys some key structural symmetries, it is less clear how to exploit them effectively at certain steps. This becomes a serious obstacle in proving that the full solution remains close to the approximate one on very long time scales, as in Theorem
1.2
. Indeed, a key point in
[
12
]
is the use of the vertical position as a modulation parameter to fix the centers of vorticity, whereas the horizontal position is automatically fixed by symmetry. For two vortices with general circulations, one can control the vortex centers by passing to a rotating frame and modulating both the angular speed and the mutual distance. However, unlike the dipole case, neither of these parameters is automatically fixed by symmetry. As a consequence, potentially dangerous terms appear in the full nonlinear problem that are not easily controlled by the symmetry arguments. In
[
49
]
, the authors introduced a new strategy to bypass the problems given by some of these terms at the cost of constructing
pseudo-momenta
, which are needed to replace standard first-order moments commonly used to control the vortex centers. We note that these pseudo-momenta are related to the presence of elements in the kernel of the leading-order operator arising from the linearization around the full approximate solution. These kernel elements are themselves linked to the symmetries of the configuration (especially the invariance with respect to a rotation around the origin of the whole configuration). Hence, symmetries still play a key role, but their use is more subtle in
[
49
]
.

For the more general configuration of vortex crystals with a central vortex, we are in a situation that is structurally close to that of
[
49
]
, but the equations are further complicated by nontrivial interactions among all exterior vortices. Nevertheless, we are able to fully exploit the only robust symmetries available, namely the
N
-fold symmetry and the rotational invariance of the total configuration. This allows us to avoid the introduction of pseudo-momenta and instead rely on a key quantity that is directly linked to these symmetries: the
total angular momentum
. This quantity varies significantly only on diffusive time scales (see
Eq.

2.10
), and it can be used to measure viscous changes to the radius of the polygon. By constructing an approximate radius
R_{\rm app}(t)
that precisely follows the vortex centers of the approximate solution, see
Eq.

4.5
, and modulating the angular speed of the full configuration, see
Eq.

5.5
, we use the almost-conservation of the total angular momentum to show that the centers of the exterior vortices can only deviate by a factor of size
\mathcal{O}(\delta\varepsilon^{3})
at most. This is related to a highly nontrivial property of the
Eulerian
part of the approximate solution (namely, the part that approximately solves the inviscid problem), and it relies on suitable cancellations related to the exact conservation of the total angular momentum in the inviscid case, see Lemma
4.22
. This result is a fundamental building block of our analysis that allows us to fix the radius
R(t)
as the approximate one
R_{\rm app}(t)
. It highlights how robust symmetries can be used directly and effectively. At the level of the full nonlinear problem, symmetries continue to play an essential role at several stages of the argument. One key fact is the self-adjointness of an operator used to define an energy functional. Another one is that a potentially dangerous term generated by the modulation of the angular speed belongs (approximately) to the kernel of the operator naturally arising from the linearization around the approximate solution. Both properties are in fact linked to the rotational invariance of the whole configuration.

1.5.
Plan of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we write the equations in self-similar variables and start exploiting the symmetries of the problem to reduce some degrees of freedom. We also introduce the relevant operators and write the equations in a compact form. In Section
3
, we discuss the important properties of these operators and their associated functional spaces that will be of use later, setting up the analytic framework for our proof. In Section
4
, we construct the approximate solution
\omega_{\mathrm{app}}
and compute explicitly the first few viscous corrections. Then, Section
5
concludes the proof of Theorem
1.2
by obtaining the control of the difference
|\omega-\omega_{\mathrm{app}}|
in the relevant norms, from which we deduce the desired behavior of
\omega
. In Section
6
we present a comparison of our results with numerical simulations made with
Basilisk
.
In the appendices, we give the details of some proofs that are non-trivial but follow by explicit computations related to symmetries, and we also compute terms for which obtaining an explicit expression is interesting but not necessary to the proofs.

2.
Reformulation of the problem

In this section we reformulate the problem and we set up the notation and framework used in the rest of the paper.

2.1.
Notations and conventions

With the adimensional parameters introduced in
Eq.

1.8
, we will work in the asymptotic regime
\delta\to 0
and, fixing
\Gamma
once and for all, this is equivalent to the standard vanishing viscosity regime
\nu\to 0
. However, it will be very convenient to perform expansions in terms of
\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon^{2}=\delta\frac{t}{T_{\mathsf{adv}}}
, we have that
\varepsilon(t)\ll 1\Longleftrightarrow\delta\ll 1
uniformly on the time interval
t\in T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma}
for any
\sigma<1
. This leads us to the following notation.

Definition 2.1
.

Given a map
t\mapsto f(t)
evolving in a space
X
, we denote by
f=\mathcal{O}_{X}(\varepsilon)
the fact that for any given
\sigma<1
, there exists a constant
C
not depending on
\delta
and
t
, and a constant
\delta_{0}>0
such that for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
and for every
t\leq T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma}
, there holds that
\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{X}\leq C\varepsilon(t)
. When
f
depends on
t
only through
\varepsilon(t)
, this definition conveniently coincides with considering
\varepsilon
as an abstract parameter and taking the asymptotics
\varepsilon\to 0
, hence the use of the notation
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Note also that, in the definition of
\delta,T_{\mathsf{adv}},T_{\mathsf{diff}}
, we could replace
\Gamma
with
\Gamma\max\{1,|\gamma|\}
. However, it would simply change how the parameter
\gamma
scales in the subsequent analysis; therefore, we prefer to use the same conventions that have already appeared in
[
12
]
.

Relative equilibria in the point vortex system are intimately related to conserved quantities in
Eq.

1.2
. For the dynamics of
Eq.

1.1
, two important quantities are the
mean

\mathrm{M}(\omega)
and the
linear momentum

\mathrm{m}^{1}(\omega)=(\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\omega),\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(\omega))
defined as

(2.1)

\mathrm{M}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x){\rm d}x,\qquad\mathrm{m}^{1}_{j}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{j}\omega(x){\rm d}x,\quad i=1,2.

We will perform operations on vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as if they were complex numbers. For instance, given
a=(a_{1},a_{2})\leftrightarrow a_{1}+ia_{2}
and
b=(b_{1},b_{2})\leftrightarrow b_{1}+ib_{2}
we identify

(2.2)

ab\leftrightarrow(a_{1}b_{1}-a_{2}b_{2},a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1})\qquad\text{or}\qquad a^{-1}b\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{|a|^{2}}(a\cdot b,a^{\perp}\cdot b).

We also identify the rotation matrices

(2.3)

Q_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\frac{2\pi}{N}j)&-\sin(\frac{2\pi}{N}j)\\
\sin(\frac{2\pi}{N}j)&\cos(\frac{2\pi}{N}j)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\text{ for }j=1,\dots,N.

with the complex number
{\rm e}^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}j}
.
For a vector
a=(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we write
{\rm Re}(a)=a_{1},\,{\rm Im}(a)=a_{2}
. Then, with this notation we define the
k
-th moment vectors as

(2.4)

{\rm m}^{k}(f)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\xi^{k}f(\xi){\rm d}\xi,\qquad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}.

As an example, we compute that
{\rm m}^{0}(f)=({\rm M}(f),0)
which we will identify with
\mathrm{M}(f)
and

(2.5)

{\rm m}^{1}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})f(\xi){\rm d}\xi,\qquad{\rm m}^{2}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2},2\xi_{1}\xi_{2})f(\xi){\rm d}\xi.

Then, we need to make use of the angular momentum that we define through the operator

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(f)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\xi|^{2}f(\xi){\rm d}\xi.

We denote the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
inner product as

(2.7)

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\xi)g(\xi){\rm d}\xi.

We conclude this section with the introduction of streamfunction and Poisson brackets. The incompressibility condition
\nabla\cdot u=0
implies the existence of a streamfunction
\psi
such that
u=\nabla^{\perp}\psi
and
\Delta\psi=\omega
. To get the expression of
\psi
in terms of
\omega
, and thus recover the Biot-Savart law in
Eq.

1.1
, one can invert the Laplacian with the following formula:

(2.8)

\psi(x,t)=[\Delta^{-1}\omega(\cdot,t)](x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|x-y|\omega(y,t){\rm d}y.

We denote by
\{f,g\}=\nabla^{\perp}f\cdot\nabla g=({\partial}_{1}f)({\partial}_{2}g)-({\partial}_{2}f)({\partial}_{1}g)
the standard Poisson bracket of
f
and
g
and then notice, since we will make extensive use of that notation, that the first two equations in the Navier-Stokes system
Eq.

1.1
can be changed to
Eq.

2.8
and

(2.9)

\partial_{t}\omega+\{\psi,\omega\}=\nu\Delta\omega.

2.2.
Symmetries and self-similar variables

For the 2D Navier-Stokes equation
Eq.

1.1
, it is well known that the total angular momentum
\mathrm{L}(\omega(t))
, as defined in
Eq.

2.6
, can be computed explicitly and changes significantly only after diffusive times. This is related to the fact that the equation is rotationally invariant and that
\mathrm{L}(\omega(t))
is exactly conserved when
\nu=0
. We then recall the following basic fact.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\omega
be the solution to
Eq.

1.1
with initial data
Eq.

1.4
. Then

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\omega(t))=\mathrm{L}(\omega^{in})+4\nu t\mathrm{M}(\omega^{in})=\Gamma N\big(\mathsf{r}^{2}+4\nu t\big(1+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big)\big).

Proof.

By testing the equation
Eq.

1.1
with
|x|^{2}
, we get

(2.11)

{\partial}_{t}\mathrm{L}(\omega)=-\langle\{\psi,\omega\},|x|^{2}\rangle+\nu\langle\Delta\omega,|x|^{2}\rangle.

Exploiting the identities

(2.12)

\displaystyle\langle\{\psi,\omega\},|x|^{2}\rangle=\langle\psi,\{\omega,|x|^{2}\}\rangle=-2\langle\psi,{\partial}_{\theta}\omega\rangle=-2\langle\psi,{\partial}_{\theta}\Delta\psi\rangle=0

(2.13)

\displaystyle\nu\langle\Delta\omega,|x|^{2}\rangle=4\nu\mathrm{M}(\omega(t))=4\nu\Gamma(N+\gamma),

the result follows by integrating in time.
∎

The initial data
Eq.

1.4
is
N
-fold symmetric and it is well known that the Navier-Stokes equations
Eq.

1.1
preserve this symmetry.
We can thus decompose the solution of equations
Eq.

1.1
as

(2.14)

\omega(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{\omega}\big(Q_{-j}x,t)+\widehat{\omega}(x,t)

where
\tilde{\omega}
is the vorticity profile of each exterior vortices and
\widehat{\omega}
is the vorticity profile of the central vortex.
Recalling that the mean
M(\omega)
and the linear momentum
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\omega)
are conserved, the
N
-fold symmetry has the following consequence on the central blob of vorticity.

Lemma 2.3
.

For every
t\geq 0
, we have that
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\omega}(t))=0
.

Proof.

By conservation of
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\omega(t))
, we have that

(2.15)

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\omega(x,t){\rm d}x=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\tilde{\omega}(Q_{-j}x,t){\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\widehat{\omega}(x,t){\rm d}x.

Then, we compute by change of variables that

(2.16)

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\tilde{\omega}(Q_{-j}x,t){\rm d}x=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{j}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\tilde{\omega}(x,t){\rm d}x=0,

hence,

(2.17)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\widehat{\omega}(x,t){\rm d}x=0.

∎

As is usually done in the context of studying solutions arising from Dirac masses in the Navier-Stokes equations (see
[
25
,
12
,
13
,
49
]
), we introduce the rescaled vorticity functions

(2.18)

\tilde{\omega}(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\Omega\left(\frac{x-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}},t\right)

and

(2.19)

\widehat{\omega}(x,t)=\gamma\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu t}},t\right),

where

(2.20)

Z(t)=(Z_{1}(t),Z_{2}(t))

is a quantity to choose that acts as the position of the
N
-th exterior vortex. By
N
-fold symmetry, the positions of the other exterior vortices are
(Q_{j}Z(t))_{j}
, and from
Lemma

2.3
, we choose to define the position of the central vortex to be 0.

Denoting by
\xi
and
\widehat{\xi}
the new variables, namely letting

(2.21)

\xi=\frac{x-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}}

and

(2.22)

\widehat{\xi}=\frac{x}{\sqrt{\nu t}},

and letting for
1\leq j\leq N

(2.23)

\xi_{j}=\frac{Q_{-j}x-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}}=Q_{-j}\xi+\frac{Q_{-j}Z(t)-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}},

we arrive in the end to the following ansatz for
\omega
in the self-similar variables:

(2.24)

\omega(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega(\xi_{j},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\xi},t).

Then, we also decompose the streamfunction as

(2.25)

\psi(x,t)=\Gamma\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Psi(\xi_{j},t)+\gamma\Gamma\widehat{\Psi}(\widehat{\xi},t),

where

(2.26)

\Psi=\Delta^{-1}\Omega\;\text{ and }\;\widehat{\Psi}=\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega},\quad\text{recalling that}\quad\Delta^{-1}f(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\xi-\eta|f(\eta){\rm d}\eta.

For later needs, we observe that we have the following identities to express
\xi_{l}
in terms of
\xi_{j}
for
1\leq j,l\leq N
:

(2.27)

\displaystyle\xi_{l}

\displaystyle=Q_{-l+j}\xi_{j}+\frac{Q_{-l+j}Z(t)-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\xi_{l}

\displaystyle=Q_{-l}\widehat{\xi}-\frac{Z(t)}{\nu t}

(2.29)

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}

\displaystyle=Q_{j}\xi_{j}+\frac{Q_{j}Z(t)}{\nu t}.

We then have the following symmetry result for the central vortex.

Lemma 2.4
.

The functions
\widehat{\Omega}
and
\widehat{\Psi}
are
N
-fold symmetric.

Proof.

We know that
\omega
is
N
-fold symmetric, therefore, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and every
l\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

(2.30)

\omega(x,t)=\omega(Q_{j}x,t)

and thus

(2.31)

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega(\xi_{j},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\xi},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega\bigg(\frac{Q_{-j+l}x-Z(t)}{\sqrt{\nu t}},t\bigg)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(Q_{l}\widehat{\xi},t)

(2.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega(\xi_{j-l},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(Q_{l}\widehat{\xi},t)

with the convention that
\xi_{-k}=\xi_{N-k}
for
k\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
. By reorganizing the terms we get

(2.33)

\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega(\xi_{j},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\xi},t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega(\xi_{j},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\widehat{\Omega}(Q_{l}\widehat{\xi},t)

namely

(2.34)

\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\xi},t)=\widehat{\Omega}(Q_{l}\widehat{\xi},t).

Thus
\widehat{\Omega}
is
N
-fold symmetric, and therefore
\widehat{\Psi}
is too.
∎

2.3.
Expression of the system in self-similar variables and rotating frame

Since we expect the system to be rigidly rotating with the unknown angular speed
\alpha^{\prime}(t)
, it is convenient to first reformulate
Eq.

1.1
in a rotating frame. Thus, with a slight abuse of notation we keep the same notation for
\omega(x,t)
and
\omega(Q_{-N\alpha(t)}x,t)
(with
Q_{-N\alpha(t)}
defined as
Q_{j}
in
Eq.

2.3
with
j=-N\alpha(t)
) and we obtain the Navier-Stokes equations on a rotating frame with angular speed
\alpha^{\prime}
which are given by

(2.35)

{\partial}_{t}\omega+\big\{\psi-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}|x|^{2},\omega\big\}=\nu\Delta\omega.

We will refer to the extra term involving
\alpha^{\prime}
as the
Coriolis term
.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that the identity in Lemma
2.2
is not affected by the presence of the Coriolis term, since this term does not contribute to the evolution of
\mathrm{L}(\omega(t))
because
\langle\{\psi,|x|^{2}\},|x|^{2}\rangle=0
.

To write down the system satisfied by
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
, we need to introduce some operators:

•

The (rescaled) diffusion operator

(2.36)

\mathcal{L}:=\Delta_{\xi}+\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla_{\xi}+1.

•

The translation operator

(2.37)

(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon(t),Z(t),d}f)(\xi)\coloneqq(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}f)(\xi)=f\Big(\xi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon(t)d}Z(t)\Big)

•

The
polygon-center
operator

(2.38)

(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon(t),Z(t),d,N}f)(\xi)\coloneqq(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}f)(\xi)=\sum_{l=1}^{N}f\Big(Q_{l}\xi-\frac{1}{\varepsilon(t)d}Z(t)\Big)

•

The
polygon-polygon
operator

(2.39)

(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon(t),Z(t),d,N}f)(\xi)\coloneqq(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}f)(\xi)=\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}f\Big(Q_{l}\xi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon(t)d}(Q_{l}-I)Z(t)\Big).

We then have the following.

Lemma 2.6
.

The vorticity
\omega
defined in
Eq.

2.24
satisfies
Eq.

2.35
if
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
solve the following system

(2.40)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}\Omega=\mathcal{L}\Omega-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z})\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\Psi}-\frac{\delta t}{\varepsilon d}\xi^{\perp}\cdot Z^{\prime}-\frac{\delta t}{2}\alpha^{\prime}\big|\xi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon d}Z(t)\big|^{2}\,,\,\Omega\Big\}

(2.41)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}=\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{\gamma\widehat{\Psi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}\Psi-\frac{\delta t}{2}\alpha^{\prime}|\xi|^{2}\,,\,\widehat{\Omega}\Big\}

(2.42)

\displaystyle\Omega(\xi,0)=\widehat{\Omega}(\xi,0)=\frac{1}{4\pi}e^{-|\xi|^{2}/4},\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

We first compute the terms associated to the heat-equation part in
Eq.

2.35
. Hence, we observe that

(2.43)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\omega(x,t)-\nu\Delta\omega(x,t)=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big(t{\partial}_{t}\Omega-\mathcal{L}\Omega-\sqrt{\frac{t}{\nu}}Z^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\Omega\big)(\xi_{j},t)

(2.44)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t^{2}}\big(t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}-\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}\big)(\widehat{\xi},t).

For the Poisson bracket terms, we compute using relation
Eq.

2.24
that

(2.45)

\{|\cdot|^{2},\omega\}_{x}=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\{|\cdot|^{2},\Omega(\xi_{j}(\cdot),t)\}_{x}+\frac{\gamma\Gamma}{\nu t}\{|\cdot|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{\xi}(\cdot),t)\}_{x},

where we interpreted the
\xi_{j}
as functions of
x
. We now want to remove the map composition in the Poisson bracket, namely express it in terms of the maps
\Omega
and
\widehat{\Omega}
with their natural variable
\xi
, applied in
\xi_{j}
and
\widehat{\xi}
. To this end, we express
|x|^{2}
in terms of
\xi_{j}
using relation
Eq.

2.23
to get that

(2.46)

x=\sqrt{\nu t}Q_{j}(\xi_{j}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon d}Z(t)),\qquad\text{for }j=1,\dots,N.

Then, to complete this change of variable, we compute using relations
Eq.

2.22
and
Eq.

2.23
that for
0\leq j\leq N
,

(2.47)

\{(\xi_{j})_{1},(\xi_{j})_{2}\}_{x}=\frac{1}{\nu t},

and thus, recalling the well-known identity for changing variables inside a Poisson bracket

(2.48)

\{f(\xi(\cdot)),g(\xi(\cdot))\}_{x}(x)=\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}_{x}(x)\{f,g\}_{\xi}(\xi(x)),

we obtain that

(2.49)

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\{|\cdot|^{2},\omega\}_{x}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\alpha^{\prime}(t)}{2\nu t}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\big\{\big|Q_{j}\big(\cdot+\frac{1}{\varepsilon d}Z(t)\big)\big|^{2},\Omega(\cdot,t)\big\}_{\xi}(\xi_{j})

(2.50)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma\Gamma\alpha^{\prime}(t)}{2\nu t}\big\{|\cdot|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}(\cdot,t)\big\}_{\xi}(\widehat{\xi}).

Similarly, keeping the notations compact, we have that

(2.51)

\displaystyle\{\psi,\omega\}_{x}(x,t)=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma^{2}}{\nu^{2}t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\bigg\{\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Psi(\xi_{l}),\Omega\bigg\}_{\xi}(\xi_{j},t)+\frac{\gamma^{2}\Gamma^{2}}{\nu^{2}t^{2}}\{\widehat{\Psi},\widehat{\Omega}\}_{\xi}(\widehat{\xi},t)

(2.52)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma\Gamma^{2}}{\nu^{2}t^{2}}\bigg\{\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Psi(\xi_{l}),\widehat{\Omega}\bigg\}_{\xi}(\widehat{\xi},t)+\frac{\gamma\Gamma^{2}}{\nu^{2}t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\{\widehat{\Psi}(\widehat{\xi}),\Omega\}_{\xi}(\xi_{j},t).

One should remember that in this expression,
\xi_{l}
is seen as a function of
\xi_{j}
given by relation
Eq.

2.27
. In particular, this means that

(2.53)

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Psi(\xi_{l},t)

\displaystyle=\Psi(\xi_{j},t)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}\Psi\Big(Q_{-l+j}\xi_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}(Q_{-l+j}-I)Z(t),t\Big)

(2.54)

\displaystyle=\Psi(\xi_{j},t)+\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\Psi\Big(Q_{-l}\xi_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}(Q_{-l}-I)Z(t),t\Big)

(2.55)

\displaystyle=((I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z})\Psi)(\xi_{j},t)

where we used the definition of the operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}
in
Eq.

2.39
and simply reordered the terms in the second equality. Analogously,

(2.56)

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Psi(\xi_{l},t)=\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Psi\bigg(Q_{-l}\widehat{\xi}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}Z(t),t\bigg)=(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}\Psi)(\widehat{\xi},t),

(2.57)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Psi}(\widehat{\xi},t)=\widehat{\Psi}\bigg(Q_{j}\xi_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}Q_{j}Z(t),t\bigg)=\widehat{\Psi}\bigg(\xi_{j}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}Z(t),t\bigg)=(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\Psi})(\xi_{j},t),

where we used that
\widehat{\Psi}
is
N
-fold symmetric. Collecting all the identities above, since
\sqrt{t/\nu}=t/(\varepsilon d)
and
Z^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\Omega=\{\xi^{\perp}\cdot Z,\Omega\}_{\xi}
, we find that we solve
Eq.

1.1
if

(2.58)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\bigg(t{\partial}_{t}\Omega-\mathcal{L}\Omega+\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z})\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\Psi}-\frac{\delta t}{\varepsilon d}\xi^{\perp}\cdot Z^{\prime}-\frac{\delta t}{2}\alpha^{\prime}|\xi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon d}Z(t)|^{2},\Omega\Big\}_{\xi}\bigg)(\xi_{j},t)

(2.59)

\displaystyle\,+\gamma\bigg(t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}-\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}+\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{\gamma\widehat{\Psi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}\Psi-\frac{\delta t}{2}\alpha^{\prime}|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}\Big\}_{\xi}\bigg)(\widehat{\xi},t)=0.

Therefore, when
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
satisfy
Eq.

2.40
-
Eq.

2.41
we clearly have the identity above.
To assign an initial data to the equations
Eq.

2.40
-
Eq.

2.41
, we argue as in
[
12
]
. By the uniqueness result in
[
20
]
we deduce that

(2.60)

\Omega_{0}(\xi)=\widehat{\Omega}_{0}(\xi)=G(\xi),\qquad\text{ with }\qquad G(\xi)=\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm e}^{-|\xi|^{2}/4}.

∎

We introduce the notation for the initial velocity and streamfunction as

(2.61)

U_{0}(\xi)\,\,:=\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\xi^{\perp}}{|\xi|^{2}}\,\Bigl(1-e^{-|\xi|^{2}/4}\Bigr)\,,\qquad\Psi_{0}(\xi)\,:=\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\mathrm{Ein}\bigl(|\xi|^{2}/4\bigr)-\gamma_{E}\big)\,,

where
\gamma_{E}\approx 0,5772
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant and
{\rm Ein}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}s^{-1}(1-{\rm e}^{-s}){\rm d}s
is the exponential integral function. In the sequel, we will need to use also the function

(2.62)

A(\xi)=-\frac{\nabla\Psi_{0}(\xi)}{\nabla\Omega_{0}(\xi)}=\frac{4}{|\xi|^{2}}({\rm e}^{|\xi|^{2}/4}-1).

2.4.
Choice of position and angular speed

In the system
Eq.

2.40
-
Eq.

2.41
, and already in the definition of the self-similar variables, we introduced
Z
and
\alpha
as given quantities to be chosen later.
The key point in the choice of these modulation parameters is to ensure that the center of vorticity of the blobs remains centered in our new coordinates, namely that

(2.63)

\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega(t))=\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))=(0,0),\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0.

This specific choice is necessary for technical needs, but for very concentrated vortices, the center of vorticity is also a good estimation of the location of most of the mass of the vortex
4
4

4

In fact, to determine the position of the vortices, one could decide to follow the maximum of vorticity or the stagnation point. None of these choices are equivalent in general and we prefer to fix the center of vorticity blobs for later convenience.
. Recall that for the central vortex
\widehat{\Omega}
, the condition above is automatically guaranteed from the
N
-fold symmetry with
N\geq 2
, see
Lemma

2.3
.

We are thus left with the freedom of choosing three parameters
Z_{1}(t),Z_{2}(t),\alpha(t)
to fix only two conditions on
\Omega
. This is simply because those three variables are linked by a triangular relation. Therefore, we choose to set

(2.64)

Z_{2}(t)=0\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

which we can do without loss of generality by adjusting the choice of
\alpha
.
The quantity
Z_{1}(t)
is now the distance to the center of the
N
-th vortex, meaning that it is the radius of our regular polygon and we thus call it

(2.65)

\displaystyle Z_{1}(t)=R(t),\qquad R(0)=\mathsf{r}.

Then, in analogy with
[
12
,
22
]
, the choice that fixes the center of mass is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
be the solutions to
Eqs.

2.40
,
2.41
and
2.42
. Define the radius of the vortex polygon and the angular speed of the solution to be

(2.66)

\displaystyle tR^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big({\partial}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\widehat{\Psi})\Omega\big)(\xi,t){\rm d}\xi,

(2.67)

\displaystyle t\alpha^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta R(t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big({\partial}_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\widehat{\Psi})\Omega\big)(\xi,t){\rm d}\xi,

with
\alpha(0)=0
and
R(0)=\mathsf{r}
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
the following moment identities hold true:

(2.68)

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}(\Omega(t))=\mathrm{M}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))=1,\quad\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega(t))=\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))=(0,0).

Proof.

The proof for the mean is straightforward. In the sequel, we need the Poisson bracket identities
\{f,g\}=-\{g,f\}
and

(2.69)

\langle\{f,g\},h\rangle=\langle f,\{g,h\}\rangle.

Moreover, we have the following identity involving the operator
\mathcal{L}

(2.70)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}(f),\xi_{j}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{m}_{j}^{1}(f).

Since

{\partial}_{1}f=\{f,\xi_{2}\},\quad{\partial}_{2}f=-\{f,\xi_{1}\}

and
{\partial}_{i}
commute with
\Delta^{-1}
, it is not hard to verify also that

(2.71)

\displaystyle\langle\{\Delta^{-1}f,f\},\xi_{i}\rangle=0,\qquad\text{ for }i=1,2.

Then, since
\mathrm{M}(\Omega)=1
and
Z(t)=(R(t),0)
, by testing
Eq.

2.40
with
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
we deduce that

(2.72)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega)-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\Psi})\Omega\,{\rm d}\xi-\frac{tR^{\prime}(t)}{\varepsilon d}+t\alpha^{\prime}(t)\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(\Omega),

(2.73)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(\Omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(\Omega)+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\partial}_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\Psi})\Omega\,{\rm d}\xi-t\alpha^{\prime}(t)\big(\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega)+\frac{R}{\varepsilon d}\big).

By the choice of
R^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}
in
Eqs.

2.66
and
2.67
, the equation above simplifies to

(2.74)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega)-t\alpha^{\prime}(t)(\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega))^{\perp}.

The identity above readily implies that

(2.75)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}(t|\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega)|^{2})=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad|\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega(t))|^{2}=0,\text{ for all }t>0.

Since clearly
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega(0))=\mathrm{m}^{1}(G)=(0,0)
the proof of the lemma is over.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

The choice of the modulation parameters
R
and
\alpha
as in Lemma
2.7
is fundamental in the construction of the approximate solution. Indeed, we avoid the iterative construction in
[
12
,
49
]
by showing that the choices in
Eqs.

2.66
and
2.67
can be used to fix the first-order moments of the approximate solution. However, in the full nonlinear problem, we do not choose the radius as in
Eq.

2.66
, but instead fix it to be the one obtained from the approximate solution. This is because the operators depend in a complicated way on the radius, and a fully nonlinear choice as in
Eq.

2.66
introduces several technical difficulties (an issue also present in
[
49
]
). Thus, we will drop the requirement that
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega(t))=0
. From the proof of Lemma
2.7
, it is straightforward to see that one can still fix
\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(\Omega(t))=0
with the choice

(2.76)

\displaystyle t\alpha^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta(R+\varepsilon d\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega))(t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big({\partial}_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}\widehat{\Psi})\Omega\big)(\xi,t){\rm d}\xi,

for any
R(t)
. On the other hand, we now have to guarantee that
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega(t))
remains sufficiently small on the time scale of interest. To this end, we fully exploit a property of the approximate solution related to the evolution of the angular momentum in Lemma
2.2
(see Lemma
4.22
).

2.5.
Equations in reduced form

In view of the previous sections, we introduce the operator

(2.77)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,Z}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla^{\perp}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}\varphi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\widehat{\varphi})\Delta\widetilde{\varphi}{\rm d}\xi,

and by abuse of notation, define

(2.78)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,Z}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}):=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,Z}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\varphi).

We make once and for all the choice that the parameter
Z(t)
of the change of variable
Eq.

2.21
is given by

Z(t)=(R(t),0),

and we identify
(R(t),0)\simeq R
for notational convenience.

Then, we introduce

(2.79)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})=\Psi+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\widehat{\Psi}+\xi_{2}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi\big|^{2}

(2.80)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})=\gamma\widehat{\Psi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Psi-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,Z}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))|\xi|^{2}.

When the parameters
R,\alpha
from the rotating frame are defined by relations
Eqs.

2.66
and
2.67
, observe that

(2.81)

tR^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon d}{\delta}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})),\qquad t\alpha^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\varepsilon d}{\delta R}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))

with
\Psi
and
\widehat{\Psi}
the streamfunctions of the solutions to equations
Eqs.

2.40
,
2.41
and
2.42
.
Equipped with all the previous notations, and with this specific choice of parameters, we obtain that
\Omega
and
\widehat{\Omega}
are solutions of the system
Eqs.

2.40
and
2.41
if and only if

(2.82)

t{\partial}_{t}\Omega=\mathcal{L}\Omega-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}),\Omega\Big\},\qquad t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}=\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}),\widehat{\Omega}\Big\},

and in that case, by Lemma
2.7
, we have that
\mathrm{M}(\Omega(t))=\mathrm{M}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))=1
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega(t))=\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))=(0,0)
.
Moreover, when there will be no ambiguity, we even use the notation
\Phi=\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})
and
\widehat{\Phi}=\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})
, so that we arrive to the compact form

(2.83)

t{\partial}_{t}\Omega=\mathcal{L}\Omega-\frac{1}{\delta}\{\Phi,\Omega\},\qquad t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}=\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}-\frac{1}{\delta}\{\widehat{\Phi},\widehat{\Omega}\},

for the system
Eqs.

2.40
and
2.41
. The use of such similar notation in the equations for the vortex at one vertex
\Omega
and the central vortex
\widehat{\Omega}
is made to stress the structural analogies. Indeed, the construction of the approximate solution follows a rather general asymptotic expansion scheme, in which the differences are all encoded in the different expansions for
\Phi
and
\widehat{\Phi}
.
Therefore, we will often present detailed computations for
\Omega
, which satisfies the most complicated equation, while only highlighting the interesting differences for
\widehat{\Omega}
when needed. One has to remember that equations
Eq.

2.83
are coupled since
\Phi
depends on
\widehat{\Omega}
and vice versa.

3.
Function spaces, operators and their asymptotic expansion

In this section, following
[
24
,
25
,
12
]
, we introduce the function spaces needed to construct the approximate solution, and state some relevant properties of the operators introduced above. As some facts are either well-known, or simply coming from explicit computations, some details and proofs are delayed to Appendix
A
.

3.1.
Function spaces

We first define the weighted
L^{2}
spaces

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\,

\displaystyle=\,\biggl\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,:\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\xi)|^{2}\,{\rm e}^{|\xi|^{2}/4}\,{\rm d}\xi<\infty\biggr\}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\,

\displaystyle=\,\bigl\{f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\,:\,\xi\mapsto{\rm e}^{|\xi|^{2}/4}f(\xi)\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr\}\,,

where
\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denotes the space of smooth functions with at most
polynomial growth at infinity. Note that
\mathcal{Z}
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{Y}
. The space
\mathcal{Y}
is a Hilbert spaces equipped with the inner product

(3.3)

\langle{f,g}\rangle_{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\xi)\,g(\xi)\,{\rm e}^{|\xi|^{2}/4}\,{\rm d}\xi\,,\qquad\forall\,f,g\in\mathcal{Y}.

In view of the Fourier transform in the angular variable in polar coordinates
\xi=(r\cos(\theta),r\sin(\theta))
, we also have the orthogonal decomposition

(3.4)

\mathcal{Y}\,=\,\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\mathcal{Y}_{n}\,,

where
\mathcal{Y}_{n}=\bigl\{f\in\mathcal{Y}\,:\,f(\xi)=a(r)\cos(n\theta)+b(r)\sin(n\theta)\text{ with }a,b:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}\bigr\}
. We also denote with
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the
L^{2}
-projection operator onto the
n
-th angular Fourier mode.

Then, we recall the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}
notation introduced in
[
12
, Definition 3.4]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be a positive integer.

1)
If
g_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
depends on a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
,
we say that
g_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M}\bigr)
if, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and all
m\in\{0,\dots,M\}
there exists
C>0
and
\widetilde{N}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(3.5)

|\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}{\partial}_{\varepsilon}^{m}g_{\varepsilon}(\xi)|\leq C(1+|\xi|)^{\widetilde{N}}\varepsilon^{M-m}\quad\forall\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

2) Similarly, if
f_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{Z}
, we write
f_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M}\bigr)
if
{\rm e}^{|\xi|^{2}/4}f_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M}\bigr)
.

Remark 3.2
.

In the sequel, we will not explicitly verify the conditions on the derivatives in Definition
3.1
. Indeed, in the asymptotic expansions of the operators proved in Appendix
A
, we either have errors involving tails of explicit integrals or errors arising by truncating a Taylor series. In both cases, it is straightforward to check the conditions for the derivatives in
Definition

3.1
.

3.2.
Diffusion and advection operators

We now recall properties of the difussion operator
\mathcal{L}
in
Eq.

2.36
and the
advection
operator arising from the linearization of the Euler equations at the Lamb-Oseen vortex.

i)
The diffusion operator

The diffusion operator
\mathcal{L}
defined by
Eq.

2.36
is
a linear operator in
\mathcal{Y}
with (maximal) domain

(3.6)

D(\mathcal{L})\,=\,\bigl\{f\in\mathcal{Y}\,:\,\Delta f\in\mathcal{Y},\,\xi\cdot\nabla f\in\mathcal{Y}\bigr\}\,.

It is well known, see
[
23
, Appendix A]
, that
\mathcal{L}
is
self-adjoint
in

\mathcal{Y}
with compact resolvent and purely discrete spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{L})\,=\,\{-n/2\,:\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}\,
. We have
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{span}(G)
where
G
is the Gaussian function defined in
Eq.

2.60
. The eigenspace corresponding to the eigenvalue
-1/2
is
\mathrm{span}({\partial}_{1}G,{\partial}_{2}G)
. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that if
f\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}\cap D(\mathcal{L})
, then
\mathcal{L}f\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}
. We also recall the result
[
12
, Lemma 3.5]
, telling us the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
\kappa>0
and any
f\in\mathcal{Z}
one has
(\kappa-\mathcal{L})^{-1}f\in\mathcal{Z}
.

ii)

The advection operator

We also need the operator associated to the linearization of the
2D
Euler equation at the Lamb-Oseen vortex. This is denoted by
\Lambda:D(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{Y}
, and is defined as

(3.7)

\Lambda f\,=\,\bigl\{\Psi_{0}\,,\,f\bigr\}+\bigl\{\Delta^{-1}f\,,\,\Omega_{0}\}\,,\qquad f\in D(\Lambda)\,,

where
\Omega_{0},\Psi_{0}
are defined in
Eq.

2.60
,
Eq.

2.61
.
The operator
\Lambda
is considered in its maximal domain

D(\Lambda)\,=\,\bigl\{f\in\mathcal{Y}\,:\,\{\Psi_{0},f\}\in\mathcal{Y}\bigr\}\,
and we also have that
\Lambda
is invariant under rotations
about the origin, so that it commutes with the direct sum decomposition

Eq.

3.4
. Moreover, it is clear that
\Lambda f\in\mathcal{Z}
if
f\in\mathcal{Z}
. In view of the definition of
A
in
Eq.

2.62
, we also note that we can write

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Lambda f=\{(A+\Delta^{-1})f,\Omega_{0}\}.

We then recall the following properties, that are well known by now, e.g.
[
25
]
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The following properties holds true:

(1)

The operator
\Lambda
is skew-adjoint in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{Y}
with
kernel

(3.9)

\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)\,=\,\mathcal{Y}_{0}\,\oplus\,\bigl\{\beta_{1}\partial_{1}G+\beta_{2}\partial_{2}G\,:\,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\bigr\}\,.

(2)

If
h\in\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{Z}
or
h\in\mathcal{Z}
with
\mathcal{P}_{0}(h)=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(h)=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(h)=0
the equation
\Lambda f=h

has a unique solution
f\in\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{Z}
, and
f
is an
even (odd) function of the variable
\xi_{2}
if
h
is an odd (even) function of
\xi_{2}
.

We remark that, in fact, to determine the leading order terms in the construction of the approximate solution one has to invert
\Lambda
on functions of the type
h=\{\varphi,\Omega_{0}\}
with
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{*}
. Since
\Omega_{0}
is radial, there is the degree of freedom of adding any radial function to
\varphi
, and we therefore fix
\mathcal{P}_{0}\varphi=0
to remove this issue. Then, thanks to the representation
Eq.

3.8
and Proposition
3.4
, we know that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Lambda f=\{\varphi,\Omega_{0}\}\quad\iff\quad(A+\Delta^{-1})f=\varphi.

Properties of the operator
A+\Delta^{-1}
on the space

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=\Big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,:\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\xi)|^{2}A(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi<\infty\Big\}

have been carefully studied in
[
21
, Section 2]
. For our purposes, observe that
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\{\varphi,\Omega_{0}\})=0
and therefore

(3.12)

\displaystyle\{\varphi,\Omega_{0}\}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\quad\iff\quad\langle\varphi,{\partial}_{i}\Omega_{0}\rangle=0\quad\text{for }i=1,2,

namely
\varphi
is orthogonal to
{\partial}_{i}\Omega_{0}
for
i=1,2
with respect to the standard
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
inner product. Moreover, solving the second equation in
Eq.

3.10
in the space
\mathcal{X}
is equivalent to the following problem in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(3.13)

\displaystyle\big({\rm Id}-A^{-\frac{1}{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)F=\phi,\quad\text{with }F=A^{\frac{1}{2}}f\text{ and }\phi=A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi.

Having that
A
is radially symmetric, we can as well introduce the angular Fourier decomposition, meaning that we can solve equation
Eq.

3.13
mode by mode. The problem is then reduced in proving a bound for the operator
T_{n}:=\mathcal{P}_{n}(A^{-\frac{1}{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}A^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathcal{P}_{n}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which can be represented as

(3.14)

\displaystyle T_{n}[g]=\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\min\big(\frac{r}{s},\frac{s}{r}\big)^{n}}{\sqrt{A(r)A(s)}}g(s)s\,{\rm d}s,\quad\text{ with }g\in L^{2}(r{\rm d}r).

In
[
21
]
, it was proved that there exists
0<c<1
such that

(3.15)

\|T_{n}\|_{X_{n}\to X_{n}}\leq\frac{c}{n},\qquad X_{n}=\begin{cases}L^{2}(r{\rm d}r)\qquad&n\geq 2\\
\{g\in L^{2}(r{\rm d}r)\,:\,\langle g,G^{\prime}A^{\frac{1}{2}}\rangle_{L^{2}(r{\rm d}r)}=0\}\qquad&n=1.\end{cases}

When
n=1
, the orthogonality condition in
Eq.

3.15
is equivalent to
Eq.

3.12
. This is related to the fact that
T_{1}[G^{\prime}A^{\frac{1}{2}}]=G^{\prime}A^{\frac{1}{2}}

5
5

5

To prove this idendity, recall that in polar coordinates
A=-\Psi_{0}^{\prime}/\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
with
({\partial}_{rr}+r^{-1}{\partial}_{r})\Psi_{0}=\Omega_{0}
. Thus
\Delta_{1}\Psi_{0}^{\prime}=\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
and therefore
(-\Delta_{1})^{-1}\Omega_{0}^{\prime}=-\Psi_{0}^{\prime}=A\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
, which proves the desired identity after dividing by
A^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
, meaning that
G^{\prime}A^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathrm{Ker}({\rm Id}-T_{1})
. Moreover, one can deduce that
1
is the largest eigenvalue of
T_{1}
on
L^{2}(r{\rm d}r)
by Sturm-Liouville theory
[
21
]
, that is the key point to justify the bound
Eq.

3.15
above. By taking the projection onto the
n
-th angular Fourier mode in
Eq.

3.13
, we see that we can define

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}F=\big({\rm Id}-T_{n}\big)^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{n}\phi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T_{n}^{j}(\mathcal{P}_{n}\phi).

With these observations, we have actually proved the following reinterpretation of Proposition
3.4
to invert
\Lambda
on some particular functions.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
h=\{\varphi,G\}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{Z}
with
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{*}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}\varphi=0
. Then, the projections on the
n
-th angular Fourier mode of the unique solution to
\Lambda f=h
can be written as

(3.17)

\mathcal{P}_{n}f=A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}T_{n}^{j}[A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\varphi],\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

3.3.
Properties and expansions of the configurational operators

As explained in Section
2
, we are only interested in computing the operators
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}
in
Z=(R,0)
so we simply write
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R},\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R},\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R},\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}
instead of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)},\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)},\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)},\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,(R,0)}
.

i)
Expansions

We start by introducing the constants

(3.18)

c_{0,k}=(-1)^{k-1},\qquad c_{n,k}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}n\\
k\end{pmatrix}(-1)^{n+k-1}\frac{d^{n}}{2\pi n}\qquad\text{for }n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\},\,k\in\mathbb{N},

and

(3.19)

{\rm S}_{n,k}=\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{l}^{k}}{(Q_{l}-1)^{n}},\qquad Q_{l}\leftrightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi il}{N}},\qquad 0\leq k\leq n.

We provide in Appendix
A.1
details about the values of the constants
{\rm S}_{n,k}
, and we only note that
{\rm S}_{n,k}\in\mathbb{R}
as proved in Lemma
A.1
.
The expansion of the operators is encoded in the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume that
f\in\mathcal{Z}
, let
\varphi=\Delta^{-1}f
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the translation operator admits the expansion

(3.20)

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi(\xi)\,=\,\frac{{\rm M}(f)}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{R}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{R^{n}}c_{n,k}{\rm Re}\Big(\xi^{n-k}{\rm m}^{k}(f)\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M+1}\bigr)\,.

The polygon-center operator admits the expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi(\xi)\,=\,&\mathrm{M}(f)\frac{N}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{R}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+N\sum_{n=1}^{M}(-1)^{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{R^{n}}c_{n,n}{\rm Re}\big({\rm m}^{n}(f)\big)\\
&+N\sum_{n=N}^{M}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\\
\ell N\leq M\end{subarray}}(-1)^{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{R^{n}}c_{n,n-\ell N}{\rm Re}\Big(\xi^{\ell N}\mathrm{m}^{n-\ell N}(f)\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M+1}\bigr)\,.\end{split}

The polygon-polygon operator admits the expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi(\xi)\,=&\,\frac{\mathrm{M}(f)}{2\pi}\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\log\Big(\frac{R}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{R^{n}}c_{n,k}{\rm S}_{n,n-k}{\rm Re}\Big(\xi^{n-k}\mathrm{m}^{k}(f)\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).\end{split}

These expansions are proven in Appendix
A
, by applying Lemma
A.2
in the case
Z=(R,0)
.
Let us now gather some direct consequences of these expansions that will be of use later.

Proposition 3.7
.

For any
\varphi\in\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{Z}
, the gradient of the operators is always at least of order
\varepsilon
:

(3.21)

\nabla Q_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon),\qquad\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon),\qquad\nabla\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\varphi=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon),

meaning in particular that for any
\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}\in\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{Z}
,

(3.22)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),

and thus that for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}
,

(3.23)

\big\{\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}),f\big\}=\{\varphi,f\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon).

Moreover, let
X_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
|X_{\varepsilon}|,|Y_{\varepsilon}|\geq c>0
for a constant
c
independent of
\varepsilon
and
|X_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}|=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M})
for some
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(3.24)

(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,X_{\varepsilon}}-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Y_{\varepsilon}})\varphi=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

The same holds true with
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,\cdot}
replaced by
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,\cdot}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,\cdot}

Proof.

Taking the gradient in the expansions of Lemma
3.6
immediately gives
Eq.

3.21
and thus
Eq.

3.22
. Applying those relations to the definition of
\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}
gives in turn relation
Eq.

3.23
. Instead, the relation
Eq.

3.24
follows by comparing term by term the expansions in Lemma
3.6
, where we gain at least a factor of
\varepsilon|X_{\varepsilon}-Y_{\varepsilon}|
since the constant term vanishes and
|X_{\varepsilon}|,|Y_{\varepsilon}|\geq c>0
.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
3.7
, we have the following.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\varphi_{1},\widehat{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\widehat{\varphi}_{2}\in\Delta^{-1}\mathcal{Z}
and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(3.25)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi_{1}+\varepsilon^{n}\varphi_{2},\widehat{\varphi}_{1}+\varepsilon^{n}\widehat{\varphi}_{2})=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi_{1},\widehat{\varphi}_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{n+1}).

Proof.

It directly follows by the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}
in
Eq.

2.77
and Proposition
3.7
.
∎

ii)

Symmetries, commutators and adjoint

We finally characterize useful properties of our operators.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}
be the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-adjoint of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}
respectively.

Then the following holds.

(1)

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}=\mathcal{T}_{-{\varepsilon,R}}
and
[\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R},\Delta]=[\mathcal{T}^{*}_{\varepsilon,R},\Delta]=0
. Moreover, if
f
is even in
\xi_{2}
then
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}(f)
is also even in
\xi_{2}
.

(2)

The adjoint
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}
is given by

(3.26)

(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}f)(\xi)=\sum_{l=1}^{N}f\big(Q_{l}(\xi+\frac{(R,0)}{\varepsilon d})\big),

and satisfies
[\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R},\Delta]=[\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*},\Delta]=0
.
Moreover, if
f
is
N
-fold symmetric, then

\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}f=N\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}f.

(3)

The operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}
is self-adjoint, namely
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}
, and
[\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R},\Delta]=0.
Moreover, if
f
is even in
\xi_{2}
then
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}(f)
is also even in
\xi_{2}
.

(4)

The following identities holds true

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi|^{2},f\}=\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi|^{2},\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}f\},

(3.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi|^{2},f\}=\{|\xi|^{2},\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}f\},

(3.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon,R}\{|\xi|^{2},f\}=\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi|^{2},\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon,R}f\}.

Remark 3.10
.

The identities in
Eqs.

3.27
,
3.29
and
3.28
reflect the invariance of the vortex polygon with respect to global rotations. In the shifted frame used for the exterior vortices, the function
|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\xi|^{2}
acts as the generating function of rotations around the global origin. Consequently, the first identity states that the polygon-polygon operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}
, describing the interactions between the vortices at the vertices of the polygon, commutes with the generator of global rotations. Analogously, the second and third identities show that the operators
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}
intertwine the rotation of the central vortex with the global rotation of the exterior vortices.

The proof of Lemma
3.9
follows by direct computations, and we thus postpone it to Appendix
A.4
. Finally, we record a key property of the operator
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in
Eq.

2.77
.

Lemma 3.11
.

If
\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}
are even in
\xi_{2}
, then

(3.31)

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}))=0.

In general, for any
\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}_{*}
, denoting
w=\Delta\varphi,\widehat{w}=\Delta\widehat{\varphi}
and
\widetilde{w}=\Delta\widetilde{\varphi}
, we have the expansion

(3.32)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\widetilde{\varphi})=i\sum_{n=1}^{M}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{R}\right)^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)c_{n,k}\left({\rm S}_{n,n-k}\overline{\mathrm{m}^{k}(w)}+\gamma\overline{\mathrm{m}^{k}(\widehat{w})}\right)\overline{\mathrm{m}^{n-k-1}(\widetilde{w})}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

The identity in
Eq.

3.31
is telling us that, with the radius defined as in
Eq.

2.66
, changes on the radius are only due to the odd-in-
\xi_{2}
