part of the solution. In particular, in the approximate solution constructed in the next section, we will show that the even-in-
\xi_{2}
symmetry is lost only through viscous effects, meaning that one is able to effectively gain a factor
\mathcal{O}(\delta)
on the right-hand side of
Eq.

2.66
.

Proof.

From the definition in
Eq.

2.77
, we know that

(3.33)

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))=-\langle{\partial}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}\Psi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\widehat{\Psi}),\Delta\Psi\rangle.

Appealing to Lemma
3.9
, since
\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}
are even in
\xi_{2}
, we know that
{\partial}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R}\Psi)
and
{\partial}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R}\widehat{\Psi})
are odd in
\xi_{2}
whereas
\Delta\Psi
is even in
\xi_{2},
meaning that the integral above is zero.

The expansion in
Eq.

3.32
is a consequence of Lemma
3.6
. In fact, we show in Lemma
A.3
a detailed proof that holds for any position parameter
Z.

∎

4.
The approximate solutions

The objective of this section is to construct an approximate solution
(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})
to the system
Eq.

2.40
–
Eq.

2.41
at any desired order of precision
M
. Our approach follows the systematic asymptotic framework initiated in
[
25
]
and subsequently refined in
[
22
,
12
,
13
,
49
]
. The new aspect of our derivation is the self-consistent coupling of the modulation parameters: we fix the approximate radius and angular velocity to be those defined in
Eqs.

2.66
and
2.67
, evaluated directly at the
M
-th order profiles
(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})
. This technical choice streamlines the construction of higher-order profiles and, more crucially, leads to improved error bounds in the subsequent nonlinear analysis. Since this specific coupling modifies the recursive hierarchy of the expansion, previous results cannot be applied directly as a black box. Consequently, while we follow the general strategy established
[
12
]
, we provide a self-contained construction to clarify the precise impact of the choice of the modulation parameters in the full expansion.

4.1.
Setting up the construction

We look for approximate solutions of the equations in Lemma
2.6
, using the simplified notation in
Eq.

2.82
. At every order
M\geq 0
, we denote the approximate solutions by
\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and we assume the asymptotic expansion

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}(t)\Omega_{k}(\xi),\qquad\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}(t)\widehat{\Omega}_{k}(\xi)

where the families of maps
(\Omega_{k})_{k>0}
and
(\widehat{\Omega}_{k})_{k>0}
must be determined. Recall that
\Omega_{0}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{0}
are already defined at relation
Eq.

2.60
, a choice that ensures in particular that for any
M
,

\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,0)=\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,0)=\Omega_{0}(\xi),

which coincides with the true solution at time 0, see relation
Eq.

2.42
.

We then denote by

(4.2)

\Psi_{k}:=\Delta^{-1}\Omega_{k},\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{k}:=\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}_{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\sum_{k=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}\Psi_{k},\quad\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\sum_{k=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}\widehat{\Psi}_{k},

which, by linearity, satisfy
\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Delta^{-1}\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and
\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
.
It is convenient to expand also in
\delta
as to handle separately viscous effects, since we will be working in the large Reynolds number regime
\delta\ll 1
. We will then expand each approximation at
k
-th order with the Eulerian and Navier-Stokes component as

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}=\Omega_{k,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\Omega_{k,\mathrm{NS}},\qquad\widehat{\Omega}_{k}=\widehat{\Omega}_{k,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\widehat{\Omega}_{k,\mathrm{NS}}

and the same expansion for the associated streamfunctions.
Accordingly, we also split

(4.4)

\displaystyle\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\delta\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,NS}},\qquad\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\delta\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,NS}}.

Then, we fix the radius and angular speed of the rotating frame associated with each approximate solution as the ones given in Lemma
2.7
. Namely, we define
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
through the relation
Eqs.

2.66
and
2.67
as

(4.5)

\displaystyle t(R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}))

(4.6)

\displaystyle t(\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}))

(4.7)

\displaystyle R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(0)=\mathsf{r},\quad\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(0)=0.

This choice of
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
differs from what was done in previous works
[
12
,
49
]
, where the modulation parameters are constructed iteratively with an asymptotic expansion. One then needs to justify
a posteriori
that the formulas
Eqs.

4.5
and
4.6
hold up to small enough errors. We believe it is more intuitive to fix the approximate modulation parameter from the beginning and verify that it is sufficient to proceed with the inductive construction of the approximate solution. From a practical point of view, it also matches better the technical difficulties that we face.

To measure how good of an approximate solution we have constructed, we introduce the remainders

(4.8)

\mathcal{R}_{M}=\delta(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}\},\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=\delta(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\{\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}\},

where
\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
are an abuse of notation for the use of operators
Eq.

2.79
and
Eq.

2.80
applied at the approximate solutions, namely

(4.9)

\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}:=\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}),\qquad\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}:=\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}).

We now state the existence of a suitable choice of approximate solution.

Proposition 4.1
.

There exists two families
(\Omega_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and
(\widehat{\Omega}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of functions belonging to the space
\mathcal{Z}
such that for every
k\geq 0
:

C1)

\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega_{k})=\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\Omega}_{k})=(0,0)
and, for
k>0
,
\mathrm{M}(\Omega_{k})=\mathrm{M}(\widehat{\Omega}_{k})=0
,

C2)

the functions
\Omega_{k,E}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{k,E}
are
even in
\xi_{2}
,

C3)

\widehat{\Omega}_{k}
is
N
-fold symmetric.

Moreover, for every
M\in\mathbb{N}
, the associated approximate solution defined by
Eq.

4.1
satisfies:

C4)

there exists a constant
C
independent of
\delta
such that for every
t\leq C\,T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-1}
,

\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{r}\leq R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathsf{r},

where
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
is given by
Eq.

4.5
,

C5)

the remainders
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
defined by
Eq.

4.8
are of order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\delta^{2}\varepsilon^{2})
and satisfy
\mathrm{m}^{j}(\mathcal{R}_{M})=\mathrm{m}^{j}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M})=(0,0)
for
j=0,1
.

The rest of Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.1
. The constraints
C1)
to
C3)
are technical constraints ensuring that we can manipulate correctly the functions we construct, and that the approximate solution has important properties related to symmetries. The constraint
C4)
is a consequence of the even symmetry of the Eulerian part, and it is necessary to ensure that we can safely expand our operators. The constraint
C5)
is the quantitative control of how close to the approximate solution is the real solution, which is the whole point of the construction.

Remark 4.2
.

In the case
\gamma=0
, the choice of the family
(\widehat{\Omega}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is irrelevant. Therefore, while computing the
\widehat{\Omega}_{k}
, we will assume
\gamma\neq 0
. This assumption does not need to be made to construct the family
(\Omega_{k})_{k\geq 0}
, hence our construction is perfectly valid for
\gamma=0
, even if the expression
1/\gamma
appears in the formula for
\widehat{\Omega}_{k}
.

The construction will be done iteratively, meaning that after having constructed
\Omega_{0},\ldots,\Omega_{M}
, and
\widehat{\Omega}_{0},\ldots,\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
, satisfying constraints
C1)
to
C3)
, and such that the associated approximate solution
\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and
\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
satisfy constraints
C4)
,
C5)
at order
M
, then we construct
\Omega_{M+1}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{M+1}
, satisfying constraints
C1)
to
C3)
such that the next order approximate solution
\Omega^{(M+1)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and
\widehat{\Omega}^{(M+1)}_{\mathrm{app}}
satisfy constraints
C4)
,
C5)
at order
M+1
.

The rest of the present section is organized as follows. In Section
4.2
and
4.3
we present the computations of the first orders, giving both insights and numerically exploitable results on the actual deformation of vortices. Then, in Section
4.4
, we construct iteratively the whole family
(\Omega_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, leading to the construction of an approximate solution at every order. Then we compute the first corrections to the point-vortex dynamics in Section
4.5
. In Sections
4.6
and
4.7
, we establish some important properties of the Eulerian part of the approximate solution such as the existence of an approximate functional relationship and remarkable relations for its angular momentum.

4.2.
The zero-th and first order

We recall that the choice of the zero-th order is dictated by the initial data, which is the Lamb-Oseen vortex

(4.10)

\Omega_{0}=\widehat{\Omega}_{0}=G,

Therefore,
\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=G
. Since
\mathrm{m}^{1}(G)=0
, we have that
\Omega_{0}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{0}
satisfy constraints
C1)
to
C3)
. Moreover, since
G
is radially symmetric,
\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0}
are radially symmetric as well and thus even in
\xi_{2}
. By Lemma
3.11
, we readily deduce that
t(R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}=0
and therefore

(4.11)

R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=\mathsf{r}.

With those expressions in hand, we can compute and expand the zero-th order remainders.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any
M\geq 2
we have
\mathcal{R}_{0},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2})
with explicit expansion

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=2}^{M}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{n}}({\rm S}_{n,n}+\gamma)\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{n}),G\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}

\displaystyle=N\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq M/N,\\
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\frac{\varepsilon^{\ell N}c_{\ell N,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{\ell N}}\big\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{\ell N}),G\big\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

Proof.

We start by observing that since
G
is a self-similar solution of the heat equation, we have
(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})(G)=0
. Thus, for
\mathcal{R}_{0}
we see that

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}=\{\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{0}\}=\Big\{\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\Psi_{0}+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}_{0}-\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0}))|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{0}\Big\}.

Since
\mathrm{m}^{k}(G)=0
for any
k\geq 1
, and
R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\mathsf{r}
,
applying
Lemma

3.6
, we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\Psi_{0},\Omega_{0}\}=\frac{\varepsilon c_{1,0}}{\mathsf{r}}\mathrm{S}_{1,1}\{{\rm Re}(\xi),G\}+\sum_{n=2}^{M}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{n}}{\rm S}_{n,n}\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{n}),G\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}),

and

(4.16)

\displaystyle\gamma\{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}_{0},\Omega_{0}\}=\gamma\frac{\varepsilon c_{1,0}}{\mathsf{r}}\{{\rm Re}(\xi),G\}+\gamma\sum_{n=2}^{M}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{n}}\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{n}),G\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

We now turn to the term involving
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}
. Using Lemma
3.6
combined with the fact that
\mathrm{m}^{k}(G)=0
for every
k>0
, we get that

(4.17)

\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0}))=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}d}{2\mathsf{r}^{2}}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma)+o(\varepsilon^{\infty}),

and since

(4.18)

\displaystyle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{0}\}=\{|\xi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon d}(\mathsf{r},0)|^{2},G\}=\frac{2\mathsf{r}}{\varepsilon d}\{\xi_{1},G\}=\frac{2\mathsf{r}}{\varepsilon d}\{{\rm Re}(\xi),G\},

we obtain that

(4.19)

\mathcal{R}_{0}=\sum_{n=2}^{M}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{n}}({\rm S}_{n,n}+\gamma)\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{n}),G\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}),

where the term of order
\varepsilon
has canceled out.

Turning our attention to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
, the formula
Eq.

4.13
directly follows by recalling the definition
Eq.

2.80
, using
Lemma

3.6
and the radial symmetry of
G
.
∎

Since,
\mathcal{R}_{0},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, one can choose, as in
[
25
,
12
,
13
,
49
]
, the first order to vanish exactly:

(4.20)

\Omega_{1}=\widehat{\Omega}_{1}=0,

leading in particular to
\Omega^{(1)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Omega_{0}
. Indeed, this choice of
\Omega_{1}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{1}
satisfies constraints
C1)
to
C3)
. In addition, it leads to identical formulas for the remainders:
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{0}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{1}=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
, so by Lemma
4.3
, constraints
C4)
,
C5)
are satisfied at order
M=1
, which concludes the construction of the first order.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that if
\gamma=-{\rm S}_{2,2}
then by Lemma
4.3
,
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})
, meaning that, as we did for the first order, one can take
\Omega_{2}=0
and satisfy all the constraints at order
2
. This explains the fact observed in Section
1.3
that for the special value
\gamma_{N}^{*}=-S_{2,2}=\frac{(N-1)(N-5)}{12}
, the exterior vortices do not show any
2
-fold symmetric deformation. By the same argument, if
N>2
, for any value of
\gamma
, the choice
\widehat{\Omega}_{k}=0
for
k=2,\dots,N-1
satisfies all constraints since in that case
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{N-1}=\ldots=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{N})
.

4.3.
Construction of the second order

As a propaedeutical way to understanding the iterative scheme, and in order to get the first non-trivial correction explicitly, we now give in detail the construction of the second order. The procedure is clearly analogous to the one first proposed in
[
25
]
and extended in
[
12
,
49
]
. However, here we first need to guarantee some
a priori
properties of the approximate position in
Eq.

4.5
. We begin by computing an expression of
R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume that
\Omega_{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
satisfy constraints
C1)
to
C3)
. Let
T
be the first time such that
C4)
fails. Then, for every
t\in[0,T]
we have that,

(4.21)

t(R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}(t)),\qquad R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\mathsf{r}(1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}(t))),\qquad\delta t(\alpha^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\alpha_{0})^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}(t)).

Proof.

We start by decomposing
\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\,\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
, and the approximate solution as

(4.22)

\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\underbrace{\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}}_{\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta\,\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}

and similary,
\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\delta\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
.
Recalling that
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}
is actually a map in three variables (see its full definition
Eq.

2.77
), linear with respect to the third variable, but affine with respect to the other two, we compute that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})

(4.24)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{2}\delta\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{2,\mathrm{NS}},\widehat{\Psi}_{2,\mathrm{NS}},\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})+\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\Psi_{2,\mathrm{NS}})\Big)

(4.25)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\delta^{2}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{2,{\rm NS}},\Psi_{2,{\rm NS}}).

Using
Eq.

3.31
in Lemma
3.11
, by the
\xi_{2}
symmetry of the purely Eulerian part we get that

(4.26)

{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}))=0.

For the remaining terms we use
Eq.

3.32
and we observe the following: since
\mathrm{m}^{k}(\Omega_{2,NS})=(0,0)
for
k\geq 0
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})=(0,0),
the associated sum in
Eq.

3.32
starts from
n=3
. Thus, applying that expansion with
M=3
, we see that

(4.27)

{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}))=\mathcal{O}(\delta\varepsilon^{5})

from which we infer

(4.28)

t(R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{d\varepsilon}{\delta}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}))=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}).

Integrating over time, it yields that

(4.29)

R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=\mathsf{r}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}).

To control the angular velocity, we do not have the same cancellations for the Eulerian part, but we observe that

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})

(4.31)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{2,\mathrm{E}},\widehat{\Psi}_{2,\mathrm{E}},\Psi_{0})+\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0},\Psi_{2,\mathrm{E}})\Big)

(4.32)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{2,{\rm E}},\Psi_{2,{\rm E}}).

Thus, by using again
Eq.

3.32
in Lemma
3.11
, we observe that the first term is of order
\varepsilon
, whereas for the other ones we again start from the order
n=3
, meaning that

(4.33)

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})=i\frac{\varepsilon}{R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{5}).

Thus, using the information on
R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}
, we infer

(4.34)

\delta t(\alpha^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{d\varepsilon}{R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}))=\delta t\alpha_{0}^{\prime}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}).

∎

Please note that the fact that we cannot recover a
\delta
factor in
(\alpha^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}
means that the difference
|\alpha^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\alpha_{0}|
may become of order 1 after a time
t\ll T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-1}
. The explicit computation of this difference is delayed in Section
4.5
, since it is not relevant to the construction of the approximate solution. We now infer from Lemma
4.5
that constraint
C4)
is satisfied at the second order regardless of the choice of
\Omega_{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
as long as it satisfies the first three constraints.

Corollary 4.6
.

Any
\Omega_{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
satisfying constraints
C1)
to
C3)
immediately satisfy constraint
C4)
.

Proof.

By continuity of
R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}
, Lemma
4.5
implies that there exists a constant
C
independent of
\delta
such that

(4.35)

\frac{R}{2}=|R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}(T)-R|\leq C\varepsilon^{6}(T)=C(\nu T)^{3}

hence

(4.36)

T\geq\frac{1}{\Gamma\delta}\left(\frac{R}{2C}\right)^{1/3}.

So there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
independent of
\delta
such that for every
t\leq C^{\prime}\delta^{-1}
,

(4.37)

\frac{1}{2}R\leq R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)\leq\frac{3}{2}R.

∎

Let us compute
\mathcal{R}_{2}
in terms of
\Omega_{2}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
to understand how constraint
C5)
will dictate the construction. Many terms are involved, and the important thing is to isolate the main contributions as follows.

Lemma 4.7
.

We have that

(4.38)

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2}+\Lambda\Omega_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}),

where

(4.39)

\mathcal{H}_{2}:=\frac{c_{2,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}}(S_{2,2}+\gamma)\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{2}),G\}

is the error at order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2})
coming from
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Similarly,

(4.40)

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\widehat{\Omega}_{2}+\gamma\Lambda\widehat{\Omega}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}),

where

(4.41)

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2}:=\begin{cases}2\frac{c_{2,0}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}}\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{2}),G\}&\text{ if }N=2\\
0&\text{ if }N>2,\end{cases}

is the error at order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2})
coming from
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
.

Proof.

We compute the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{2}
associated with the second-order approximate function given by the expression
\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}(\xi,t)=\Omega_{0}(\xi)+\varepsilon^{2}(t)\Omega_{2}(\xi)
:

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}=\delta(t\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L})\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\{\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}.

We look at the first term. Recalling that
t\partial_{t}\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}
, we get that

(4.43)

\delta(t\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L})(\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}\Omega_{2})=\varepsilon^{2}\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2}.

For the second term, we isolate the terms already computed (the ones present in the construction of the previous order) and two different kinds of errors:

(4.44)

\displaystyle\{\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}=\{\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}+\varepsilon^{2}\{\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{2}\}+\{\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}.

We now observe that

(4.45)

\{\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}=\mathcal{R}_{0}=\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})

as obtained in Section
4.2
, then, using Proposition
3.7
we obtain that

(4.46)

\varepsilon^{2}\{\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{2}\}=\varepsilon^{2}\{\Psi_{0},\Omega_{2}\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}).

The last term involves
\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}
, which we recall that properly written is

(4.47)

\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}:=\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})-\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}).

Not only are those maps applied in different functions, but they are also computed at different approximate positions. Beware that
\Phi
is not a linear operator (but an affine one), and the following identity holds:

(4.48)

\displaystyle\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\Big[(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}})\Psi_{2}+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}_{2}\Big]

(4.49)

\displaystyle+\xi_{2}{\rm Re}\big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})-\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})\big)

(4.50)

\displaystyle-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi\big|^{2}{\rm Im}\big(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}})-\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})\big)

(4.51)

\displaystyle+(\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}-\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}})(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0}).

From Proposition
3.7
we have that

(4.52)

\varepsilon^{2}\big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}})\Psi_{2}+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}_{2}\,,\,\Omega^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}\big\}=\varepsilon^{2}\{\Psi_{2},\Omega_{0}\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}).

Then, we compute the last term coming from changing the approximate position:

(4.53)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}-\Phi_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}})(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})=(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}-\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\big)\Psi_{0}+\gamma(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}-\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}})\widehat{\Psi}_{0}

(4.54)

\displaystyle\qquad+\xi_{2}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})-\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0}))

(4.55)

\displaystyle\qquad-{\rm Im}\Big(\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi\big|^{2}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi\big|^{2}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi_{0},\widehat{\Psi}_{0})\Big).

Combining Lemma
4.5
and Proposition
3.7
, we get that all the terms involved in the last expression are of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})
at least. Moreover, for the terms in
Eqs.

4.49
and
4.50
we can apply Corollary
3.8
and see that indeed

(4.56)

\Phi^{(2)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\varepsilon^{2}\Psi_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{3}).

Therefore, we conclude that

(4.57)

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2}+\{\Psi_{0},\Omega_{2}\}+\{\Psi_{2},\Omega_{0}\}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}),

which gives the desired result by recalling that
\Lambda\Omega=\{\Psi_{0},\Omega\}+\{\Delta^{-1}\Omega,\Omega_{0}\}
.

Similar computations, left to the reader, yield the result regarding
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
.
∎

With Lemma
4.7
, we have a very natural (and, remarkably, somewhat
unique
) choice of
\Omega_{2}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
satisfying constraint
C5)
. This choice is made so that all
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
terms in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
vanish. We can now formulate the construction of the approximate solution up to the second order.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
be defined by the following formulas:

(4.58)

\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{2},\qquad\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}.

Similarly, let
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}=\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
be defined by

(4.59)

\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{E}}:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Lambda^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2},\qquad\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{E}}:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Lambda^{-1}(1-\mathcal{L})\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{E}}.

Then,
\Omega_{2}
,
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
and the associated second order approximate solution satisfy all constraints
C1)
to
C5)
.

Remark 4.9
.

Note that the dependence on
N
in the definition of
\Omega_{2}
is only through the constant
c_{2,0}({\rm S}_{2,2}+\gamma)
. As observed in Remark
1.4
, we know that
d/\mathsf{r}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}),
and therefore, by
Eq.

3.18
, we have
c_{2,0}/\mathsf{r}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})
. Moreover
{\rm S}_{2,2}=\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
, meaning that the
N
-dependent constant hidden in the definition of
\Omega_{2}
is indeed of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
as announced in Remark
1.4
.

Proof.

As explained in Remark
4.4
, if
\gamma=-S_{2,2}
then setting
\Omega_{2}=0
satisfies all the constraints, and so does setting
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}=0
in the case
N\geq 3
.

Let
h(\xi):=\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{2}),G\}(\xi)=-\xi_{1}\xi_{2}G(\xi)
. Since
h
satisfies that

•

h\in\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{Z}
,

•

h
is odd in
\xi_{2}
,

•

h
is also two-fold symmetric (hence
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
is always
N
-fold symmetric)

•

M(h)=0
and
m^{1}(h)=(0,0)
,

then, using the properties of
\Lambda
gathered in Proposition
3.4
, we have that if
\gamma\neq-S_{2,2}
, then
\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}
is well defined, and if
N=2
, then
\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{E}}
is well defined (else, they are well defined by taking them vanishing everywhere). Moreover, we also get that so are
\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{NS}}
, and that
\Omega_{2}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
satisfy constraints
C1)
to
C3)
. With these definitions, we compute using Lemma
4.7
that

(4.60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2}+\Lambda\Omega_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})

(4.61)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\delta(1-\mathcal{L})(\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}})+\Lambda\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})

(4.62)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}(1-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})

(4.63)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}).

Similarly, one can compute that
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2})
, and thus constraint
C5)
is now fully satisfied at order
M=2
. Note that these estimates still hold if one (or both) of
\Omega_{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
is vanishing.

This concludes the proof and therefore the construction of the approximate solution at second order.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Observe that, up to an error of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2})
, Lemma
4.7
ensures the existence of a unique choice of
\Omega_{2}
satisfying constraint
C5)
. Furthermore, by defining
\Omega_{2}
as a higher order polynomial in
\delta
, namely

(4.64)

\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2,\mathrm{E}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\delta^{k}\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS},k},

for some
K\geq 1
, one can uniquely choose
\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS},1},\ldots,\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS},K}
such that
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{K+1})
. Indeed, to define the approximate solution, one could write
(\Lambda+\delta(1-\mathcal{L}))^{-1}=\Lambda^{-1}(\mathrm{Id}+\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Lambda^{-1})^{-1}
and then use a truncation of the Neumann series associated with the operator
(\mathrm{Id}+\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Lambda^{-1})^{-1}
, which essentially corresponds to our approach. However, in our functional setting, rigorously inverting
\mathrm{Id}+\delta(1-\mathcal{L})\Lambda^{-1}
presents significant difficulties due to an accumulation of losses in powers of
|\xi|
as
|\xi|\to\infty.

Finally, a few other remarks on the construction of the third-order approximate solution can be found in Section
4.5
. However, instead of performing the complete computations of the third order, we now provide the general procedure to construct every order inductively.

4.4.
The inductive construction

Recall from Section
3
that
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is the orthogonal projection in
\mathcal{Y}
onto
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
(the average in the angular direction).
We now state how to construct iteratively the approximate solutions at any order.

Proposition 4.11
.

Assume that for some
M\geq 1
, all the maps
\Omega_{0},\ldots,\Omega_{M-1}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{0},\ldots,\widehat{\Omega}_{M-1}
are constructed satisfying constraints
C1)
to
C5)
. Then by hypothesis, there exists
\mathcal{H}_{M}=\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}
such that

(4.65)

\mathcal{R}_{M-1}(\xi,t)=\varepsilon^{M}(t)\mathcal{H}_{M}(\xi)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}(t)+\delta^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(t)),

and
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M}=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\delta\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}
such that

(4.66)

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M-1}(t,\xi)=\varepsilon^{M}(t)\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M}(\xi)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}(t)+\delta^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(t)).

Let
\Omega_{M}=\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}+\delta\Omega_{M,\mathrm{NS}}
be defined by

(4.67)

\displaystyle\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}}

(4.68)

\displaystyle\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}:=\left(\mathcal{L}-\frac{M}{2}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}),

(4.69)

\displaystyle\Omega_{M,\mathrm{NS}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\left((1-\mathcal{P}_{0})\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}+\left(\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}\right).

Similarly, let
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}=\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}+\delta\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}
with

(4.70)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{E}}:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Lambda^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}

(4.71)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}:=-\left(\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}),

(4.72)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\left((1-\mathcal{P}_{0})\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}+\left(\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)\widehat{\Omega}_{M,\mathrm{E}}\right).

Then,
\Omega_{M}
,
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
and the associated approximate solution at order
M
satisfy constraints
C1)
to
C5)
.

The rest of Section
4.4
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.11
and to the proof that it implies Proposition
4.1
. It follows the same plan as the construction of the second order, except for one major difference which is the need to define a new Eulerian part
\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}
which is radially symmetric, since
\mathcal{H}_{M}
does not necessarily lie within
(\mathrm{Ker}\Lambda)^{\perp}
, a problem that only appears from the fourth order approximation onward.

Let us now begin with the proof of Proposition
4.11
. By definition of
\mathcal{R}_{M-1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M-1}

Eq.

4.8
, since all the involved operators and maps can be expanded as series in
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, then
\mathcal{R}_{M-1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M-1}
can themselves be expanded as series in
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. Therefore, we have indeed the existence of
\mathcal{H}_{M-1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M-1}
as stated is Proposition
4.11
.

Now let us prove that
\Omega_{M}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
given in Proposition
4.11
are well defined and satisfy constraints
C1)
to
C3)
.

Lemma 4.12
.

We have that
M(\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=M(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=0
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=0
.

Proof.

By definition,

(4.73)

\mathrm{m}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{M-1})=\delta\mathrm{m}^{1}\big((t\partial_{t}-\mathcal{L})\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\big)+\mathrm{m}^{1}\big(\{\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}\big).

By hypothesis
\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}
satisfies constraint
C1)
, hence
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}})=(0,0)
. Then, by the definition
Eq.

4.9
of the operator
\Phi
and the choice of
R^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\alpha^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}
, it is not hard to check that

(4.74)

\mathrm{m}^{1}\big(\{\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}\big)=(0,0).

Note that this identity is exactly what we used to define
R(t),\alpha(t)
in Lemma
2.7
. Thus, we conclude that

(4.75)

\mathrm{m}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{M-1})=(0,0)

By the separation of orders in
\varepsilon,\delta
, the statement of the lemma follows by the identity above. ∎

Now, we check that the Eulerian part of the remainders are indeed in the kernel of
\Lambda.

Lemma 4.13
.

We have that
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}},\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}\in(\mathrm{Ker}\Lambda)^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{Z}
and are odd in
\xi_{2}
.

Proof.

From the definition of the operators
\Phi,\widehat{\Phi}
in
Eqs.

2.79
and
2.80
and Lemma
3.9
, we know that the only term that does not respect the even symmetry in
\xi_{2}
is the one involving
{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}))
. However, this term vanishishes on the purely Eulerian part of the approximate solution thanks to
Eq.

3.31
. Then, when we set
\delta=0
to define
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}},\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}
we see that we need to only look at
\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Phi}^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}
in
Eq.

4.9
when computed on
\Psi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
, and then take the Poisson bracket with
\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
. Since by the constraint
Item

C2)
the Eulerian part is even in
\xi_{2}
, we get that
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}},\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}
are odd in
\xi_{2}
. This automatically tells us that
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=\mathcal{P}_{0}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}})=0
and we can conclude that
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}},\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{M,\mathrm{E}}\in(\mathrm{Ker}\Lambda)^{\perp}
by using Lemma
4.12
. The fact that they are in
\mathcal{Z}
is a trivial consequence of properties of the approximate solution. ∎

By combining Lemmas
4.12
and
4.13
with the definitions of
\Omega_{M}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
, the properties of the operators
\Lambda
and
\mathcal{L}
in Proposition
3.4
and Lemma
3.3
, we conclude that
\Omega_{M}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
satisfy constraint
C1)
to
C3)
. We now compute the remainder.

Lemma 4.14
.

With this choice of
\Omega_{M+1}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{M+1}
one has that

(4.76)

\mathcal{R}_{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}),\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}).

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{M}
, we have

(4.77)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{M}

\displaystyle=\delta(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}

(4.78)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{M-1}+\delta(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})(\varepsilon^{M}\Omega_{M})+\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}-\{\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}

(4.79)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{M-1}+\varepsilon^{M}\Big(\delta(t{\partial}_{t}+\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{M}+\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{M}\}\Big)+\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}.

We now compute term by term, only at the required precision. By hypothesis,

(4.80)

\mathcal{R}_{M-1}=\varepsilon^{M}\mathcal{H}_{M}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}).

Using Proposition
3.7
, we have that

(4.81)

\varepsilon^{M}\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{M}\}=\varepsilon^{M}\{\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega_{M}\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1})=\varepsilon^{M}\{\Psi_{0},\Omega_{M}\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

Then, using again Proposition
3.7
, and Lemma
4.5
, proceeding as done for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
(see the proof of Lemma
4.7
), we deduce that

(4.82)

\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\Phi^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}},\Omega^{(M-1)}_{\mathrm{app}}\}=\varepsilon^{M}\{\Psi_{M},\Omega_{0}\}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

Gathering all the previous relations leads to

(4.83)

\mathcal{R}_{M}=\varepsilon^{M}\Big(\mathcal{H}_{M}+\delta\Big(t\partial_{t}+\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\Big)\Omega_{M}+\Lambda\Omega_{M}\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}),

which is the expression that allows us to determine that the choice made in the statement of Proposition
4.11
is correct, or if one is searching for such a choice, allows one to find it. Remarkably, this equation does not depend on
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
, meaning that we can choose
\Omega_{M}
independently of the choice of
\widehat{\Omega}_{M}
. The main Eulerian part is killed by the choice of
\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}
as

(4.84)

\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\Lambda(\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}})=0.

Then, since

\varepsilon^{M}\delta^{2}(t\partial_{t}+\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L})\Omega_{M,\mathrm{NS}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{2}),

and since
\partial_{t}\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}=0
, we only need to check that

(4.85)

\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}+\left(\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)(\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}}+\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}})+\Lambda\Omega_{M,\mathrm{NS}}=0.

By construction, we have that

(4.86)

\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}+\left(\frac{M}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)\Omega_{M,\mathrm{E}_{r}}=(1-\mathcal{P}_{0})\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathrm{NS}}

and thus relation
Eq.

4.85
holds true.
∎

Conclusion of the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Proof.

We have constructed the
M=0
order functions
\Omega_{0},\widehat{\Omega}_{0}
(and actually up to the
M=2
order), so there only remains to apply Proposition
4.11
iteratively.
∎

4.5.
First non trivial corrections

Although it is not necessary to compute the third order
\Omega_{3},\widehat{\Omega}_{3}
maps, as mentioned in Section
1.3
, the third order can be the leading order correction; hence, let us give it explicitly.

Mostly due to the fact that the zero-th and first order are trivial, the computation of the third order is actually very similar to the computations of the second order, although estimating all the error terms is slightly more complicated. One can also directly apply Proposition
4.1
to obtain the expression of the third-order term
\Omega_{3}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{3}
, but to do so, one has to compute the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})
term in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
. Going back to the computation of
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and making finer use of the properties of
\Omega_{2}
and the operators
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,\cdot}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,\cdot}
one can show that
\mathcal{R}_{2}
contains no other term of order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{3})
than the term coming from
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, namely

(4.87)

\mathcal{H}_{3}:=\frac{c_{3,0}}{R^{3}}({\rm S}_{3,3}+\gamma)\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{3}),G\},\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}:=\begin{cases}3\frac{c_{3,0}}{R^{3}}\{{\rm Re}(\xi^{3}),G\}&\text{ if }N=3\\
0&\text{ if }N\neq 3.\end{cases}

Therefore, Proposition
4.1
gives that

(4.88)

\Omega_{3,\mathrm{E}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{3},\qquad\widehat{\Omega}_{3,\mathrm{E}}:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Lambda^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}

and

(4.89)

\Omega_{3,\mathrm{NS}}:=-\Lambda^{-1}\left(\frac{3}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)\Omega_{3,\mathrm{E}},\qquad\widehat{\Omega}_{3,\mathrm{E}}:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Lambda^{-1}\left(\frac{3}{2}-\mathcal{L}\right)\widehat{\Omega}_{3,\mathrm{E}}.

Things get more complicated at the fourth order, as new non trivial error terms at order
\varepsilon^{4}
come from the nonlinear interactions of the second order term with itself.

One can now wonder what is the first non trivial term for the associated approximate radius
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and angular velocity
(\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}
of the rotating frame.

Proposition 4.15
.

There exists
r_{6}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha_{4}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that for any
M\geq 5
, and any
t\leq C\delta^{-1}T_{\mathsf{adv}}
,

(4.90)

R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=\mathsf{r}(1+r_{6}\varepsilon^{6}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}(t)))

and

(4.91)

(\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\mathsf{a}\big(1+\alpha_{4}\varepsilon^{4}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{5}(t)+\delta\varepsilon^{4}(t))\big),

where we recall that
\mathsf{a}=\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi\mathsf{r}^{2}}(N-1+2\gamma)
is the angular velocity of the point-vortex polygonal vortex crystal configuration defined in
Eq.

1.6
. The constant
r_{6}
and
\alpha_{4}
are given by:

(4.92)

r_{6}=-\frac{d}{3\mathsf{r}^{4}}\Big(3c_{3,0}\big({\rm S}_{3,3}+\gamma\big)\mathrm{m}_{2}^{2}(\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}})+c_{3,2}\big({\rm S}_{3,1}\mathrm{m}_{2}^{2}(\Omega_{2,\mathrm{NS}})+\gamma\mathrm{m}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}_{2,\mathrm{NS}})\big)\Big)

and

(4.93)

\alpha_{4}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}^{2}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma)}\Big(3c_{3,0}({\rm S}_{3,3}+\gamma)\mathrm{m}_{1}^{2}(\Omega_{2})+c_{3,2}({\rm S}_{3,1}\mathrm{m}_{1}^{2}(\Omega_{2})+\gamma\mathrm{m}_{1}^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}_{2})\big)\Big).

Proof.

Plugging Lemma
B.1
into the definition
Eq.

4.5
of
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
, we have that

(4.94)

t(R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=3\mathsf{r}^{4}\frac{r_{6}\varepsilon^{6}}{(R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}).

Denoting by
T
the maximal time for which
|R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)-r|\leq\varepsilon
, we have that for
t\leq T
,

(4.95)

t(R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=3r_{6}\varepsilon^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}).

Recalling that
\varepsilon^{2}(t)=\frac{\nu t}{d^{2}}
, by dividing by
t
and integrating,

(4.96)

(R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})(t)=\mathsf{r}(1+r_{6}\varepsilon^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7})).

The bootstrap assumption is therefore satisfied for as long as
\varepsilon
is small enough, namely for
t\leq c\delta^{-1}T_{\mathsf{adv}}
for some small constant
c
.
Plugging this into Lemma
B.1
and the definition
Eq.

4.6
of
\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
gives that

(4.97)

\delta t(\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=d\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma)\Big(1+\alpha_{4}\varepsilon^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}+\delta\varepsilon^{6})\Big).

Recalling that
\varepsilon^{2}=\delta\frac{t}{T_{\mathsf{adv}}}
, we conclude that

(4.98)

(\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}})^{\prime}(t)=\mathsf{a}(1+\alpha_{4}\varepsilon^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{5}+\delta\varepsilon^{4}))

by noticing that
\mathsf{a}=\frac{\Gamma}{4\pi\mathsf{r}^{2}}(N-1+2\gamma)=\frac{d}{T_{\mathsf{adv}}\mathsf{r}^{2}}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma).

∎

Remark 4.16
.

Please note that, by integrating in time the formula in Proposition
4.15
we get that

|\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)-\mathsf{a}t|\lesssim\mathsf{a}t\varepsilon^{4}(t)
. In particular, defining

(4.99)

\zeta^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app},j}(t)=R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)\Big(\cos\Big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)\Big),\sin\Big(\frac{2\pi j}{N}+\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)\Big)\Big),

we have that

(4.100)

\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}|\zeta^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app},j}(t)-z_{j}(t)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}|R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)-\mathsf{r}|+|\alpha^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}(t)-\mathsf{a}t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{6}(t)+\mathsf{a}t\varepsilon^{4}(t).

Then, since
\varepsilon^{6}(t)\ll\mathsf{a}t\varepsilon^{4}(t)
whenever
\nu/d^{2}\ll\Gamma/\mathsf{r}^{2}
, for
\delta
sufficiently small we arrive at

(4.101)

\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}|\zeta^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app},j}(t)-z_{j}(t)|=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{t}{T_{\rm adv}}\varepsilon^{4}(t)\big),

where we also used that
\mathsf{a}
is proportional to
1/T_{\rm adv}
.
The error above is of order
\mathcal{O}(1)
as soon as
t
becomes of order
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-2/3})
. This means that although our solution remains a sum of concentrated vortices for a time of order
T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma}
, its angular position is completely out of phase with respect to the point vortex dynamics after a time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-2/3})
.

Moreover, the constant
\alpha_{4}
can be easily computed numerically
6
6

6

Besides the definition of the constants, we only need to compute numerically
\mathrm{m}^{2}_{1}(\Omega_{2,\rm E})
as in Proposition
4.8
. To define
\Omega_{2,\rm E}=-\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{2}
we make use of the considerations leading to Proposition
3.5
. Namely, looking at
\mathcal{H}_{2}
in Lemma
4.7
, it is enough to solve
(A+\Delta^{-1})f_{2}={\rm Re}(\xi^{2})
. To do so, we discretize the integral operator
T_{2}
in
Eq.

3.14
and cut the integral in its definition, invert the matrix associated to
{\rm Id}-T_{2}
and obtain an approximation for
f_{2}
.
. When
N=2
and
\gamma=0
, we observe that
\alpha_{4}\approx 8.7362
which is in agreement with the corresponding correction to the vertical speed for a dipole computed in
[
26
]
(and in agreement as well with the numerical value found in
[
12
]
). This is not surprising because the second order correction for the dipole and two equal co-rotating vortices are exactly the same except for a sign, meaning that the dipole slows down whereas the likely signed vortex pair
increase
their angular speed. In the case
N=6
,
\gamma=10
(and
\gamma=-10
) in Figure
2
we get that
\alpha_{4}\approx 513.496
(and
\alpha_{4}=-1011.1373
).

For the special value of
\gamma=\gamma_{N}^{*}
in
Eq.

1.20
and
N>2
, we know that
\Omega_{2}=\widehat{\Omega}_{2}=0
and therefore the correction to the Helmholtz-Kirchhoff angular speed starts at order
\varepsilon^{5}
, meaning that
\alpha_{4}=0
and the next coefficient
\alpha_{5}
can be explicitly defined with the formulas provided in Lemma
B.1
.

Finally, when
N\to\infty
, we know that
c_{n,k}=\mathcal{O}(L_{N}^{n})=\mathcal{O}(N^{-n})
whereas
{\rm S}_{3,j}=\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
and
{\rm S}_{1,1}=\mathcal{O}(N)
, meaning that
\alpha_{4}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
in view of Remark
4.9
. For general
N\geq 3
and
\gamma=0
, see Figure
3
.

d
in

and

computed for
N=3,\dots,10
(left) and plot of
\alpha_{4}
as a function of

(right). For

, we have
\gamma_{5}^{*}=0
and note the behavior
\alpha_{4}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
for large values of

.

Finally, let us conclude this section with the following remark on the direction of deformation.

Remark 4.17
.

As observed in Figure
2
, we expect that the direction of deformation changes depending on the sign of
\gamma-\gamma_{N}^{*}=\gamma+S_{2,2}
. Gathering the expressions of
\Omega_{2}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
obtained in Section
4.3
, one can compute (similarly to what was done in
[
25
,
12
,
13
]
) that there exists a map
w:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(4.102)

\Omega_{2}(\xi)=\frac{c_{2,0}}{R^{2}}(S_{2,2}+\gamma)w(r)\cos(2\theta),

where
(r,\theta)
denotes the polar coordinates of
\xi
. With that expression, we see that
\Omega_{2}
changes sign with the quantity
\gamma-\gamma_{N}^{*}
and that it results in
\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}\Omega_{2}
changing its direction of deformation with the sign of
\Omega_{2}
(since it is a
\pi
-periodic map in
\theta
).

4.6.
The functional relationship

A key point to justify the validity of the approximate solution, is the construction of an approximate functional relationship between vorticity and streamfunction for the Eulerian part of the solution, arising when setting
\delta=0
in the expansion done in the previous sections. In particular, this will be a crucial property that allow us to apply the Arnold’s energy method in the nonlinear problem. In order for that Eulerian part of the solution to make sense in itself, we consider in this section
\varepsilon
to be a parameter independent of
\delta
, and to define
\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
and
\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
through formulas
Eq.

4.1
as a series in this parameter
\varepsilon
.

First of all, by construction (constraint
C5)
), we know that

(4.103)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Omega_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{2}\Omega_{k,E}

(4.104)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Omega_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{2}\widehat{\Omega}_{k,E}.

Then, we recall that by
Eqs.

2.79
and
2.80
that

(4.105)

\displaystyle\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\,

\displaystyle(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}})\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\xi_{2}{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}))

(4.106)

\displaystyle-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}))|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi|^{2}

(4.107)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}=\,

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}-\frac{d\varepsilon}{2R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}))|\xi|^{2}.

In the expression above, we now keep only the Eulerian part of the approximate solution but we keep the radius
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
fixed. By Lemma
3.11
and the constraint
Item

C2)
, note that

(4.108)

{\rm Re}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}))=0.

Hence, by also shifting the streamfunctions by a constant (that will not be seen in the Poisson brackets), we define

(4.109)

\displaystyle\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}})\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}

(4.110)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}))|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\xi|^{2}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon)

(4.111)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\gamma\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}-\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}}(\Psi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Psi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}))|\xi|^{2}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon).

Then, by following the same proof of Proposition
4.11
with
\delta
set to
0
in the definition of the errors
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
, it is not hard to show inductively that

(4.112)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{M,{\rm E}}\coloneqq\{\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}\}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}),\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M,{\rm E}}\coloneqq\{\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}\}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

Hence, from the identities above we are very close to being an exact solution of the Euler equation in a moving frame, for which one can expect that there is (at least locally) a functional relationship between
\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
and
\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
.

Remark 4.18
.

Once the even symmetry in
\xi_{2}
is guaranteed, the modulation parameter
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
is actually “free”, in the sense that
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{M,{\rm E}})=0
being
\mathcal{R}_{M,{\rm E}}
odd in
\xi_{2}
. Thus,
R^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app}}
enters in the construction of the Eulerian part only as a multiplicative constant. This reflects a scaling symmetry of the Eulerian configuration, where a change in the radius corresponds to a scaling of the angular speed.

Unfortunately, it is not possible to justify an exact functional relationship up to errors like
\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=-F(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{M+1})
. Nonetheless, in
[
12
]
the authors introduced a way of defining an approximate functional relationship that suffices for the scope of the result. The key idea is to define everything as a Taylor expansion around the Lamb-Oseen vortex
\Omega_{0}
. Indeed, at leading order
M=0
we know that
\Omega^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\widehat{\Omega}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Omega_{0}
and
\Phi^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\gamma^{-1}\widehat{\Phi}^{(0)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Psi_{0}
where
\Omega_{0},\Psi_{0}
are given in
Eq.

2.60
,
Eq.

2.61
respectively. Moreover, by the definition in
Eq.

2.60
and
Eq.

2.61
we also deduce that

(4.113)

\Psi_{0}+F_{0}(\Omega_{0})=0,\qquad\gamma\Psi_{0}+\widehat{F}_{0}(\Omega_{0})=0

with

(4.114)

F_{0}(s)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\Big(\gamma_{E}-{\rm Ein}(\log\big(\frac{1}{4\pi s}\big)\Big),\quad\widehat{F}_{0}(s)=\gamma F_{0}(s),\qquad 0<s\leq\frac{1}{4\pi},

Note that
A(|\xi|)=F^{\prime}_{0}(\Omega_{0}(|\xi|))
with
A
being defined in
Eq.

2.62
. The functional relationship at leading order is then our starting point to construct an approximate relationship inductively at order
M
. To accomplish this task, we proceed as in
[
12
]
and we introduce the following definition.

Definition 4.19
.

We say that a smooth function
F:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to the class
\mathsf{K}
if
F(\Omega_{0})\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

This class is introduced because it contains all the necessary information we can hope to propagate from
F_{0}
, since we clearly have
F_{0}\in\mathsf{K}
.
Then, a useful property that we need for functions in this class is contained in the following lemma, whose proof can be found in
[
12
, Lemma 3.19]
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
F:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be in the class
\mathsf{K}
. Then, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th order derivative of
F
has the property that
F^{(k)}(\Omega_{0})\Omega_{0}^{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, there exists an
F\in\mathsf{K}
such that
F(\Omega_{0})=\mathcal{H}
if and only if
\{\mathcal{H},\Omega_{0}\}=0
, namely
\mathcal{H}
is radially symmetric.

Note that the solvability of
F(\Omega_{0})=\mathcal{H}
is not contained in
[
12
, Lemma 3.19]
, but this is a straightforward consequence of the change of variables
s=\Omega_{0}(r)
. In addition, since
\mathcal{H}
may grow polynomially at infinity,
F
can have logarithmic singularity at
s=0
(which is the case for
F_{0}
).

We finally introduce the Taylor polynomial up to order
M
associated to a given smooth function
f(\varepsilon,s):(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(4.115)

\Pi_{M}f=\sum_{k=0}^{M}\frac{\varepsilon^{k}}{k!}\frac{{\rm d}^{k}f}{{\rm d}\varepsilon^{k}}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}.

This allow us to find a functional relations in the form
F^{(M)}=F_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}F_{2}+\dots\varepsilon^{M}F_{M}
, with
F_{i}\in\mathsf{K}
, for which the functional relationship holds for the associated Taylor polynomial at order
M
, safely computed at
\Omega_{0}
. Note that it is important to compute everything at
\Omega_{0}
since it is not true that
\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}
is always positive in the whole domain (but this is true locally), which is a condition required to work in class
\mathsf{K}
. We now have all the ingredients to conclude the construction of the functional relationship and obtain the desired properties.

Proposition 4.21
.

Given any integer
M\geq 2
, let
\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
and
\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}},\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}
be given as in
Eq.

4.103
,
Eq.

4.104
and
Eq.

4.109
,
Eq.

4.111
respectively. Then, there exists
\{F_{i},\widehat{F}_{i}\}_{i=2}^{M}\in\mathsf{K}
such that for
F^{(M)}=F_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}F_{k}
and
\widehat{F}^{(M)}=\widehat{F}_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}\widehat{F}_{k}
one has

(4.116)

\displaystyle\Pi_{M}\big(\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+F^{(M)}(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})\big)=0,\qquad\Pi_{M}\big(\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\widehat{F}(\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})\big)=0

Moreover, there exists
\sigma_{1}\in(0,1)
,
C>0
and
\widetilde{N}\in\mathbb{N}
depending on
M
such that, for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the functions

(4.117)

\Theta^{(M)}\coloneqq\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+F^{(M)}(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}),\qquad\widehat{\Theta}^{(M)}\coloneqq\widehat{\Phi}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+{\widehat{F}}^{(M)}(\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}})

are well defined for all
|\xi|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}
and satisfy the bound

(4.118)

\displaystyle\big|\nabla\Theta^{(M)}(\xi)\big|+\big|\nabla\widehat{\Theta}^{(M)}(\xi)\big|\leq C\varepsilon^{M+1}(1+|\xi|)^{\widetilde{N}},\qquad\text{for all }|\xi|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}.

Proof.

The proof can be reduced to the same arguments done in
[
12
, Proof of Proposition 3.20]
. Indeed, first of all we observe that the construction for the “hats”
\widehat{\cdot}
variables is the same and we avoid detailing this case. Then, we know that by Proposition
4.11
that we can expand

(4.119)

\Omega^{(M+1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\varepsilon^{M+1}{\Omega}_{M+1,{\rm E}},

Thus, in view of the definitions in
Eq.

4.109
and
Eq.

4.111
, we can expand the Eulerian streamfunction as

(4.120)

\Phi^{(M+1)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}=\Phi^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}+\varepsilon^{M+1}\Phi_{M+1,{\rm E}}.

Dropping from now on all the subscripts “
\rm app,E
”, by construction we know that

(4.121)

\{\Phi^{(M+1)},\Omega^{(M+1)}\}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+2}).

We can then proceed by induction on
M
as done in
[
12
, Proof of Proposition 3.20]
. The case
M=0
is covered,
M=1
is trivial and we can assume by induction that we are given

F^{(M)}=F_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{M}\varepsilon^{k}F_{i}\in\mathsf{K}

for some fixed
M\geq 1
. To construct the function
F^{(M+1)}=F^{(M)}+\varepsilon^{M+1}F_{M+1}
, we take
\Omega^{(M+1)}
and
\Phi^{(M+1)}
as defined before. We can then run the same arguments in
[
12
]
(just based on Poisson bracket’s identities and properties of Taylor polynomials) to deduce that there exists a function
\mathcal{H}_{M+1}\in\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that the following holds true:

(4.122)

\Pi_{M+1}(\Phi^{(M+1)}+F^{(M+1)}(\Omega^{(M+1)}))=\varepsilon^{M+1}\big(\underbrace{\mathcal{H}_{M+1}+\Phi_{M+1}+F_{0}^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})\Omega_{M+1}}_{\mathcal{E}_{M+1}}+F_{M+1}(\Omega_{0})\big).

and

(4.123)

\displaystyle 0=\{\mathcal{H}_{M+1},\Omega_{0}\}+\{\Phi_{M+1},\Omega_{0}\}+\{\Phi_{0},\Omega_{M+1}\}=\{\mathcal{E}_{M+1},\Omega_{0}\},

where in the last identity we used that
\Phi_{0}=-F_{0}(\Omega_{0})
and
\{F_{0}^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})\Omega_{M+1},\Omega_{0}\}=\{\Omega_{M+1},F_{0}(\Omega_{0})\}.

The identity
Eq.

4.123
guarantees that we satisfy the solvability condition in Lemma
4.20
to define
F_{M+1}\in\mathsf{K}
such that
F_{M+1}(\Omega_{0})=-\mathcal{E}_{M+1}
, so that we obtain
\Pi_{M+1}(\Phi^{(M+1)}+F^{(M+1)}(\Omega^{(M+1)}))=0
as desired.

The proof of the property
Eq.

4.118
is the same of
[
12
]
. This directly follows by a Taylor expansion in
\varepsilon
of
\Theta^{(M)}
at
\Omega_{0}
, since by definition we know that
\Pi_{M}\Theta^{(M)}=0
. Thus, cutting the Taylor expansion with the integral remainder it is not hard to prove
Eq.

4.118
.
∎

4.7.
Eulerian approximation and total angular momentum

To end our analysis of the approximate solution, we need to deduce a property of the Eulerian part of the approximate solution related to evolution of the total angular momentum, which is defined through the operator
Eq.

2.6
.
First of all, in analogy with
Eq.

2.24
we define

(4.124)

\displaystyle\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}(\xi_{j}(x),t)+\gamma\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}^{(M)}(\widehat{\xi}(x),t)\Big).

Then, by the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
in (
4.8
), following the derivation of the equations for
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
in Lemma
2.6
it is not hard to see that
\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}
solves the following forced
2D
Navier-Stokes equations

(4.125)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\partial}_{t}\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}+\{\psi_{\rm app}^{(M)}-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{\rm app}^{(M)})^{\prime}|x|^{2},\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}\}=\nu\Delta\omega+f_{M},\\
\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}|_{t=0}=\omega^{in}\end{cases},

where
\omega^{in}
as in
Eq.

1.4
and

(4.126)

\displaystyle f_{M}(x,t)=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t^{2}}\delta^{-1}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}_{M}(\xi_{j}(x),t)+\gamma\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}(\widehat{\xi}(x),t)\Big).

Then, as in the proof of Lemma
2.2
, a direct computation shows that

(4.127)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\omega_{\rm app}^{(M)}(t))=\Gamma N\big(\mathsf{r}^{2}+4\nu t\big(1+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big)\big)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{L}(f_{M}(\tau))\,{\rm d}\tau.

Using this exact evolution, we are able to deduce the following property that will be crucial in the nonlinear section.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\Omega_{\rm app}^{(M)},\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}^{(M)}
be the ones constructed in Proposition
4.11
. Then

(4.128)

\displaystyle\frac{(R_{\rm app}^{(M)})^{2}-\mathsf{r}^{2}}{d^{2}}+\varepsilon^{2}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}^{(M)}-\Omega_{0})+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}^{(M)}-\Omega_{0})\big)=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{M+3}+\delta\varepsilon^{4}).

Remark 4.23
.

Note that, by Proposition
4.15
,
(R_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)})^{2}=\mathsf{r}^{2}(1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6}))
. Moreover, since
\mathrm{L}(g)=\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{P}_{0}g)
for any function
g
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}
denotes the angular average, the explicit form of the second and third-order approximations defined in Sections
4.3
and
4.5
implies that
\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{P}_{0}(\Omega^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}-\Omega_{0}))
and
\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{P}_{0}(\widehat{\Omega}^{(M)}_{\mathrm{app,E}}-\Omega_{0}))
are of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4})
. Thus, the left-hand side of
Eq.

4.128
consists of terms of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{6})
and higher, indicating exact cancellations at each order up the ones needed to validate
Eq.

4.128
. One could directly verify this condition by hand, but this would be tedious given the number of terms involved in the iterative construction. Instead, we deduce the identity
Eq.

4.128
from the exact evolution of the total angular momentum in the 2D forced Navier-Stokes equations and the fact that our approximate radius fixes
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)})=0
.

Proof.

We omit the superscript
(M)
in this proof. By
Eq.

4.124
we know that

(4.129)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\omega_{\rm app}(t))=\frac{\Gamma}{\nu t}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\Omega_{\rm app}(\xi_{j}(x),t){\rm d}x+\gamma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}(\widehat{\xi}(x),t)\Big){\rm d}x.

Then, by the definition of
\xi_{j}
in
Eq.

2.23
and
\widehat{\xi}
in
Eq.

2.22
we can change variables in each integral as

(4.130)

\displaystyle x=Q_{j}\big(\sqrt{\nu t}\xi_{j}+(R_{\rm app}(t),0)\big),\qquad x=\sqrt{\nu t}\,\widehat{\xi}.

Since
|Q_{j}v|=|v|
for any vector
v
, the integrals of each term in
\xi_{j}
are equal and we therefore rename the dummy variables
\xi_{j},\widehat{\xi}
as
\xi
, to obtain that

(4.131)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\omega_{\rm app}(t))=\Gamma N\nu t\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\xi+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}(R_{\rm app},0)|^{2}\Omega_{\rm app}(\xi,t){\rm d}\xi+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}(t))\Big).

Note that

(4.132)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle|\xi+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}(R_{\rm app},0)|^{2}\Omega_{\rm app}(\xi,t){\rm d}\xi

(4.133)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app}(t))+2\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu t}}R_{\rm app}(t)\,\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega_{\rm app}(t))+\frac{R_{\rm app}^{2}}{\nu t}\mathrm{M}(\Omega_{\rm app}(t)).

Hence, since
\mathrm{M}(\Omega_{\rm app})=1
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega_{\rm app})=0
, combining the identity above with
Eqs.

4.131
and
4.127
, we see that

(4.134)

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{2}+4\nu t\big(1+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big)+\frac{1}{\Gamma N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{L}(f_{M}){\rm d}\tau=\nu t\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app}(t))+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}(t))\big)+R_{\rm app}^{2}.

Considering only the Eulerian part, since
\Omega_{0}=\frac{1}{4\pi}{\rm e}^{-|\xi|^{2}/4}
, we have

(4.135)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})=\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{0})+\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})=4+\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})

and analogously for
\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}
.
Thus, the term
4\nu t(1+\gamma/N)
cancels out with the Lamb-Oseen part of the approximate solution and we arrive at

(4.136)

\displaystyle R_{\rm app}^{2}-\mathsf{r}^{2}+(\varepsilon d)^{2}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})\big)

(4.137)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\Gamma N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{L}(f_{M}){\rm d}\tau-\delta(\varepsilon d)^{2}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,NS})+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,NS})\big).

By the definition
Eq.

4.126
of
f_{M}
, since
\mathrm{M}(\mathcal{R}_{M})=\mathrm{M}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M})=\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{M})=\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M})=0
, following the same computations in
Eqs.

4.131
and
4.133
we deduce that

(4.138)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{L}(f_{M}){\rm d}\tau=\nu\delta^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{R}_{M})+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M})\Big){\rm d}\tau=(\varepsilon d)^{2}\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{M+1}+\delta\varepsilon^{2}),

where in the last identity we used that
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}+\delta^{2}\varepsilon^{2}).
Having that
\Omega_{\rm app,NS},\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2}),
we finally get

(4.139)

\displaystyle\frac{R_{\rm app}^{2}-\mathsf{r}^{2}}{d^{2}}+\varepsilon^{2}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}-\Omega_{0})\big)=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{M+3}+\delta\varepsilon^{4}),

which proves the desired result.
∎

5.
The nonlinear corrections

The goal of this section is to show that the approximate solutions constructed in Section
4
are close to the solutions
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
in
Eq.

2.40
,
Eq.

2.41
with initial data
Eq.

2.42
. With the simplified notation in
Eqs.

2.79
,
2.80
and
2.83
the equations we need to study are

(5.1)

t{\partial}_{t}\Omega=\mathcal{L}\Omega-\frac{1}{\delta}\{\Phi_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}),\Omega\},\qquad t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{\Omega}=\mathcal{L}\widehat{\Omega}-\frac{1}{\delta}\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,R}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi}),\widehat{\Omega}\}.

We then split the solution as

(5.2)

\displaystyle\Omega=\Omega_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)}+\delta w,\qquad\widehat{\Omega}=\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)}+\delta\widehat{w},

(5.3)

\displaystyle\Psi=\Psi_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)}+\delta\varphi,\qquad\widehat{\Psi}=\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{app}}^{(M)}+\delta\widehat{\varphi}.

The main objective is to control the solution on a time interval
[0,T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma})
for a given fixed
\sigma\in[0,1)
. To ensure that the remainders
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
are small enough, we will assume that on the whole time interval
[0,T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma})

(5.4)

\displaystyle\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}(t)\ll\varepsilon^{2}(t)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad M>\frac{5-\sigma}{1-\sigma}.

Therefore, given any
\sigma\in[0,1)
, the order of the approximate solution is fixed as a number
M\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying the condition above. We will think to
\sigma
and therefore
M
as fixed parameters from now on. Thus, we will always omit the superscript
(M)
in the rest of this section.

5.1.
Preliminary considerations on the moments

Before writing down the nonlinear system, we need to specify the radius and angular speed. Unfortunately,
given the complicated dependence of the operators with respect to changes in the radius, we are not able to fix exactly the first order moments with the choices made in
Lemma

2.7
. Thus, we choose

R(t)=R_{\mathrm{app}}(t)

with
R_{\mathrm{app}}(t)
defined in
Eq.

4.5
. Since this is fixed, from now on we avoid tracking the explicit dependence on all the operators on
R_{\rm app}
.

Remark 5.1
.

With the choice of
R(t)=R_{\rm app}(t)
, in view of Proposition
4.15
we automatically satisfy the expansion provided in
Eq.

1.15
.

Then, as announced in Remark
2.8
, we slightly modify the definition of
Eq.

2.67
to

(5.5)

\displaystyle t\alpha^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{d\varepsilon(t)}{\delta(R_{\rm app}+\varepsilon d\,\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega))(t)}{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi(t),\widehat{\Psi}(t))),

where we need to guarantee that
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega(t))
remains sufficiently small on our time-scale. As one can directly see from
Eq.

2.72
, we know that the choice above guarantees that

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(\Omega(t))=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(w(t))=0,\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

where we used that
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm app}(t))=(0,0).
Another consequence of the zero first order moment condition for the approximate solution, is that
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega(t))=\delta\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w(t))
and we can thus rewrite
Eq.

5.5
as

(5.7)

\displaystyle t\alpha^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq t\alpha_{\rm app}^{\prime}(t)+\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app},\varphi,\widehat{\varphi})(t)

where, suppressing the explicit
t
dependence, we define

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app},\varphi,\widehat{\varphi}):=\frac{d\varepsilon}{R_{\rm app}}{\rm Im}\Big(

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app},\varphi))+\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\Psi_{\rm app}+\delta\varphi)

(5.9)

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon d\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)}{R_{\rm app}+\delta\varepsilon d\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app}+\delta\varphi,\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app}+\delta\widehat{\varphi})\Big)

Having lost one modulation parameter, we can only hope to obtain some bound on the first order moment
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)
. To this end, we crucially use the property of the Eulerian part of the approximate solution stated in Lemma
4.22
to obtain the following.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\Omega,\widehat{\Omega}
be as in
Eq.

5.2
. Then, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(5.10)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w(t))|\leq C\big(\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+2}+\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon(\mathrm{L}(w)+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{w}))\big)(t)

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Lemma
4.22
, but now our
\omega(t)
solves the 2D Navier-Stokes equation
Eq.

2.35
without external force, meaning that Lemma
2.2
holds true. Thus, arguing as done to obtain the identities
Eqs.

4.131
and
4.133
, we see that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{2}+4\nu t\big(1+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big)=\nu t\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega(t))+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}(t))\big)+2\sqrt{\nu t}R_{\rm app}\,\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\Omega(t))+R_{\rm app}^{2}.

Since
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(\Omega)=\delta\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)
, using Lemma
4.22
we get

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)=\,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{2\delta\varepsilon dR_{\rm app}}\Big(\frac{\mathsf{r}^{2}-R_{\rm app}^{2}}{d^{2}}-\varepsilon^{2}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}-G)+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}-G)\big)\Big)

(5.13)

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R_{\rm app}}\big(\mathrm{L}(\Omega_{\rm app,NS}+w)+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,NS}+\widehat{w})\big)

(5.14)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+2}+\varepsilon^{3})-\frac{\varepsilon d}{2R_{\rm app}}\big(\mathrm{L}(w)+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathrm{L}(\widehat{w})\big),

where in the last identity we also used that
R_{\rm app}\approx\mathsf{r}
by the constraint
Item

C4)
and
\Omega_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2}).

∎

5.2.
The nonlinear system

Having fixed
R=R_{\rm app}
and
\alpha
as in
Eq.

5.5
, we are ready to write down the nonlinear system for
w,\widehat{w}
. To simplify the notation, we avoid keeping track of the subscripts
\alpha,R
since are fixed from now on, and we will write
\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}
to actually denote the integral operator defined in
Eq.

5.8
.

Then, we observe that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})=\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{app}})+\delta((I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})(\varphi)+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\varphi}))-\frac{\delta}{2}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},

(5.16)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi,\widehat{\Psi})=\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{app}})+\delta\big(\gamma\widehat{\varphi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\big)-\frac{\delta}{2}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}|\xi|^{2}.

recalling the definitions of
\Phi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app}
in
Eq.

4.9

and
\Phi_{\rm app,E},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}
in
Eqs.

4.109
and
4.111
we get

(5.17)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{app}})=\Phi_{\rm app}=\Phi_{\rm app,E}+\delta\Phi_{\rm app,NS},

(5.18)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\mathrm{app}},\widehat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{app}})=\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app}=\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}+\delta\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,NS}.

Moreover, by construction
\Phi_{\rm app,NS},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{2})
.
Finally, recalling the definitions of
\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
in
Eq.

4.8
,
by a direct computation we find that
w,\widehat{w}
satisfy

(5.19)

\displaystyle(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})w+\frac{1}{\delta}\big(\Lambda_{\mathrm{app}}(w)+\Upsilon_{\mathrm{app}}(\widehat{w})\big)=\frac{1}{2\delta}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\delta^{-2}\mathcal{R}_{M}+\mathcal{N}(w,\widehat{w})

(5.20)

\displaystyle(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L})\widehat{w}+\frac{1}{\delta}\big(\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{app}}(w)+\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{app}}(\widehat{w})\big)=\frac{1}{2\delta}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\delta^{-2}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M}+\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(w,\widehat{w})

where we define:

(1)

The linear operators arising from the linearization at the Eulerian approximate solution are

(5.21)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{app}}(f)=\{\Phi_{\rm app,E},f\}+\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}f,\Omega_{\rm app,E}\},

(5.22)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\rm app}(f)=\gamma\{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f,\Omega_{\rm app,E}\},

(5.23)
