\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{app}}(f)=\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E},f\}+\gamma\{\Delta^{-1}f,\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\},

(5.24)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\rm app}(f)=\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f,\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\}.

(2)

The errors associated to the Navier-Stokes part of the approximate solution and the nonlinear interactions for the outer vortices are

(5.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{N}_{j}(w,\widehat{w})

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=-\{\Phi_{\rm app,NS},\,w\}-\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\varphi},\,\Omega_{\rm app,NS}\}

(5.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{2}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=-\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\gamma\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\varphi},\,w\}

(5.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{3}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\rm app,NS}+w\}.

For the central vortex we have

(5.29)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{j}(w,\widehat{w})\,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{1}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=-\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,NS},\,\widehat{w}\}-\{\gamma\widehat{\varphi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\varphi,\,\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,NS}\}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{2}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=-\{\gamma\widehat{\varphi}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\varphi,\,\widehat{w}\}

(5.32)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{3}(w,\widehat{w})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,NS}+\widehat{w}\}.

The equations
Eqs.

5.19
and
5.20
are solved with zero initial data. Indeed,
\Omega_{\rm app},\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}\to\Omega_{0}
as
t\to 0
and therefore, by the uniqueness of the solution
[
20
]
, we know that
w,\widehat{w}\to 0
as
t\to 0
. Regarding the first order moments, it is straightforward to see that

(5.33)

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}(w(t))=\mathrm{M}(\widehat{w}(t))=0,\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

Then, the
N
-fold symmetry of
\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app}
ensures that

(5.34)

\displaystyle\widehat{w}(Q_{l}\xi)=\widehat{w}(\xi)\text{ for any }l=1,\dots N,\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{m}^{1}(\widehat{w}(t))=(0,0)\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

where in the last identity we used
N\geq 2
. The choice of the angular speed guarantees that
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(w(t))=0
whereas by Lemma
5.2
we have the a priori bound
Eq.

5.10
for
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w(t))
.
We are then ready to state the main theorem of this section, which implies the main result of the paper Theorem
1.2
as we explain below.

Theorem 5.3
.

Fix
\sigma\in[0,1)
, let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
Eq.

5.4
is satisfied and
w,\widehat{w}
be the solutions to
Eqs.

5.19
and
5.20
with zero initial data. Then, there exists positive constants
C>1,\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for any
\Gamma,\nu
satisfying
\nu/\Gamma=\delta\leq\delta_{0}
the following holds true:

(5.35)

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\widehat{w}(t)\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}(t)\quad\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma}),

where
\varepsilon,T_{\mathsf{adv}}
are defined in
Eq.

1.8
, and the functions spaces
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon},\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are defined in
Eq.

5.56
.

The proof of this theorem follows the strategy put forward in
[
22
]
and adapted to reach time-scales arbitrary close to the diffusive one in
[
12
]
. The main ideas and difficulties in the proof are explained in Section
5.3
as a preparation for the technical proof, which is the content of the rest of the paper.

During the proof, we need to propagate a control of the first order moment
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)
that depends on
w
as well. Thus, we are going to prove Theorem
5.3
under the bootstrap assumption that there exists a maximal time
T\leq T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
such that

(B)

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\widehat{w}(t)\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\leq 2C_{\star}\varepsilon^{2}(t)\qquad\text{ for all }t\in[0,T),

where
C_{\star}>0
is a constant fixed during the proof
7
7

7

We can take
C_{\star}=\sqrt{KC_{1}}
with
K
in Proposition
5.11
and
C_{1}
in Proposition
5.15
.
.
This hypothesis can be easily verified by a short time estimate for
T=1
and with
2
replaced by
1/2
. By continuity, we can assume that
Eq.

B
holds true up to some time
T>1
. If under the bootstrap assumption
Eq.

B
we are able to show that

(5.36)

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\widehat{w}(t)\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{\star}\varepsilon^{2}(t)\qquad\text{ for all }t\in[0,T),

then by a standard continuity argument we can take
T=T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
and thus prove Theorem
5.3
. Then the proof of Theorem
1.2
readily follows.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Assume that the result in Theorem
5.3
holds true. By rescaling, the bound
Eq.

5.35
readily implies
Eq.

1.14
in Theorem
1.2
. The proof of
Eq.

1.15
is then a consequence of Remark
5.1
and Corollary
5.10
below, which combines the bounds on
w,\widehat{w}
with the definition of
\alpha^{\prime}
in
Eq.

5.7
and the expansion for
\alpha^{\prime}_{\rm app}
established in Proposition
4.15
.
∎

Further conventions and notations

In this section, we introduce the following conventions. Given an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-based Hilbert space
X
, we denote

(5.37)

\widehat{X}=\{f\in X\,:\,f\text{ is $N$-fold symmetric}\}.

Then, for
f\in X
and
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{X}
we write

(5.38)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{f}}=(f,\widehat{f}\,)\in X\times\widehat{X}=:{\mathbf{X}},

(5.39)

\displaystyle\nabla{\mathbf{f}}=(\nabla f,\nabla\widehat{f}\,),

(5.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}{\mathbf{f}}=(\mathcal{L}f,\mathcal{L}\widehat{f}\,),

(5.41)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{g}}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}=\langle f,g\rangle_{X}+\langle\widehat{f},\widehat{g}\rangle_{\widehat{X}},

(5.42)

\displaystyle\mathrm{m}^{k}_{j}({\mathbf{f}})=(\mathrm{m}^{k}_{j}(f),\mathrm{m}^{k}_{j}({\widehat{f}})).

For a given
N
-fold symmetric function
g
, we write
g{\mathbf{f}}=(gf,g\widehat{f})
. When
X=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we do not write the subscript in the inner products and norms.

5.3.
Main difficulties and key ideas

Looking at the equations
Eqs.

5.19
and
5.20
, we isolated explicitly the most dangerous terms containing a factor
\delta^{-1}
.
The key idea to control those terms, is to exploit the Arnold’s strategy in the viscous setting as recently revisited in
[
21
]
, which was first applied to control a viscous vortex ring over a very long-time scale in
[
22
]
and then in 2D situations in
[
12
,
49
]
. To apply this strategy, one has to understand properties of the operator

(5.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{\mathrm{app}}&\Upsilon_{\mathrm{app}}\\
\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\mathrm{app}}&\widehat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{app}}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, one would like to solve the following problem:

Find a functional space where the operator
\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}

is skew-adjoint.

For our purposes, it is in fact enough that the operator
\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}
is skew-adjoint up to errors of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M+1})
. Here, we have to crucially exploit Proposition
4.21
, that provides us with an approximate functional relationship between
\Phi_{\rm app,E}
and
\Omega_{\rm app,E}
in the region
|\xi|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}
. This allow us to heuristically approximate

(5.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}\approx\begin{pmatrix}\big\{\big(F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})+(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}\big)(\cdot),\Omega_{\rm app,E}\big\}&\gamma\big\{\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\Omega_{\rm app,E}\big\}\\[6.0pt]
\big\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\big\}&\big\{\big(\widehat{F}^{\prime}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E})+\gamma\Delta^{-1}\big)(\cdot),\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\big\}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, exploiting the fact that
\widehat{w}
is
N
-fold symmetric, we can restrict our attention to the action of the second column above to
N
-fold symmetric functions. Appealing to Lemma
3.9
, we know that

(5.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f=\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\quad\text{for any }N\text{-fold symmetric }f.

To construct a functional space where
\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}
is skew-adjoint, looking at its structure we define

(5.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{app}}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})+(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\\[6.0pt]
\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}(\widehat{F}^{\prime}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E})+\gamma\Delta^{-1}).\end{pmatrix}

By Lemma
3.9
, we know that
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}

is self-adjoint in
\mathbf{L^{2}}
.
Moreover, from the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
, one has that
\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}
is approximately skew-adjoint with respect to the
\mathbf{L^{2}}
inner product

\langle{\mathbf{f}},\,\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}({\mathbf{g}})\rangle.

Namely, if we assume that
Eq.

5.44
holds up to errors of order
\varepsilon^{M+1}
, by a direct computation we get

(5.47)

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}({\mathbf{f}}),\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{app}}({\mathbf{f}})\big\rangle\approx\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

Therefore, if
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
is a positive operator, we would have a nice inner-product space where we can work (since
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
is self-adjoint). Unfortunately,
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
can have non-trivial elements in its kernel or even be negative on some functions. Indeed, even by formally setting
\varepsilon\to 0
, one has that
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
reduces to

(5.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{0}(\Omega_{0})+\Delta^{-1}&0\\
0&\frac{\gamma^{2}}{N}(F^{\prime}_{0}(\Omega_{0})+\Delta^{-1}).\end{pmatrix}

For the operator above, one has that
{\partial}_{1}\Omega_{0},{\partial}_{2}\Omega_{0}
are in the kernel
8
8

8

This is also why
{\partial}_{i}\Omega_{0}={\partial}_{i}G
are in the kernel of
\Lambda
as stated in Proposition
3.4
, from which one can deduce that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is at most non-negative (see
[
21
]
). This implies that we cannot use
\mathcal{M}_{0}
to define an inner-product space. On the other hand, one can avoid problems related to these elements in the kernel by assuming that the first order moments are sufficiently small. Indeed, from
[
21
]
it is possible to deduce that

(5.49)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{f}},\mathcal{M}_{0}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle\geq\kappa(\|\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})}\widehat{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\big)-C(|\mathrm{M}({\mathbf{f}})|^{2}+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}({\mathbf{f}})|^{2}+|\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}({\mathbf{f}})|^{2})

where
\kappa,C>0
are fixed constant. Therefore, if we can impose the zero first order moments condition, we would indeed have a nice inner-product space defined through
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Moreover, once things are well behaved for
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, following
[
22
,
12
]
one can essentially see
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
as a perturbation of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Regarding the first order moments, one possibility is to exploit the modulation of some parameters to set them to zero. The modulation can however bring new error terms that can be very large on long time-scales, and usually one has to exploit symmetries to handle them
[
12
]
in the time-scale of interest. To avoid issues related to the modulation parameters (as the radius in our case), the bound
Eq.

5.49
is enough if one is able to propagate the smallness of the first-order moments by some other arguments, as done for instance in
[
22
]
by using some approximate conservation law in the system or in
[
49
]
by controlling
pseudo-momenta
.

In our case, we have seen in Lemma
5.2
that with a good enough approximate position we can guarantee the smallness of
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)
(provided
w,\widehat{w}
decay sufficiently fast an infinity). However, the modulation of the angular speed used to guarantee
\mathrm{m}_{2}^{1}(w)=0
is generating the potentially dangerous term

(5.50)

\frac{\mathfrak{b_{\varepsilon}}}{2\delta}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}.\end{pmatrix}

The element above is related to the rotation of the Eulerian part of the whole approximate vortex polygon around the center, because the derivative
\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\cdot\}
is just an angular derivative with respect to the global origin in the reference frame of an exterior vortex. But the whole Eulerian approximate configuration is symmetric with respect to rotation around the global origin. This symmetry is reflected in the fact that the element
Eq.

5.50
is approximately in the kernel of
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
. Namely, as we show more precisely in Lemma
5.8
, we expect that

(5.51)

\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}\approx\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).

This is also a consequence of the existence of the functional relationship between
\Omega_{\rm app,E}
,
\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}
and
\Phi_{\rm app,E}
,
\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}
and the commutation properties in Lemma
3.9
(themselves related to symmetries in the problem). We note that a property analogous to the one above was in fact the starting point in the construction of the “non-trivial pseudo-momenta” in
[
49
]
.

The rest of this section is devoted in making the arguments above rigorous, which requires the introduction of many technical tools developed in
[
22
,
23
,
12
]
. In particular, we will use some of the results in
[
12
]
as a black box and we only focus on the novelties and difficulties of our problem.

5.4.
The functional setting and basic properties

We introduce two parameters and a constant as follows

\sigma_{1}\in(0,1),\qquad\sigma_{2}>1,\qquad\varsigma\coloneqq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{2}}<1.

Here
\sigma_{1}
is a sufficiently small parameter, depending only on the order
M
, that is implicitly updated during the proof. A first condition on this parameter is that Proposition
4.21
holds true (namely one can start with
\sigma_{1}
as the parameter introduced in Proposition
4.21
). Instead,
\sigma_{2}>1
is sufficiently large, used to exploit the decay at infinity of some parts of the solution. Then, by Proposition
4.21
in the region
|\xi|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}
we have the existence of the functional relationship between
\Omega_{\rm app,E}
and
\Phi_{\rm app,E}
. Therefore, for the exterior vortices we define three time-dependent (through the parameter
\varepsilon
) regions

(5.52)

\displaystyle{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,|\xi|<2\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}},\,F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}(\xi))<\exp(\varepsilon^{-2\sigma_{1}/4})\}

(5.53)

\displaystyle{\rm II}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,\xi\notin{\rm I}_{\varepsilon},\,|\xi|<\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{2}}\}

(5.54)

\displaystyle{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,|\xi|>\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{2}}\}.

For the central vortex, we define
{\rm\widehat{I}}_{\varepsilon},{\rm\widehat{II}}_{\varepsilon}
by replacing
F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})
with
\widehat{F}^{\prime}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E})
.
The weight functions are then defined as

(5.55)

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon}(\xi)=\begin{cases}F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}(\xi))&\text{in }{\rm I}_{\varepsilon},\\
\exp(\varepsilon^{-2\sigma_{1}}/4)&\text{in }{\rm II}_{\varepsilon},\\
\exp(|\xi|^{2\varsigma}/4)&\text{in }{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}\qquad\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}(\xi)=\begin{cases}\widehat{F}^{\prime}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}(\xi))&\text{in }{\rm\widehat{I}}_{\varepsilon},\\
\exp(\varepsilon^{-2\sigma_{1}}/4)&\text{in }{\rm\widehat{II}}_{\varepsilon},\\
\exp(|\xi|^{2\varsigma}/4)&\text{in }{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

We introduce the weighted
L^{2}
space

(5.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}=\big\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,:\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}=\sqrt{\langle f,A_{\varepsilon}f\rangle}<\infty\big\}.

The space
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
is defined as
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
A_{\varepsilon}
replaced by
\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}
and restricted to
N
-fold symmetric functions. Note that when defining
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
as a function space acting only on
N
-fold symmetric functions, we are implicitly using the
N
-fold symmetry of
\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}
. Then, we denote

(5.57)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A_{\varepsilon}w}=(A_{\varepsilon}f,\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{f}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}\times\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon},

(5.58)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{f}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}=\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}.

Moreover, since as
\varepsilon\to 0
we formally have
A_{\varepsilon}(\xi)\to A_{0}(\xi)=F_{0}^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})=A(\xi)
and
\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}(\xi)\to\widehat{F}_{0}^{\prime}(\Omega_{0})=\gamma A(\xi)
with
A
defined in
Eq.

2.62
, we denote

\mathcal{X}_{0}\coloneqq L^{2}(A(\xi){\rm d}\xi)\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\{f\in\mathcal{X}_{0}\,:\,f\text{ is }N-\text{fold symmetric}\}.

The important properties of these spaces are summarized in the following.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the following holds true:

i)

\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon},\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}\hookrightarrow L^{p}
for any
p\in[1,2]
.

ii)

\exp(|\xi|^{2\varsigma}/4)\lesssim A_{\varepsilon}(\xi),\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}(\xi)\lesssim A_{0}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

iii)

\|A_{\varepsilon}-A_{0}\|_{C^{1}({\rm I}_{\varepsilon})}+\|\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}-\widehat{A}_{0}\|_{C^{1}({\rm\widehat{I}}_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\varsigma_{1}}A_{0}
for any
\varsigma_{1}<2
.

These properties can be proved as in
[
12
, Proposition 4.4]
, and we thus omit the proof here.

Remark 5.5
.

By the exponential growth of
A_{\varepsilon}
, we know that for a function
f\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}
we can bound any moment at a fixed order
n\in\mathbb{N}
since

(5.59)

\displaystyle\|(1+|\xi|)^{n}f\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}.

Therefore, under the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
, we know that for any
T\leq T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
we get

(5.60)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w(t))|\lesssim\varepsilon^{3}(t)\quad\text{ for any }t\leq T.

We also recall the following estimates.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}
be such that
,|\nabla f|\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}
and let
\phi=\Delta^{-1}f
. Then, for all
q\in(2,\infty)
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(5.61)

\displaystyle\|(1+|\cdot|)^{2}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(1+|\cdot|)^{-1}\phi\|_{L^{q}}+\|(1+|\cdot|)^{2}\nabla\phi\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}

(5.62)

\displaystyle\|(1+|\cdot|)^{3}\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\big(\|\nabla f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Moreover, if
\mathrm{M}(f)=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(f)=0
and
|Z|>c>0
then

(5.63)

\displaystyle\|\mathbbm{1}_{\rm I_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\phi\|_{L^{q}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\|\mathbbm{1}_{\rm I_{\varepsilon}}(1+|\cdot|)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\phi\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\varepsilon(\varepsilon\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(f)|).

The statements remain true true with
(\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon},{\rm I}_{\varepsilon})\to(\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon},{\rm\widehat{I}}_{\varepsilon})
and
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\to\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z},\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}
(possibly with a different constant
C>0
).

The proof with
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(f)=0
can be found in
[
12
, Section A.4]
, and the extension to
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(f)\neq 0
is a straightforward consequence of that proof.

Finally, following the discussion in Section
5.3
, we modify the definition of the operator
\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
and we introduce

(5.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\varepsilon}+(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\\[6.0pt]
\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}(\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}+\gamma\Delta^{-1})\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{M}_{\rm app}
in the interior region
\boldsymbol{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}
.
It is also often convenient to isolate the “lower-order entries” of
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
given by

(5.65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\\[6.0pt]
\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},

so that we have the following splitting

(5.66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\varepsilon}+\Delta^{-1}&0\\[6.0pt]
0&\frac{\gamma}{N}(\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}+\gamma\Delta^{-1})\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}.

We can then exploit Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
to obtain the following useful properties.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}\to\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
be the operators defined in
Eqs.

5.64
and
5.65
. Then,
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
are self-adjoint with respect to the standard
L^{2}
-inner product. Moreover, under the hypotheses of Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(5.67)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle|\leq C\|{\mathbf{f}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\|{\mathbf{g}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}

(5.68)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle|+\varepsilon^{-1}|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\nabla\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle|\leq C\varepsilon(\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{f}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(f)|)\|{\mathbf{g}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}},

for any
{\mathbf{g}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
and any
{\mathbf{f}}=(f,\widehat{f})
with
\mathrm{M}(f)=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(f)=0
as in Lemma
5.6
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
are self-adjoint in
L^{2}
is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.9
. To prove the bound
Eq.

5.68
, we first observe that by Lemma
5.6
and the Hölder inequality with exponents
1/p+1/q=1
and
q>2
, we have

(5.69)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\Delta^{-1}{\mathbf{f}}\rangle|\lesssim\|{\mathbf{g}}\|_{L^{p}}\|\Delta^{-1}{\mathbf{f}}\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\|{\mathbf{f}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\|{\mathbf{g}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}

where in the last inequality we used also Lemma
5.4
since
p<2
. Thus, by the splitting
Eq.

5.66
we get

(5.70)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle|\lesssim\|{\mathbf{f}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\|{\mathbf{g}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+|\langle{\mathbf{g}},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{f}})\rangle|.

It remains to control the term involving the operator
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
. Let us consider for instance the term involving
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}
, since the others are analogous. Splitting the domain and applying the Hölder inequality, observe that

(5.71)

\displaystyle|\langle g,\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\rangle|\lesssim\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}g\|_{L^{p}}\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\|_{L^{q}}+\big\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}A^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}g\big\|\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}A^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\|,

where
1/p+1/q=1
with
q>2
.
To bound the first term on the right-hand side above, we use
Eq.

5.63
and Lemma
5.4
. For the second term, we bound
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f
in
L^{\infty}
thanks to
Eq.

5.61
and we use the fast decay properties of
A_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
to get that
\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}A_{\varepsilon}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\lesssim\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\infty})(1+|\xi|)^{-2}
. Overall, we have

(5.72)

\displaystyle|\langle g,\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\rangle|\lesssim\varepsilon\|g\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(f)|)\lesssim\varepsilon\|g\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}},

where in the last bound we used Remark
5.5
.
For the other terms in
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
we can perform analogous estimates and therefore we deduce that
Eq.

5.68
holds true.
To prove the bound involving
\nabla\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
, observe that

(5.73)

\displaystyle|\langle g,\nabla\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\rangle|\lesssim\,

\displaystyle\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}(1+|\cdot|)g\|_{L^{p}}\|\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}(1+|\cdot|)^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\|_{L^{q}}

(5.74)

\displaystyle+\big\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}A^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}g\big\|\|\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}A^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}\nabla\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}f\|.

In the first term, we gain an extra factor of
\varepsilon
thanks to
Eq.

5.63
. For the second term, it is enough to apply Hölder inequality with
1/q+1/p=1/2
in the term involving
A^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}
and combine the properties in Lemma
5.6
with the exponential decay of
A_{\varepsilon}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
outside the inner region. Hence, it is not hard to conclude that
Eq.

5.68
holds true.
∎

Finally, to end this section let us formalize the property claimed in
Eq.

5.51
as follows.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
be defined as in
Eq.

5.64
. Then, for any
\mathbf{f}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}

(5.75)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}f\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{f}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle=\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Theta\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Theta}\}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}f\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{f}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle

where
\Theta,\widehat{\Theta}
are the functions defined in Proposition
4.21
.

Proof.

We check the identity component wise for the term involving
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
. Since we are looking at the first component in the interior region
{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}
and the second on
\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}
, we know that
A_{\varepsilon}=F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E})
and
\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}=\widehat{F}^{\prime}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E})
. Thus, on the support of the integral we can write

(5.76)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}

(5.77)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},F(\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E})\}+(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\frac{\gamma}{N}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{F}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E})\}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{N}\Delta^{-1}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}.

Appealing to Lemma
3.9
and using
Eqs.

3.27
,
3.29
and
3.28
, we have that

(5.78)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\frac{\gamma^{2}}{N}\Delta^{-1}\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}

(5.79)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\frac{\gamma}{N}\{|\xi|^{2},\gamma\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}+\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}.\end{pmatrix}

Recalling the definition of
\Phi_{\rm app,E},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}
in
Eqs.

4.109
and
4.111
, since
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}=N^{-1}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}
, we get that on the support of the integral

(5.80)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},F(\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E})+\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{F}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E})+\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}\}\end{pmatrix}.

The proof of the lemma is then a direct consequence of the definition of
\Theta,\widehat{\Theta}
in Proposition
4.21
.
∎

5.5.
Bound on the angular speed correction

Our first task is to bound the correction to the approximate angular speed
\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}
defined in
Eq.

5.8
. This bound is reminiscent of what was done in
[
12
, Lemma 4.7]
, since in that case one fixes the moment
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}(w)
by a nonlinear correction to the vertical speed of the dipole. In our case, we can obtain better estimates compared to
[
12
, Lemma 4.7]
because the approximate angular speed is exactly the one given by the approximate solution. This is reflected in the fact that for
w=\widehat{w}=0
we get
\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}=0
, whereas in the dipole case in
[
12
]
there are errors left due to the iterative construction of the approximate vertical speed.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
be defined as in
Eq.

5.8
. Under the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
t\in[0,T)

(5.81)

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app},\varphi,\widehat{\varphi})(t)|\leq C\big(\varepsilon^{3}(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})(1+\delta\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}})\big)(t)\lesssim\varepsilon^{6}(t).

Proof.

We bound the term in bracket in
Eq.

5.8
. To this end, note that

(5.82)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(h,\widehat{h},\widetilde{h}))={\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{h}},\widetilde{h}))=\langle{\partial}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\Delta{\mathbf{h}}),(\Delta\widetilde{h},0)\rangle.

Then, by Lemma
3.6
we can expand
{\partial}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\rm app})
and use
\mathrm{m}^{1}(\Omega_{\rm app})=(0,0)
to see that

(5.83)

\displaystyle|{\rm Im}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app},\varphi))|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon|\mathrm{M}(w)|+\varepsilon^{2}|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon^{3}\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}

(5.84)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}).

For the second term in bracket in
Eq.

5.8
, we directly combine the identity
Eq.

5.82
with Lemma
5.7
to get

(5.85)

\displaystyle|{\rm Im}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}((\varphi,\widehat{\varphi},\Psi_{\rm app}+\delta\varphi))|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})(1+\delta\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}).

Finally, for the last term we observe that by the constraint
Item

C4)
and
Eq.

5.60
we get

(5.86)

\displaystyle|R_{\rm app}+\delta\varepsilon d\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w(t))|\gtrsim\mathsf{r}

Since also
|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app},\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app})|\lesssim\varepsilon
, arguing as for the other terms it is not hard to get

(5.87)

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon d|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|}{|R_{\rm app}+\delta\varepsilon d\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|}|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app}+\delta\varphi,\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app}+\delta\widehat{\varphi})|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})(1+\delta\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}).

Hence, the proof follows by plugging the bounds above in
Eq.

5.8
and the bound with
\varepsilon^{4}
is a consequence of the bootstrap hypothesis.
∎

As a corollary of the bound in
Lemma

5.9
, we can verify the formula for the vertical speed in
Eq.

1.15
.

Corollary 5.10
.

Under the hypotheses of Lemma
5.9
, the formula for
\alpha^{\prime}
in
Eq.

1.15
holds true.

Proof.

Combining the identity
Eq.

5.7
with
Eq.

4.97
in the proof of Proposition
4.15
, note that

(5.88)

\displaystyle\delta t\alpha^{\prime}=d\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}}c_{1,0}(S_{1,1}+\gamma)\Big(1+\alpha_{4}\varepsilon^{6}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{7}+\delta\varepsilon^{6})\Big)+\delta\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}.

Using Lemma
5.9
, we can include the factor
\delta\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}
in the
\mathcal{O}(\delta\varepsilon^{6})
term and proceed as in the proof of Proposition
4.15
to prove
Eq.

1.15
and
Eq.

1.16
.
∎

5.6.
Energy and dissipation functionals

In view of the discussion in Section
5.3
and the definition of the operator
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
in
Eq.

5.64
, we introduce the energy functional that mimics the Arnold quadratic form, given by

(5.89)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbf{w}},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle,\qquad{\mathbf{w}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon},

where we have used the notation
Eqs.

5.38
and
5.41
.
In the formal limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, thanks to Lemma
5.4
note that
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
. In view of
Eq.

5.49
, we then expect that
E_{0}
defines a norm equivalent to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
, provided that
\mathrm{m}^{k}_{j}({\mathbf{w}})=(0,0)
for
k=0,1
and
j=1,2
. As in
[
12
,
22
]
, we are able to extend this property to any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, as stated below.

Proposition 5.11
.

If
\varepsilon,\sigma_{1}>0
are sufficiently small, there exist a constant
K>1
, independent of
\varepsilon
, such that the following holds true: let
{\mathbf{w}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
be such that
\mathrm{M}({\mathbf{w}})=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}({\mathbf{w}})=(0,0)
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\widehat{w})=0
. Then

(5.90)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}\leq K(E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|^{2})

Remark 5.12
.

In the bound
Eq.

5.90
, one cannot hope to easily get a small constant in front of
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)
. This is the reason why we need to bound a priori
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)
with Lemma
5.2
.

Proof.

We first estimate the quadratic form associated to the operator
Eq.

5.65
.
In view of Lemma
5.7
, we have

(5.91)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{w}},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle|\lesssim\varepsilon(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}.

We then have to control

(5.92)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{w}},(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}){\mathbf{w}}\rangle=\big(\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\langle\varphi,w\rangle\big)+\big(\|\widehat{w}\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\langle\widehat{\varphi},\widehat{w}\rangle\big).

For these terms we can argue exactly as done in the proof of
[
12
, Proposition 4.9]
(see the bounds for
E_{\varepsilon}^{1}(w)
in
[
12
, Proposition 4.9]
) and easily adapt the proof to include the case where
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)\neq 0
. We omit these details and therefore the proof is over.
∎

With this proposition at hand, we know that it is enough to control
E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}(t))
to improve our bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
and consequently prove Theorem
5.3
. Before proceeding with the estimates for
E_{\varepsilon}
, we introduce the
diffusive quadratic form

(5.93)

\displaystyle D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}t{\partial}_{t}A_{\varepsilon}&0\\
0&\frac{\gamma}{N}t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{w}}\Big\rangle-\langle\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

where we have used the notation
Eqs.

5.38
,
5.41
,
5.40
and
5.57
.
To state its properties, we define the radially symmetric function

(5.94)

\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}(\xi)=\begin{cases}|\xi|,\qquad&\text{if }|\xi|<\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}\\
\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}\qquad&\text{if }\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}\leq|\xi|<\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{2}},\\
|\xi|^{\varsigma}\qquad&\text{if }|\xi|\geq\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{2}},\end{cases}

Then, in analogy with
[
12
, Proposition 4.13]
, we have the following result.

Proposition 5.13
.

If
\varepsilon,\sigma_{1}>0
are sufficiently small and
\sigma_{2}
is sufficiently large, there exist
\kappa_{D},C>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
, such that the following holds true: let
{\mathbf{w}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
be such that
\rho_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
,
|\nabla w|\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon},|\nabla\widehat{w}|\in\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}
,
\mathrm{M}({\mathbf{w}})=\mathrm{m}^{1}_{2}({\mathbf{w}})=(0,0)
and
\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(\widehat{w})=0
. Then

(5.95)

D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\geq\kappa_{D}\big(\|\nabla{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\rho_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\|{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\big)-C|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|^{2}

Proof.

Using the notation in
Eq.

5.65
, observe that

(5.96)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{w}),\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\rangle=-\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{w},\nabla(\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w}))\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{w},\xi\cdot\nabla(\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w}))\rangle.

Hence, appealing to Lemma
5.7
and using properties of the weights in Lemma
5.4
, we deduce that

(5.97)

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{w}),\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{w})\rangle|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})\big(\|\nabla{\mathbf{w}}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\|{\mathbf{w}}_{\varepsilon}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\big).

For the operator

(5.98)

\displaystyle D_{\varepsilon}^{1}(w)=-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}t{\partial}_{t}A_{\varepsilon}&0\\
0&\frac{\gamma}{N}t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}{\mathbf{w}},{\mathbf{w}}\Big\rangle-\langle\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{w}}),(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon})({\mathbf{w}})\rangle

we can argue as in the proof of
[
12
, Proposition 4.13]
(specifically the control of their terms
\mathcal{D}_{W_{\varepsilon}}[w]
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{L},\varepsilon}[w])
), and the arguments are straightforward to adapt to the case where
\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)\neq 0
. Thus, we obtain the desired lower bound provided
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.
∎

5.7.
Energy estimates

We now have all the ingredients to prove a priori bounds on the energy defined in
Eq.

5.89
. Before proceeding with the arguments, we remark that we will be heavily using the notations in
Eqs.

5.38
,
5.39
,
5.40
,
5.41
,
5.42
,
5.57
and
5.58
in this section. In particular, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}
in
Eq.

5.43
, we rewrite
Eqs.

5.19
and
5.20
as

(5.99)

\displaystyle(t{\partial}_{t}-\mathcal{L}){\mathbf{w}}+\frac{1}{\delta}\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}}{2\delta}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix}+\delta^{-2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{w}})

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M}=(\mathcal{R}_{M},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{M})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{w}})=(\mathcal{N}(w,\widehat{w}),\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(w,\widehat{w}))
as defined in
Eqs.

5.25
and
5.29
.

We are now ready to compute the time-derivative of
E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}(t))
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
{\mathbf{w}}=(w,\widehat{w})
with
w,\widehat{w}
solving
Eqs.

5.19
and
5.20
with zero-initial data and
E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})
be defined as in
Eq.

5.89
. Then

(5.100)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})={\rm F}+{\rm A}+{\rm N}

where
D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})
is defined in
Eq.

5.93
and we define:

\circ

The forcing term

(5.101)

\displaystyle{\rm F}\coloneqq\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}}{2\delta}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\Big\rangle+\delta^{-2}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\rangle.

\circ

The advection term

(5.102)

\displaystyle{\rm A}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{\delta}\langle\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

\circ

The Navier-Stokes and nonlinear error term

(5.103)

\displaystyle{\rm N}\coloneqq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbf{w}},[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}]({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
is self-adjoint on
L^{2}\times\widehat{L}^{2}
, one has that

(5.104)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}(E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle t{\partial}_{t}{\mathbf{w}},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbf{w}},t{\partial}_{t}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}))\rangle

(5.105)

\displaystyle=\langle t{\partial}_{t}{\mathbf{w}},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbf{w}},[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}]({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

Notice that

(5.106)

\displaystyle[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}]=\begin{pmatrix}t{\partial}_{t}A_{\varepsilon}&0\\
0&\frac{\gamma}{N}t{\partial}_{t}\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}+[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}].

The first term on the right-hand side is included in the definition of
D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})
, and the other one in the definition of
{\rm N}.
All other terms simply arise by using the equation
Eq.

5.99
to replace the
t{\partial}_{t}{\mathbf{w}}
, and notice that the term with
\mathcal{L}
is included in the definition of
D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}).

∎

The goal is to estimate each of the terms in
Eqs.

5.101
,
5.102
and
5.103
. In view of the key property related to symmetries in Lemma
5.8
, the proof is similar in spirit to
[
12
]
. Here we slightly simplify the exposition and we highlight the structural properties that one can exploit in a system rather than the scalar case handled in
[
12
]
. The strategy in
[
12
]
was in fact already adapted in
[
49
]
to handle systems, but in their case they need to decompose the solution to make full use of the pseudo-momenta and choose appropriately the modulation of the angular speed.

Overall, our objective is to prove the following proposition which is at the core of this section.

Proposition 5.15
.

Fix
\sigma\in[0,1)
, let
M\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
Eq.

5.4
and
{\mathbf{w}}
be the solution to
Eq.

5.99
with zero initial data. Then, for
\delta,\sigma_{1}>0
sufficiently small and
\sigma_{2}>1
sufficiently large, there exists a positive constant
C_{1}>1
such that the following holds true: under the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
, for any
t\in[0,T)
with
T\leq T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
, the quantities
Eqs.

5.101
,
5.102
and
5.103
satisfy

(5.107)

\displaystyle(|{\rm F}|+|{\rm A}|+|{\rm N}|)(t)\leq(C_{1}\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{1}{2}D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}))(t).

In the proof of this proposition, it is not hard to deduce that the constant
C_{1}
can be explicitly related to
\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}
(see in particular the bound
Eq.

5.115
). We can now finally prove Theorem
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

Combining the bound
Eq.

5.107
with the energy identity in Lemma
5.14
, we deduce that

(5.108)

\displaystyle t{\partial}_{t}E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+\frac{1}{2}D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\leq C_{1}\varepsilon^{4}.

Dividing by
t
and integrating in time, we see that

(5.109)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})\leq\frac{1}{2}C_{1}\varepsilon^{4}.

Then, by Proposition
5.11
and Lemma
5.2
, since on the time-scale under consideration
\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}\ll\varepsilon^{2}
, we get

(5.110)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}\leq K(E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|^{2})\leq\frac{1}{2}KC_{1}\varepsilon^{4}+C(\varepsilon^{6}+\varepsilon^{2}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}),

for a suitable constant
C>0
independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon
.
Therefore, if
\delta
is sufficiently small, we can conclude that

(5.111)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}\leq KC_{1}\varepsilon^{4},

where we recall that
C_{1},K
are the constants in Proposition
5.15
and Proposition
5.11
that do not depend on the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
. It is then enough to choose
C_{\star}=\sqrt{KC_{1}}
in
Eq.

B
and we see that indeed
Eq.

B
holds true with
2
replaced by
1.
Thus, by a continuity argument this imply that we can take
T=T_{\rm adv}\delta^{-\sigma}
, thus concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

The rest of the paper is then dedicated to the proof of the bound
Eq.

5.107
, which we divide into three steps below.

Step 1: bound on the forcing term

To handle the forcing term
{\rm F}
in
Eq.

5.101
, we use the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
is self-adjoint and we split
{\rm F}={\rm F}_{1}+{\rm F}_{2}
where

(5.112)

\displaystyle{\rm F}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\delta^{-2}\langle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M},{\mathbf{w}}\rangle,

(5.113)

\displaystyle{\rm F}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\Big\langle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix},{\mathbf{w}}\Big\rangle.

To bound
{\rm F}_{1}
, by Lemma
5.7
, the bound for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
in Proposition
4.1
and using Proposition
5.13
we readily get

(5.114)

\displaystyle|{\rm F}_{1}|\lesssim(\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}+\varepsilon^{2})\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{1}{100}(D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|^{2}),

where we used that
\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}\ll\varepsilon^{2}
and
C>0
is a constant independent of
\delta,\varepsilon
and
C_{\star}
in
Eq.

B
. Appealing to the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
, combining the inequality above with Lemma
5.2
and
Eq.

5.60
, we conclude that for
\delta
sufficiently small, there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}_{1}>0
independent of
\delta,\varepsilon
and
C_{\star}
in
Eq.

B
such that

(5.115)

\displaystyle|{\rm F}_{1}|\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{1}{100}D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}).

This bound is consistent with
Eq.

5.107
.

For the term
{\rm F}_{2}
, from Lemma
5.8
we get

(5.116)

\displaystyle{\rm F}_{2}=\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Theta\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Theta}\}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}w\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{w}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle+\frac{\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\Big\langle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Omega_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathrm{app},E}\}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}w\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\widehat{w}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle

In the interior region, we can combine the properties of
\Theta,\widehat{\Theta}
in Proposition
4.21
with properties of the weight in Lemma
5.4
to see that

(5.117)

\displaystyle\Big|\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},\Theta\}\\
\{|\xi|^{2},\widehat{\Theta}\}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}w\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{w}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\|\mathbf{A}_{\varepsilon}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|\xi|)\nabla\boldsymbol{\Theta}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{M}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}

Outside the interior region, we can exploit the fact that
\Omega_{\rm app,E},\Omega_{\rm app,E}\in\mathcal{Z}
and argue as in the proof of Lemma
5.7
to exploit the exponential decay of
A_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
and handle the terms involving
\Delta^{-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
. Overall, we obtain that

(5.118)

\displaystyle|{\rm F}_{2}|\lesssim|\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}|\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{M}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}

Appealing to Lemma
5.9
and the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
, we see that

(5.119)

\displaystyle|{\rm F}_{2}|\lesssim\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{M+8}\lesssim\delta\varepsilon^{9},

where in the last bound we used that in the time-scale under consideration
\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{M+1}\leq\varepsilon^{2}
. This bound is consistent with
Eq.

5.107
upon choosing
\delta
sufficiently small.

Step 2: bound on the advection term

To handle this term, we make rigorous the arguments sketched in Section
5.3
. To highlight some crucial cancellation, we split

(5.120)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{M}_{d}+\mathcal{M}_{nl},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{\rm app}=\mathcal{J}_{d}+\mathcal{J}_{nl}

where we define the “diagonal” terms as

(5.121)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{d}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\varepsilon}&0\\
0&\frac{\gamma}{N}\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{d}=\begin{pmatrix}\{\Phi_{\rm app,E},\cdot\}&0\\
0&\{\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E},\cdot\}\end{pmatrix}

and the “nonlocal” terms as

(5.122)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{nl}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\\
\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}&\frac{\gamma^{2}}{N}\Delta^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

(5.123)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{nl}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\Omega_{\rm app,E}\}&\frac{\gamma}{N}\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\Omega_{\rm app,E}\}\\
\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\}&\gamma\{\Delta^{-1}(\cdot),\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E}\}.\end{pmatrix}

Hence

(5.124)

\displaystyle{\rm A}=-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big(\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle\Big)

Then, since
\langle\{f,g\},f\rangle=0
and
\langle\{f,g\},h\rangle+\langle f,\{h,g\}\rangle=0
for any
f,g,h
, note that

(5.125)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle=0.

Similarly, since
\langle\{f,g\},hf\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\{g,h\}f,f\rangle
we have

(5.126)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}\,w,w\big\rangle+\frac{\gamma}{2N}\big\langle\{\widehat{A}_{\varepsilon},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}\}\,\widehat{w},\widehat{w}\big\rangle.

The bound for this term is analogous to the term
{\rm A}_{1}
in
[
12
]
. However, we have to be a bit more careful for the treatment of the exterior region in view a potentially problematic term related to
\{|\xi|^{2},\cdot\}
and the fact that
\rho_{\varepsilon}
only controls
|\xi|^{\gamma}
for
\gamma<1
. This problem is easily solved thanks to the radial symmetry of the weight
A_{\varepsilon}
in the exterior region.

We claim that

(5.127)

\displaystyle\big|\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{M+1-\sigma_{1}\widetilde{N}}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{1+\sigma_{2}}\|\rho_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}.

Indeed, consider the first term on the right-hand side of
Eq.

5.126
. Thanks to Proposition
4.21
, in the interior region one has

(5.128)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}),\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{F^{\prime}(\Omega_{\rm app,E}),\Theta\}=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{A_{\varepsilon},\Theta\}.

In view of Proposition
4.21
and Lemma
5.4
, we know that there exists
\widetilde{N}
such that

(5.129)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}|\{A_{\varepsilon},\Theta\}|\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}(1+|\xi|)^{\widetilde{N}}\varepsilon^{M+1}A_{\varepsilon}\lesssim\varepsilon^{M+1-\sigma_{1}\widetilde{N}}A_{\varepsilon},

meaning that

(5.130)

\displaystyle\big|\big\langle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}\,w,w\big\rangle\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{M+1-\sigma_{1}\widetilde{N}}\|w\|^{2}_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}

In region
{\rm II}_{\varepsilon}
the weight
A_{\varepsilon}
is constant and therefore
\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}=0
here. Thus, it remains to study the exterior region
{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}.
Since the weight
A_{\varepsilon}
is radially symmetric in this region, note that

(5.131)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}\{A_{\varepsilon},|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2}\}=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{2R_{\rm app}}{\varepsilon d}\{A_{\varepsilon},\xi_{1}\}

Therefore, from the definition of
\Phi_{\rm app,E}
in
Eq.

4.109
and since
\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}|\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}|\xi|^{2\varsigma-1}A_{\varepsilon}
we have

(5.132)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}|\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}|\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}|\xi|^{2\varsigma-1}A_{\varepsilon}\big(|\nabla(1+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Psi_{\rm app,E}|+\gamma|\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\Psi}_{\rm app,E}|+|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app,E},{\widehat{\Psi}}_{\rm app,E})|\big).

Since
|\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\Psi_{\rm app,E},{\widehat{\Psi}}_{\rm app,E})|=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
and
|\xi|^{-1}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\sigma_{2}}
in region
{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}
, appealing to Lemma
5.6
we can control the terms involving the approximate solution and deduce that

(5.133)

\displaystyle\big|\langle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}\{A_{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\}w,w\rangle\big||\lesssim\varepsilon^{\sigma_{2}}\|\rho_{\varepsilon}w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

Thus, the desired claim
Eq.

5.127
is proved since for the second term in
Eq.

5.126
one can repeat exactly the same arguments above.

It remains to handle the mixed terms. Integrating by parts, note that

(5.134)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle=\,

\displaystyle\big\langle\big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\big\},w\big\rangle

(5.135)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big\langle\big\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}w+\gamma\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}\big\},\widehat{w}\big\rangle

On the other hand

(5.136)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle=\,

\displaystyle\big\langle\big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},F(\Omega_{\rm app,E})\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}w\big\rangle

(5.137)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big\langle\big\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}w+\gamma\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\widehat{F}(\widehat{\Omega}_{\rm app,E})\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{w}\big\rangle

(5.138)

\displaystyle+\langle(\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})_{1},\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})_{2}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

Thus, by adding the mixed terms we get

(5.139)

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle

(5.140)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\big\langle\big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\Theta\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}w\big\rangle+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big\langle\big\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}w+\gamma\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\widehat{\Theta}\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{w}\big\rangle

(5.141)

\displaystyle+\big\langle\big\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\Phi_{\rm app,E}\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}w\big\rangle

(5.142)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma}{N}\big\langle\big\{\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,R}\Delta^{-1}w+\gamma\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w},\widehat{\Phi}_{\rm app,E}\big\},\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}\widehat{w}\big\rangle

(5.143)

\displaystyle+\langle(\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})_{1},\mathbbm{1}_{\widehat{{\rm I}}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})_{2}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle.

We see that from the functional relationship we gain smallness thanks to the presence of
\Theta,\widehat{\Theta}.

Outside the interior regions, we can always gain smallness by the fast growth of the weight
A_{\varepsilon}
. Therefore, appealing to Proposition
4.21
and Lemma
5.6
, it follows that

(5.144)

\displaystyle\big|\langle\mathcal{M}_{d}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle+\langle\mathcal{M}_{nl}({\mathbf{w}}),\mathcal{J}_{d}({\mathbf{w}})\rangle\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{M+1}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

Collecting the bounds
Eqs.

5.125
,
5.127
and
5.144
, we arrive at

(5.145)

\displaystyle|{\rm A}|\lesssim\delta^{-1}\big(\varepsilon^{M+1-\sigma_{1}N}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{1+\sigma_{2}}\|\rho_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}\big).

Choosing
\sigma_{1}
sufficiently small and
\sigma_{2}
sufficiently large such that
\sigma_{1}<(M+1)/(2N)
and
\sigma_{2}>(M-1)/2
, in the time-scale under consideration the bound above and Proposition
5.13
imply that

(5.146)

\displaystyle|{\rm A}|\leq C\delta^{s}(D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|^{2}),

where
C>0
is a large constant and
s\in(0,1)
is a sufficiently small constant. Hence, in view of
Eq.

5.60
, the bound above is consistent with
Eq.

5.107
if
\delta
is sufficiently small.

Step 3: bound on the Navier-Stokes and nonlinear term

We finally present the bound for the term
{\rm N}
in
Eq.

5.103
. First of all, we control the term involving
[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}]
. By the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
in
Eq.

5.65
, we know that this operator involves only translations and rotations composed with
\Delta^{-1}
, where the time dependency is only through the functions
\varepsilon(t)
and
R_{\rm app}(t)
. Therefore, when computing the commutator, we get terms of the type
f(t)Q(\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon})\circ\tilde{Q}
where
f(t)
is related to
t{\partial}_{t}
derivatives of
\varepsilon(t)
and
R_{\rm app}(t)
,
Q,\tilde{Q}
are rotation matrices and there is a shift related to rotations of
(R_{\rm app},0)
. Thus, we can appeal to properties of
R_{\rm app}
, Lemma
5.7
and use the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
to deduce that

(5.147)

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbf{w}},[t{\partial}_{t},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}]{\mathbf{w}}\rangle|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|+\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{7}.

In the time scale under consideration, this bound is consistent with
Eq.

5.107
when
\delta
is sufficiently small.

Then, let us control for instance the term arising from
\mathcal{N}(w,\widehat{w})
defined in
Eq.

5.25
, since the one in
Eq.

5.29
can be treated in a completely analogous way. Namely, we aim at controlling

(5.148)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{3}\langle\mathcal{N}_{j}(w,\widehat{w}),(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}})_{1}\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}{\rm N}_{j},

where
\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2},\mathcal{N}_{3}
are defined in
Eqs.

5.26
,
5.27
and
5.28
.
The term
{\rm N}_{1}
is similar to the Navier-Stokes error handled in
[
12
]
. Indeed, it is enough to combine
\Phi_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{2})

\Omega_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2})
with Lemma
5.6
and Proposition
5.13
to infer that

(5.149)

\displaystyle|{\rm N}_{1}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}(\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\|{\nabla\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}})\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}\sqrt{D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|^{2}}

(5.150)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{6}+\varepsilon^{2}(D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|^{2})\lesssim\varepsilon^{6}+\varepsilon^{2}D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}),

where in the last bound we used the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
(and Remark
5.5
). This is again in agreement with
Eq.

5.107
if
\delta
is sufficiently small.

For the nonlinear term
{\rm N}_{2}
, note that

(5.151)

\displaystyle{\rm N}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\big\langle\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\widehat{\varphi},w\},A_{\varepsilon}w+(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\widehat{\varphi}\big\rangle

(5.152)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\{(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\widehat{\varphi},A_{\varepsilon}\}w,w\big\rangle.

Now, we can argue as in the treatment of the nonlinear term in
[
12
]
and deduce that

(5.153)

\displaystyle|{\rm N}_{2}|\lesssim\sqrt{E_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|^{2}}(D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}_{1}^{1}(w)|^{2})\lesssim\varepsilon(D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+\varepsilon^{6}),

where we used the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
in the last inequality.
Finally, for
{\rm N}_{3}
we have to be a bit careful for the term involving
w
. We write

(5.154)

\displaystyle\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},w\},(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}))_{1}\rangle=\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},w\},A_{\varepsilon}w\rangle+\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},w\},(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w}\rangle

(5.155)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},A_{\varepsilon}\}w,w\rangle-\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},(I+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})\Delta^{-1}w+\frac{\gamma}{N}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Delta^{-1}\widehat{w}\},w\rangle.

For the first term, we see that since
A_{0}
is radially symmetric, we can write

(5.156)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},A_{\varepsilon}\}=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\{|\xi|^{2},A_{\varepsilon}-A_{0}\}+\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{2R_{\rm app}}{\varepsilon d}{\partial}_{2}A_{\varepsilon}

By Lemma
5.4
, since
|\xi|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\sigma_{1}}
in
{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}
, we deduce that

(5.157)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}|\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},A_{\varepsilon}\}|\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon A_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{-1}|\xi|A_{\varepsilon})\lesssim\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon})A_{\varepsilon}.

Similarly, since
A_{\varepsilon}
is constant on
{\rm II}_{\varepsilon}
and radial on
{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

(5.158)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}|\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},A_{\varepsilon}\}|=\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm I}^{c}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{2R_{\rm app}}{\varepsilon d}|{\partial}_{2}A_{\varepsilon}|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1+\sigma_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\rm III}_{\varepsilon}}\rho_{\varepsilon}A_{\varepsilon}.

For the remaining terms in
Eq.

5.155
(the ones involving the
\Delta^{-1}
) we can combine
Eq.

5.60
in Lemma
5.6
with Hölder’s inequality and the fast growth of
A_{\varepsilon}
to get that

(5.159)

\displaystyle|\langle\{|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\xi|^{2},w\},(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}))_{1}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}(\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\rho_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}})

For the terms in
{\rm N}_{3}
involving the Navier-Stokes part of the approximate solution, we can simply use
\Omega_{\rm app,NS}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\varepsilon^{2})
. Thus, appealing to Lemma
5.9
, Proposition
5.13
and using the bootstrap hypothesis
Eq.

B
we finally see that

(5.160)

\displaystyle|{\rm N}_{3}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\mathfrak{b}_{\varepsilon}|(\varepsilon\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}(\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}+\|\rho_{\varepsilon}{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{\varepsilon}}))

(5.161)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{9}+\varepsilon^{7}\sqrt{D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}})+|\mathrm{m}^{1}_{1}(w)|^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{9}+\varepsilon^{2}D_{\varepsilon}({\mathbf{w}}).

This bound is also in agreement with
Eq.

5.107
if
\delta
is sufficiently small. Therefore, recalling that the terms involving
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(w,\widehat{w})
can be handled similarly, we conclude that the whole
{\rm N}
satisfy a bound consistent with
Eq.

5.107
and therefore the proof is over. ∎

6.
Numerical simulations

The aim of this section is to present numerical simulations with various combinations of parameters
N
and
\gamma
and check that the behaviour of the deformation of vortices matches what is predicted by the computation of
\Omega_{2}
and
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
.

All the following simulations, as well those presented in Figure
2
are made using the centered Navier-Stokes solver of the free software
Basilisk
. We use
\delta=2\cdot 10^{-4}
, and instead of Dirac masses, we consider the initial vortices to be concentrated Gaussian functions, so that at time 0 we have that, depending on the exact choice of parameters for each simulation,
0.02\lesssim\varepsilon(0)\lesssim 0.05
(except when
N\geq 10
where
d
becomes small, increasing the value of
\varepsilon(0)
).
Let us recall from
[
13
]
that it is expected that the solution of the Navier-Stokes equation with such initial data relaxes quickly to the solution of the problem with Dirac masses as initial datum. Except for the initial time, where vortices are radially symmetric, the pictures shown below are always taken after that transient process is completed.

6.1.
Different situations when
N=2

We start with displaying the simulation of the pair of co-rotating vortices, corresponding to
N=2
and
\gamma=0
, in Figure
4
. The expected two-fold symmetric deformation is visibly clear.

Figure 4
.
The pair of initially Gaussian vortices rotates and deforms.

Taking now
\gamma=1
, we see a similar behaviour, with the central vortex also deforming heavily due to the strain, see Figure
5
.

Figure 5
.
Three identical vortices rotate and deform.

Let us recall that only when
N=2
can
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}
be non-vanishing. Moreover, unlike
\Omega_{2}
, no value of
\gamma
can ensure
\widehat{\Omega}_{2}=0
either (except
\gamma=0
). This means that for
N=2
and
\gamma\neq 0
, we expect the central vortex to always display 2-fold symmetric deformation. For the outer vortices, the deformation changes direction at the critical value
\gamma_{2}^{*}=-\frac{1}{4}
. All three cases are displayed in Figure
6
.

Figure 6
.
Initial configuration (top) evolves in time (bottom). In the cases
\gamma=-3
(left),
\gamma=\gamma_{2}^{*}=-\frac{1}{4}
(middle) and
\gamma=3
(right), after half of a rotation, we see the deformation of the outer vortices in the non-critical cases (left and right) with different orientations depending on the case, and a less visible but present deformation of the central vortex. In the critical case, after just a fraction of rotation, the central vortex has largely deformed while the outer vortices have not.

6.2.
The
N=5
case

An interesting particular situation is taking
N=5
, as in that case the critical value
\gamma_{5}^{*}=0
. This means that we expect the pentagonal vortex crystal, without central vortex, to not display any clear 2-fold symmetric deformation. This is visible in Figure
7
. Even after a full rotation of the configuration, and although the diffusive effects have significantly spread the vortices, they remain mostly radially symmetric. Only after the third, and last before vortex merging, rotation the next order deformation becomes clearly visible.

Figure 7
.
The pentagonal vortex crystal does not display any clear 2-fold symmetric deformation. After a very long time, close to the beginning of vortex merging, the 3-fold symmetric deformation starts to be visible. Each picture is taken after a full rotation of the configuration. It is interesting to compare the shape of these vortices with the ones found in
[
16
]
for a pentagonal configuration.

6.3.
Stability of the polygonal vortex crystal

Before going further, let us quickly discuss the stability of these polygonal vortex crystal.
For general details on how to compute the stability properties of configurations of point-vortices, we refer the reader to
[
43
]
. The stability of our particular vortex crystal of interest has been studied in
[
6
]
, which states the following result.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
6
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

If the intensity of the central vortex satisfies that

\begin{split}\displaystyle\frac{N^{2}-8N+8}{16}<\gamma<\frac{(N-1)^{2}}{4}&\text{ if $N$ is even,}\vskip 8.53581pt\\
\displaystyle\frac{N^{2}-8N+7}{16}<\gamma<\frac{(N-1)^{2}}{4}&\text{ if $N$ is odd,}\end{split}

then this equilibrium is nonlinearly stable to perturbations that preserve
A
.

We mention that according to
[
33
]
, the equilibrium is nonlinearly stable only under the additional assumption to the hypotheses of Theorem
6.1
that
\gamma\geq 0
, which makes a difference in the case
N\leq 6
. Notice that changing
\gamma
changes the angular velocity of the configuration, so in general this equilibrium can only be orbitally nonlinearly stable. If
\gamma=0
, then the Theorem
6.1
states that the equilibrium is nonlinearly stable only for
N\leq 6
. The case
N=7
is a degenerate equilibrium (see
[
6
, Section 7]
) and for
N\geq 8
, the configuration is unstable. See also the results in
[
3
,
4
]
for other interesting relative equilibria with
N+1
-vortices.

In Theorem
1.2
, which is the main result of the paper, we prove in particular that the vortices remain close to the point-vortex solution at least for times
t\ll T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-2/3}
(see Remark
4.16
), and orbitally close for times
t\leq T_{\mathsf{adv}}\delta^{-\sigma}
, for any value of
\gamma
. The mechanism that prevents possible point-vortex instabilities from appearing is the
N
-fold symmetry. With the additional assumption that the perturbation should preserve the
N
-fold symmetry, the point vortex stability problem is completely trivialized, and the configuration is always orbitally stable, for any value of
\gamma
. However, this means that Theorem
1.2
strongly relies on the
N
-fold symmetry, and necessarily fails to be true when
\gamma
is outside of the stability range, if for instance one changes the initial position of the vortices in an unstable
2(N+1)
-dimensional direction.

6.4.
Larger values of
N

The result and behaviour does not change in principle for larger values of
N
. For
N=10
for instance, we compute that
\gamma_{10}^{*}=3
, and thus for various values of
\gamma
compared to
\gamma_{10}^{*}
we see the deformation of the outer vortices changing direction, see Figure
8
. However, one has to be more careful with instabilities. Recall from Theorem
6.1
that for
N=10
one has a threshold
\gamma_{\rm min}=\frac{7}{4}
, which does not leave much room to observe the deformation for
\gamma<\gamma_{10}^{*}
without triggering instabilities. In particular, for
\gamma=0
, we see in Figure
9
that the instability kicks in very early, the symmetry is broken and vortices start merging by pairs. Taking even larger values of
N
, the same applies, and in particular, we recover the vortex sheet instability as discussed in Remark
1.4
.

Figure 8
.
With
N=10
, in the case
\gamma=5
(right) the deformation towards the center is well visible, more than in the case
\gamma=\frac{3}{2}
which is close to
\gamma^{*}=3
, but already below the stability threshold
\gamma_{\rm min}=\frac{7}{4}
.

Figure 9
.
For
N=10
(top) or
N=18
(bottom) with
\gamma=0
, the expected deformation happens, then the typical vortex sheet instability develop very fast. Taking more concentrated vortices would delay this instability, and this is related to the joint
N\to\infty
and
\delta\to 0
limit discussed in Remark
1.4
, corresponding essentially to the first two columns above. However, fixing
\delta
while taking
N\to\infty
the Kelvin-Helmholtz type instability, visible in the third and fourth column above, is the expected behavior as soon as
t>0
.

Appendix A
Function spaces and operators

In this appendix we give the details of the proofs in Section
3
.

A.1.
Preliminary: relevant constants and roots of unity

We first establish formulas to compute the constants
{\rm S}_{n,k}
explicitly, which we recall are defined in
Eq.

3.19
.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
0\leq k\leq N
, there holds that

(A.1)

{\rm S}_{0,0}=N-1,\qquad{\rm S}_{n,0}=-\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\Big(\frac{p(z)^{\prime}}{p(z)}\Big)^{(n-1)}\Big|_{z=1}\in\mathbb{R}\qquad\text{for }n\geq 1

where
p(z)=\frac{1-z^{N}}{1-z}
, and

(A.2)

{\rm S}_{n,k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}{\rm S}_{n-k+j,0}.

Proof.

We rewrite the polynomial
p(z)
as

(A.3)

p(z)=\frac{1-z^{N}}{1-z}=1+z+\dots+z^{N-1}=(z-Q_{1})\dots(z-Q_{N-1}),

and thus

(A.4)

\frac{p^{\prime}(z)}{p(z)}=\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{z-Q_{l}}.

Differentiating, we have that

(A.5)

\Big(\frac{p(z)^{\prime}}{p(z)}\Big)^{(m)}=(-1)^{m}m!\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(z-Q_{l})^{m+1}}.

Therefore, for
n\geq 1
, we have

(A.6)

{\rm S}_{n,0}=(-1)^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(1-Q_{\ell})^{n}}=-\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\Big(\frac{p(z)^{\prime}}{p(z)}\Big)^{(n-1)}\Big|_{z=1}\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover

(A.7)

{\rm S}_{0,0}=N-1.

To compute the value of
{\rm S}_{n,k}
, we first observe that for
1\leq k\leq n
,

(A.8)

{\rm S}_{n,k}=\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{l}^{k}}{(Q_{l}-1)^{n}}=\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{\ell}^{k-1}}{(Q_{l}-1)^{n-1}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{\ell}^{k-1}}{(Q_{l}-1)^{n}}={\rm S}_{n-1,k-1}+{\rm S}_{n,k-1}.

We recognize a Pascal’s triangle-type formula, and one can prove by induction that for every
0\leq k\leq n
,

(A.9)

{\rm S}_{n,k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}{\rm S}_{n-k+j,0}.

∎

In view of Lemma
A.1
, we can compute recursively every value of
{\rm S}_{n,k}
for
0\leq k\leq n
. Let us give the result for some relevant values.

(A.10)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{1,0}=-\frac{p^{\prime}(1)}{p(1)}=-\frac{N-1}{2},

(A.11)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{1,1}=\frac{N-1}{2}

(A.12)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{2,0}=\Big(\frac{p^{\prime}(1)}{p(1)}\Big)^{2}-\frac{p^{\prime\prime}(1)}{p(1)}=-\frac{(N-1)(N-5)}{12}

(A.13)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{2,1}={\rm S}_{1,0}+{\rm S}_{2,0}=\frac{1-N^{2}}{12}

(A.14)

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{2,2}={\rm S}_{0,0}+2{\rm S}_{1,0}+{\rm S}_{2,0}={\rm S}_{2,0}.

A.2.
Expansion of the configurational operators

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}
defined in
Eqs.

2.37
,
2.38
and
2.39
.
We then prove a more general version of Lemma
3.6
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{Z}
,
\varphi=\Delta^{-1}f
,
0<z_{\min}\leq|Z|
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the translation operator admits the expansion

(A.15)

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\varphi(\xi)\,=\,\frac{{\rm M}(f)}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,k}{\rm Re}\Big(\frac{\xi^{n-k}}{Z^{n}}{\rm m}^{k}(f)\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M+1}\bigr)\,.

The polygon-center operator admits the expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,Z}\varphi(\xi)\,=\,&\mathrm{M}(\Omega)\frac{N}{2\pi}\log\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+N\sum_{n=1}^{M}(-1)^{n}\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,n}{\rm Re}\Big(\frac{1}{Z^{n}}{\rm m}^{n}(f)\Big)\\
&+N\sum_{n=N}^{M}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\\
\ell N\leq M\end{subarray}}(-1)^{n}\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,n-\ell N}{\rm Re}\Big(\frac{\xi^{\ell N}}{Z^{n}}\mathrm{m}^{n-\ell N}(f)\Big)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{M+1}\bigr)\,.\end{split}

The polygon-polygon operator admits the expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon,Z}\varphi(\xi)\,=&\,\frac{\mathrm{M}(\Omega)}{2\pi}\sum_{l=1}^{N-1}\log\Big(\frac{|Z_{l}|}{\varepsilon d}\Big)+\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\varepsilon^{n}c_{n,k}{\rm S}_{n,n-k}{\rm Re}\Bigg(\frac{\xi^{n-k}}{Z^{n}}\mathrm{m}^{k}(f)\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{M+1}).\end{split}

Proof.

By
Eq.

2.8
and the definition
Eq.

2.37
, we have

(A.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,Z}\varphi(\xi)\,
